\title{The Era of 1-bit LLMs: All Large Language Models are in 1.58 Bits}

\begin{abstract}
Recent research, such as BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, is paving the way for a new era of 1-bit Large Language Models (LLMs). In this work, we introduce a 1-bit LLM variant, namely \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, in which every single parameter (or weight) of the LLM is ternary \{-1, 0, 1\}. It matches the full-precision (i.e., FP16 or BF16) Transformer LLM with the same model size and training tokens in terms of both perplexity and end-task performance, while being significantly more cost-effective in terms of latency, memory, throughput, and energy consumption. More profoundly, the 1.58-bit LLM defines a new scaling law and recipe for training new generations of LLMs that are both high-performance and cost-effective. Furthermore, it enables a new computation paradigm and opens the door for designing specific hardware optimized for 1-bit LLMs.
\end{abstract}

\section{The Era of 1-bit LLMs}

In recent years, the field of AI has seen a rapid growth in the size and capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). These models have demonstrated remarkable performance in a wide range of natural language processing tasks, but their increasing size has posed challenges for deployment and raised concerns about their environmental and economic impact due to high energy consumption. 

One approach to address these challenges is to use post-training quantization to create low-bit models for inference~\cite{smoothquant, gptq, quip, quip_sharp}. This technique reduces the precision of weights and activations, significantly reducing the memory and computational requirements of LLMs. The trend has been to move from 16 bits to lower bits, such as 4-bit variants~\cite{gptq, awq}. However, post-training quantization is sub-optimal, even though it is widely used in industry LLMs.

Recent work on 1-bit model architectures, such as BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, presents a promising direction for reducing the cost of LLMs while maintaining their performance. Vanilla LLMs are in 16-bit floating values (i.e., FP16 or BF16), and the bulk of any LLMs is matrix multiplication. Therefore, the major computation cost comes from the floating-point addition and multiplication operations. In contrast, the matrix multiplication of BitNet only involves integer addition, which saves orders of energy cost for LLMs. As the fundamental limit to compute performance in many chips is power, the energy savings can also be translated into faster computation.

In addition to computation, the process of transferring model parameters from DRAM to the memory of an on-chip accelerator (e.g., SRAM) can be expensive during inference. There have been attempts to enlarge SRAM to improve throughput, but this introduces significantly higher costs than DRAM. Compared to full-precision models, 1-bit LLMs have a much lower memory footprint from both a capacity and bandwidth standpoint. This can significantly reduce the cost and time of loading weights from DRAM, leading to faster and more efficient inference.

In this work, we introduce a significant 1-bit LLM variant called \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, where every parameter is ternary, taking on values of \{-1, 0, 1\}. We have added an additional value of 0 to the original 1-bit BitNet, resulting in 1.58 bits in the binary system. \text{BitNet b1.58} retains all the benefits of the original 1-bit BitNet, including its new computation paradigm, which requires almost no multiplication operations for matrix multiplication and can be highly optimized. Additionally, it has the same energy consumption as the original 1-bit BitNet and is much more efficient in terms of memory consumption, throughput and latency compared to FP16 LLM baselines. Furthermore, \text{BitNet b1.58} offers two additional advantages. Firstly, its modeling capability is stronger due to its explicit support for feature filtering, made possible by the inclusion of 0 in the model weights, which can significantly improve the performance of 1-bit LLMs. Secondly, our experiments show that \text{BitNet b1.58} can match full precision (i.e., FP16) baselines in terms of both perplexity and end-task performance, starting from a 3B size, when using the same configuration (e.g., model size, training tokens, etc.).

\section{BitNet b1.58}

\text{BitNet b1.58} is based on the BitNet architecture, which is a Transformer that replaces \emph{nn.Linear} with \emph{BitLinear}. It is trained from scratch, with 1.58-bit weights and 8-bit activations. Compared to the original BitNet, it introduces some modifications that we summarize below.

\paragraph{Quantization Function.} To constrain the weights to -1, 0, or +1, we adopt an \emph{absmean} quantization function. It first scales the weight matrix by its average absolute value, and then round each value to the nearest integer among \{-1, 0, +1\}:

\begin{equation}
    \widetilde{W} = \mathrm{RoundClip}(\frac{W}{\gamma + \epsilon}, -1, 1),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \mathrm{RoundClip}(x, a, b) = \max(a, \min(b, \mathrm{round}(x))),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \gamma = \frac{1}{nm}\sum_{ij} |W_{ij}|.
\end{equation}

The quantization function for activations follows the same implementation in BitNet, except that we do not scale the activations before the non-linear functions to the range $[0, Q_b]$. Instead, the activations are all scaled to $[-Q_b, Q_b]$ per token to get rid of the zero-point quantization. This is more convenient and simple for both implementation and system-level optimization, while introduces negligible effects to the performance in our experiments.

\paragraph{LLaMA-alike Components.}

The architecture of LLaMA~\cite{llama, llama2} has been the de-facto backbone for open-source LLMs. To embrace the open-source community, our design of \text{BitNet b1.58} adopts the LLaMA-alike components. Specifically, it uses RMSNorm~\cite{rmsnorm}, SwiGLU~\cite{swiglu}, rotary embedding~\cite{rope}, and removes all biases. In this way, \text{BitNet b1.58} can be integrated into the popular open-source software (e.g., Huggingface, vLLM~\cite{vllm}, and llama.cpp\footnote{\url{https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp}}) with minimal efforts.

\section{Results}

We compared \text{BitNet b1.58} to our reproduced FP16 \text{LLaMA LLM} in various sizes. To ensure a fair comparison, we pre-trained the models on the RedPajama dataset~\cite{redpajama} for 100 billion tokens. We evaluated the zero-shot performance on a range of language tasks, including ARC-Easy~\cite{arc}, ARC-Challenge~\cite{arc}, Hellaswag~\cite{hellaswag}, Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, OpenbookQA~\cite{openbookqa}, and BoolQ~\cite{boolq}. We also reported the validation perplexity on the WikiText2~\cite{wikitext2} and C4~\cite{c4} datasets.

We compared the runtime GPU memory and latency of both \text{LLaMA LLM} and \text{BitNet b1.58}. The results were measured using the FasterTransformer\footnote{\url{https://github.com/NVIDIA/FasterTransformer}} codebase, which is well-optimized for LLM inference latency on GPU devices. The 2-bit kernel from Ladder~\cite{ladder} is also integrated for \text{BitNet b1.58}. We reported the time per output token, as it is the major cost for inference.

Table~\ref{tab:ppl} summarizes the perplexity and the cost for \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. It shows that \text{BitNet b1.58} starts to match full precision \text{LLaMA LLM} at 3B model size in terms of perplexity, while being 2.71 times faster and using 3.55 times less GPU memory. In particular, \text{BitNet b1.58} with a 3.9B model size is 2.4 times faster, consumes 3.32 times less memory, but performs significantly better than \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B. 

Table~\ref{tab:zero-shot} reports the detailed results of the zero-shot accuracy on the end tasks. We followed the pipeline from \emph{lm-evaluation-harness}\footnote{\url{https://github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness}} to perform the evaluation. The results show that the performance gap between \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM} narrows as the model size increases. More importantly, \text{BitNet b1.58} can match the performance of the full precision baseline starting from a 3B size. Similar to the observation of the perplexity, the end-task results reveal that \text{BitNet b1.58} 3.9B outperforms \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B with lower memory and latency cost. This demonstrates that \text{BitNet b1.58} is a Pareto improvement over the state-of-the-art LLM models.

\paragraph{Memory and Latency}

We further scaled up the model size to 7B, 13B, and 70B and evaluated the cost. Figure~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy} illustrates the trends of latency and memory, showing that the speed-up increases as the model size scales. In particular, \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B is 4.1 times faster than the \text{LLaMA LLM} baseline. This is because the time cost for \emph{nn.Linear} grows with the model size. The memory consumption follows a similar trend, as the embedding remains full precision and its memory proportion is smaller for larger models. Both latency and memory were measured with a 2-bit kernel, so there is still room for optimization to further reduce the cost.

\paragraph{Energy}

We also estimate the arithmetic operations energy consumption of both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. We focus mainly on the calculation for matrix multiplication, since it contributes the most to the cost of LLMs. Figure~\ref{fig:energy} illustrates the composition of the energy cost. The majority of \text{BitNet b1.58} is INT8 addition calculation, while \text{LLaMA LLM} consists of both FP16 addition and FP16 multiplication. According to the energy model in~\cite{energycost, pokebnn}, \text{BitNet b1.58} saves 71.4 times arithmetic operations energy consumption for matrix multiplication on 7nm chips. We further reported the end-to-end energy cost for models with 512 tokens. Our results show that as the model size scales, \text{BitNet b1.58} becomes increasingly more efficient in terms of energy consumption compared to the FP16 \text{LLaMA LLM} baseline. This is due to the fact that the percentage of \emph{nn.Linear} grows with the model size, while the cost from other components is smaller for larger models.

\paragraph{Throughput}

We compare the throughput of \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM} with 70B parameters on two 80GB A100 cards, using pipeline parallelism~\cite{gpipe} so that \text{LLaMA LLM} 70B could be run on the devices. We increased the batch size until the GPU memory limit was reached, with a sequence length of 512. Table~\ref{tab:throughput} shows that \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B can support up to 11 times the batch size of \text{LLaMA LLM}, resulting an 8.9 times higher throughput.

\textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58} is enabling a new scaling law with respect to model performance and inference cost}. As a reference, we can have the following equivalence between different model sizes in 1.58-bit and 16-bit based on the results in Figure~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy} and \ref{fig:energy}.

\begin{itemize}
    \item 13B BitNet b1.58 is more efficient, in terms of latency, memory usage and energy consumption, than 3B FP16 LLM.
    \item 30B BitNet b1.58 is more efficient, in terms of latency, memory usage and energy consumption, than 7B FP16 LLM.
    \item 70B BitNet b1.58 is more efficient, in terms of latency, memory usage and energy consumption, than 13B FP16 LLM.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Training with 2T Tokens}

The number of training tokens is a crucial factor for LLMs. To test the scalability of \text{BitNet b1.58} in terms of tokens, we trained a \text{BitNet b1.58} model with 2T tokens following the data recipe of StableLM-3B~\cite{StableLM-3B-4E1T}, which is the state-of-the-art open-source 3B model. Both models were evaluated on a benchmark that consists of Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, SciQ~\cite{sciq}, LAMBADA~\cite{lambada}, and ARC-easy~\cite{arc}. We reported the zero-shot accuracy in Table~\ref{tab:2t}. For tasks measured with accuracy and normalized accuracy, we take the average of the two. The results of StableLM 3b at 2T tokens are taken directly from its technical report. Our findings shows that \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves a superior performance on all end tasks, indicating that 1.58-bit LLMs also have strong generalization capabilities.

\section{Discussion and Future Work}

\textbf{1-bit Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs}

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) have proven to be a cost-effective approach for LLMs. While it significantly reduces the computation FLOPs, the high memory consumption and inter-chip communication overhead limit its deployment and application. These challenges can be addressed by 1.58-bit LLMs. Firstly, the reduced memory footprint reduces the number of devices required to deploy MoE models. Moreover, it significantly reduces the overhead of transferring activations across networks. Ultimately, there would be no overhead if the entire models could be placed on a single chip.

\textbf{Native Support of Long Sequence in LLMs}

In the era of LLMs, the ability to handle long sequence has become a critical demand. One major challenge for long sequence inference is the memory consumption introduced by the KV caches. \text{BitNet b1.58} represents a significant step towards native support for long sequences, as it reduces the activations from 16 bits to 8 bits, allowing the context length to be doubled given the same resources. This can be further losslessly compressed to 4 bits or even lower for 1.58-bit LLMs, which we leave as future work.

\textbf{LLMs on Edge and Mobile}

The use of 1.58-bit LLMs has the potential to greatly improve the performance of language models on edge and mobile devices. These devices are often limited by their memory and computational power, which can restrict the performance and the scale of LLMs. However, the reduced memory and energy consumption of 1.58-bit LLMs allows them to be deployed on these devices, enabling a wide range of applications that were previously not possible. This can greatly enhance the capabilities of edge and mobile devices and enable new and exciting applications of LLMs. Moreover, 1.58-bit LLMs are more friendly to CPU devices, which are the main processors used in edge and mobile devices. This means that \text{BitNet b1.58} can be efficiently executed on these devices, further improving their performance and capabilities.

\textbf{New Hardware for 1-bit LLMs}

Recent work like~Groq\footnote{\url{https://groq.com/}} has demonstrated promising results and great potential for building specific hardware (e.g., LPUs) for LLMs. Going one step further, we envision and call for actions to design new hardware and system specifically optimized for 1-bit LLMs, given the new computation paradigm enabled in BitNet~\cite{bitnet}.

United States Court of Appeals

FOR THE DISTRICT OF COLUMBIA CIRCUIT

Argued February 12, 2025 Decided October 3, 2025

No. 24-5168

AMERICA FIRST LEGAL FOUNDATION, APPELLANT

v.

JAMIESON GREER, IN HIS OFFICIAL CAPACITY, APPELLEE

Appeal from the United States District Court

for the District of Columbia (No. 1:22-cv-03576)

Andrew J. Block argued the cause for appellant. With him on the briefs was Reed D. Rubinstein.

David L. Peters, Attorney, U.S. Department of Justice, argued the cause for appellee. With him on the briefs were Brian M. Boynton, Principal Deputy Assistant Attorney General, at the time the brief was filed, Brett A. Shumate, Acting Assistant Attorney General, Matthew M. Graves, United States Attorney, at the time the brief was filed, Michael

S. Raab, Attorney, Susan K. Ullman, General Counsel, U.S.

Office of Special Counsel, and Hnin Khaing, Senior Litigation Counsel.

GINSBURG, Senior Circuit Judge.

Opinion for the Court filed by Circuit Judge KATSAS.

KATSAS, Circuit Judge: America First Legal Foundation brought this lawsuit to compel the Office of Special Counsel to investigate whether a federal agency arbitrarily denied AFL’s Freedom of Information Act request. The district court dismissed AFL’s claims on the merits. We conclude that AFL lacks Article III standing to pursue the claims.

I

A

The Office of Special Counsel (OSC) is an investigative and prosecutorial agency established to seek “a fair, efficient, and lawfully-conducted Civil Service.” Frazier v. MSPB, 672 F.2d 150, 162 (D.C. Cir. 1982). The Civil Service Reform Act empowers OSC to investigate “prohibited personnel practices” and, if warranted, to bring enforcement proceedings before the Merit Systems Protection Board for corrective action under 5 U.S.C. § 1214 or disciplinary action under 5 U.S.C. § 1215. Section 1214 sets forth procedures to govern corrective actions for prohibited personnel practices, including provisions for notifying the complaining party about the progress of an investigation. See, e.g., 5 U.S.C. § 1214(a)(1)(B)–(D).

Section 1216 empowers OSC to investigate and seek corrective action for certain other forms of misconduct by government employees. As relevant here, it provides that OSC “shall … conduct an investigation of any allegation concerning arbitrary or capricious withholding of information prohibited under section 552”—in other words, withholdings prohibited by the Freedom of Information Act. U.S.C. § 1216(a)(3). For such alleged FOIA violations, OSC “may investigate and seek corrective action under section 1214 and disciplinary action under section 1215 in the same way as if a prohibited personnel practice were involved.” Id. § 1216(c).

FOIA contains its own separate provision for OSC enforcement. It states that if a court (1) orders production of agency records improperly withheld under FOIA, (2) awards attorneys’ fees against the agency, and (3) specifically questions whether the agency “acted arbitrarily or capriciously with respect to the withholding,” then OSC “shall promptly initiate a proceeding to determine whether disciplinary action is warranted against the officer or employee who was primarily responsible for the withholding.” U.S.C. § 552(a)(4)(F)(i).

B

As part of its effort to investigate judicial recusals, AFL requested from the Department of Justice certain emails between an outside attorney and the DOJ’s Civil Division. According to AFL, the Civil Division denied the request on the dubious ground that it does not search employee emails for responsive records without the employee’s consent. AFL then sued DOJ under FOIA. In that lawsuit, DOJ has produced— and is continuing to produce—records responsive to the request. The sufficiency of this production is not at issue here.

AFL also asked OSC to investigate DOJ’s alleged FOIA policy of not searching employee emails without consent. It invoked section 1216(a)(3), which states that OSC “shall … conduct an investigation” of arbitrary FOIA withholdings. OSC disclaimed authority to investigate because the three predicates for triggering an investigation under section 552(a)(4)(F)(i) were not satisfied. alleged FOIA policy. It argued that section 1216 mandated such an investigation even if FOIA did not.

The district court ruled in part for AFL but nonetheless dismissed its case for failure to state a claim. The court concluded that section 552(a)(4)(F)(i) does not limit OSC’s power to investigate alleged FOIA violations under section 1216. See Am. First Legal Found. v. Kerner, No. 22-cv-3576, 2023 WL 9546644, at *5 (D.D.C. Sept. 29, 2023). So, the court declared, OSC’s decision not to investigate was based on a legal error. Id. Nonetheless, the court declined to order OSC to investigate any FOIA policy because, in its view, section 1216(c) committed that decision to OSC’s discretion. Id. at *6. The court remanded the case to OSC for further consideration. Id. After OSC again declined to investigate, AFL moved for entry of final judgment, and the district court dismissed its remaining claims. J.A. 68. AFL appealed, and our review is de novo. N. Am. Butterfly Ass’n v. Wolf, 977 F.3d 1244, 1249 (D.C. Cir. 2020).

II

Before reaching the merits, we must consider whether AFL satisfied the requirements for Article III standing, a question bearing on whether the district court had subjectmatter jurisdiction. Steel Co. v. Citizens for a Better Env’t, 523 U.S. 83, 101–02 (1998). Neither party initially addressed standing, so we ordered supplemental briefing on that question.

Article III of the Constitution restricts the federal “judicial Power” to resolving certain “Cases” and “Controversies.” U.S. Const. Art. III, § 2, cl. 1. Standing doctrine follows from this case-or-controversy requirement. Allen v. Wright, 468 U.S. 737, 750 (1984). To have Article III standing, the plaintiff must have suffered an injury caused by the defendant and likely to be redressed by a favorable decision. Lujan v. Defs. of Wildlife, 504 U.S. 555, 560–61 (1992). The injury must be “an invasion of a legally protected interest” that is “concrete and particularized” and “actual or imminent, not conjectural or hypothetical.” Id. at 560 (cleaned up). At the motion-to-dismiss stage, a plaintiff must allege facts plausibly supporting each element of its standing. Food & Water Watch, Inc. v. Vilsack, 808 F.3d 905, 913 (D.C. Cir. 2015).

In its supplemental brief, AFL claims that OSC violated its alleged procedural right to an investigation under section 1216. But the “deprivation of a procedural right without some concrete interest that is affected by the deprivation—a procedural right in vacuo—is insufficient” to confer Article III standing. Summers v. Earth Island Inst., 555 U.S. 488, 496 (2009); see also Citizens for Const. Integrity v. Census Bureau, 115 F.4th 618, 625 (D.C. Cir. 2024). To overcome that problem, AFL contends that OSC’s refusal to investigate has caused it two concrete, informational injuries: First, DOJ has not provided the records that AFL requested through FOIA. Second, OSC has not provided the notices and information required for an investigation under section 1214.

Through this framing, AFL seeks to invoke the relaxed standing requirements that apply to cases involving alleged procedural injury. “Where plaintiffs allege injury resulting from violation of a procedural right afforded to them by statute and designed to protect their threatened concrete interest, the courts relax—while not wholly eliminating—the issues of imminence and redressability, but not the issues of injury in fact or causation.” Ctr. for Law & Educ. v. Dep’t of Educ., 396 F.3d 1152, 1157 (D.C. Cir. 2005); see Fla. Audubon Soc’y v. Bentsen, 94 F.3d 658, 664–65 (D.C. Cir. 1996) (en banc). In such cases, plaintiffs thus must show that the violation of a procedural right “resulted in injury” to some “concrete, particularized interest.” Ctr. for Law & Educ., 396 F.3d at 1157. AFL satisfies neither those requirements nor even a relaxed standard of redressability. Because AFL cannot satisfy even the more relaxed standing requirements for procedural injuries, we need not decide whether these injuries are procedural or substantive.

AFL’s first standing theory is that OSC’s failure to investigate DOJ under section 1216 has prevented AFL from receiving the records it requested under FOIA. This theory suffers from fatal causation and redressability problems. For one thing, any informational injury from DOJ’s refusal to disclose agency records is plainly traceable to DOJ—not OSC. And any causal connection between OSC’s failure to conduct a section 1216 investigation and DOJ’s failure to comply with the FOIA request is too speculative and attenuated to support Article III standing. The alleged causal chain is as follows: If OSC had conducted the assertedly mandatory section 1216 investigation, it might have decided to pursue a corrective action under section 1214, or a disciplinary action under section 1215, against offending DOJ employees. See 5 U.S.C. §§ 1214(b)(3), 1215(a)(1). Then, the MSPB might have decided to sanction those employees for arbitrary FOIA withholdings. See id. §§ 1214(b)(4)(A), 1215(a)(3)(A). Next, DOJ might have decided to produce the requested records. This requires speculation about choices by two government agencies not parties to this lawsuit (the MSPB and DOJ), yet “causation generally cannot rely on speculation about the unfettered choices made by independent actors not before the courts.” FDA v. All. for Hippocratic Med., 602 U.S. 367, 383 (2024) (cleaned up). And it requires speculation about how OSC might choose to exercise its enforcement discretion, which the courts generally may not compel. See Heckler v. Chaney, 470 U.S. 821, 831–32 (1985). Given this speculative chain, AFL has not shown that a favorable judgment here “would very likely spur the next steps necessary to redress” its informational injury. Hecate Energy LLC v. FERC, 126 F.4th 660, 667 (D.C. Cir. 2025). And AFL overlooks the principle that redressability generally turns on how a judgment binds the parties, not whether its rationale “might persuade actors who are not before the court.” Haaland v. Brackeen, 599 U.S. 255, 293–94 (2023). For these reasons, AFL cannot establish standing based on its interest in the information that it requested from DOJ through FOIA.

AFL’s second standing theory—based on its asserted informational interest in the fruits of the OSC investigation itself—fares no better. A plaintiff claiming informational standing suffers a concrete injury if he is denied information that a “statute entitled him to receive.” Am. Soc’y for Prevention of Cruelty to Animals v. Feld Ent., Inc., 659 F.3d 13, 23 (D.C. Cir. 2011) (quoting Zivotofsky ex rel. Ari Z. v. Sec’y of State, 444 F.3d 614, 618 (D.C. Cir. 2006)); see also FEC v. Akins, 524 U.S. 11, 21 (1998); Pub. Citizen v. DOJ, 491 U.S. 440, 448–50 (1989). But here, even if section 1216(a)(3) requires OSC to investigate FOIA violations, it creates no entitlement to receive information. AFL responds that, if OSC conducts the assertedly mandatory investigation under section 1216, it may choose to give AFL notices and information that would be required if OSC were investigating an alleged “prohibited personnel practice” under section 1214. But AFL does not contend that OSC must engraft section 1214 notice requirements onto its section 1216 investigations. To the contrary, it acknowledges cases holding that OSC’s “decision whether to follow section 1214’s procedural requirements” in a section 1216 investigation is “discretionary.” Carson v. U.S. Off. of Special Couns., 633 F.3d 487, 495–96 (6th Cir. 2011). Absent any alleged entitlement to receive information under section 1214, AFL lacks a concrete injury sufficient to support its second standing theory. Likewise, insofar as OSC need not provide section 1214 notice requirements in a section 1216 investigation, a judicial order mandating such an investigation would not be reasonably likely to redress AFL’s second alleged injury.

III

Because AFL lacks Article III standing, we vacate the district court’s judgment and remand with instructions to dismiss for lack of subject-matter jurisdiction.

So ordered.

Acute neurological Behçet’s disease complicated by a syndrome of inappropriate secretion of antidiuretic hormone

Eiichi Kakehi, Yukako Matsumoto, Shigehisa Sakurai, Kazuhiko Kotani

Abstract:

Summary: We report a rare case of acute neurological Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD) diagnosed concurrently with systemic Behçet’s disease (BD), classified as definite BD, and complicated by syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH). A man in his 60s presented with fever, headache and mucocutaneous lesions including oral aphthae, folliculitis-like rashes, genital ulcers and hyponatraemia. He was diagnosed with BD based on mucocutaneous findings. Brain MRI showed no abnormalities, and cerebrospinal fluid analysis revealed aseptic meningitis, supporting the diagnosis of acute neuro-BD. Laboratory tests confirmed the diagnostic criteria for SIADH. Colchicine monotherapy led to a resolution of the patient’s mucocutaneous symptoms, neurological manifestations and hyponatraemia, without the need for corticosteroids or other immunosuppressants. This case suggests a possible association between acute neuro-BD and SIADH and underscores the importance of recognising concurrent neurological and endocrine manifestations in patients with newly diagnosed BD.

Background:

Behçet’s disease (BD) is a chronic, multisystem inflammatory disorder characterised by recurrent oral and genital ulcers, uveitis and skin lesions. Neurological involvement, referred to as neuro-Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD), occurs in approximately 10%–20% of patients and typically develops several years after the initial diagnosis. Acute neuro-BD usually presents with meningoencephalitis and parenchymal brain lesions, which commonly involve the brainstem. Although delayed onset is more common, BD and neuro-BD are rarely diagnosed concurrently. In such cases, clinicians should assess both mucocutaneous features and neurological symptoms, including headaches, behavioural changes, psychiatric symptoms and focal deficits.

The syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH) is a well-known cause of hyponatraemia and is often the result of central nervous system (CNS) inflammation, malignancy or certain medications. Given the inflammatory nature of acute neuro-BD, affected patients may be at risk for developing SIADH. Although SIADH has been reported in association with BD patients receiving treatments such as cyclophosphamide, its occurrence in the absence of pharmacological triggers, and caused solely by CNS inflammation related to acute neuro-BD, remains unclear.

We report a rare case in which BD and acute neuro-BD were diagnosed concurrently, complicated by SIADH. This case highlights the importance of considering SIADH as a potential manifestation of acute neuro-BD, particularly in patients presenting with non-specific CNS symptoms and hyponatraemia.

Case presentation:

An Asian man in his 60s presented to the emergency department with a 3 day history of fever, headache, general malaise, loss of appetite, persistent hiccups and arthralgia affecting the shoulders and ankles. He also reported oral aphthae.

His medical history included type 2 diabetes mellitus, for which he was not receiving treatment because he had limited awareness of his condition and did not maintain appropriate dietary or physical activity habits. His family history was unremarkable, with no relatives affected by BD. He occasionally consumed alcohol, did not smoke, had not travelled recently and had not been exposed to animals or unwell individuals. He worked in the metalworking industry and had not engaged in recent sexual activity.

On examination, he was alert but appeared fatigued. His vital signs were: a temperature of 38.1°C, blood pressure of 135/73 mm Hg, a heart rate of 119 beats per minute (regular), a respiratory rate of 19 breaths per minute and an oxygen saturation of 96% on room air. Physical examination revealed oral aphthae and folliculitis-like rashes on the face and trunk (figure 1A, B and C). Cardiopulmonary and abdominal examination findings were unremarkable. Manual muscle testing revealed no muscle weakness in the upper or lower extremities. There was no peripheral oedema, and his volume status was clinically assessed as euvolemic. He was admitted for further evaluation.

Investigations:

Initial laboratory tests revealed marked hyponatraemia (124 mmol/L), elevated creatine kinase (1349 U/L), aspartate aminotransferase (77 U/L), alanine aminotransferase (64 U/L), C-reactive protein concentration (CRP) (180 mg/L; reference, <3 mg/L) and erythrocyte sedimentation rate (70 mm/hour; reference, <15 mm/hour), with normal renal function. The patient also presented with hyperglycaemia (plasma glucose 14.2 mmol/L) and an elevated HbA1c level of 8.2%, indicating poor glycaemic control. Hepatitis B and C markers were negative.

Endocrine evaluations revealed normal thyroid-stimulating hormone (1.11 mIU/L; reference, 0.50–5.00 mIU/L), free T4 (1.25 ng/dL; reference, 0.90–1.70 ng/dL) and serum cortisol (42 µg/dL; reference, >6 µg/dL). Serum osmolality was low (267 mOsm/kg; reference, <270 mOsm/kg) and urine osmolality was elevated (900 mOsm/kg, reference, >300 mOsm/kg), with an increased urinary sodium concentration (88 mmol/L; reference, >20 mmol/L), consistent with impaired water excretion. Despite hypo-osmolar hyponatraemia, antidiuretic hormone (ADH) was elevated (22.4 pg/mL). His ECG was normal, and both chest X-ray and CT of the head, chest and abdomen were unremarkable. These findings supported a diagnosis of SIADH.

On day 3, his headache and vertigo worsened. Although there was no neck stiffness or cranial nerve involvement, CNS inflammation was suspected. Brain MRI revealed no significant abnormalities involving the hypothalamus or other brain regions (figure 2). Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) analysis revealed mildly elevated opening pressure (17 cmH₂O), increased protein (0.97 g/L) and mild lymphocytic pleocytosis (11 cells/µL). CSF cultures and tests for bacterial and mycobacterial pathogens, as well as herpes simplex virus (HSV), varicella zoster virus (VZV) and cryptococcal antigen, were all negative. CSF cytology showed no malignant cells.

Further investigations were performed to evaluate underlying systemic diseases. Tests for sarcoidosis revealed normal lysozyme (5.1 µg/mL; reference 5–10) and angiotensin-converting enzyme levels (11.4 U/L; reference 7–25). Screening for haematological malignancy revealed soluble interleukin-2 receptor (sIL-2R) (704 U/mL; reference, 122–496 U/mL), with negative human T-cell leukaemia virus type 1 antibody.

On day 5, a painful scrotum ulcer appeared (figure 1D). In combination with oral aphthae and folliculitis-like rashes, these findings suggested a systemic form of BD. He did not exhibit hypersensitivity to a pinprick. Ophthalmological examination revealed no uveitis. HLA-B51 was positive.

Differential diagnosis:

At the time of presentation, the combination of fever, headache, general malaise and hyponatraemia suggested CNS infection or a systemic inflammatory disorder. Viral meningoencephalitis, particularly caused by HSV or VZV, was suspected initially. However, CSF testing was negative for viral antigen and antibodies, effectively excluding these possibilities. Bacterial meningitis was also considered but ruled out based on the absence of neutrophilic predominance, mild lymphocytic pleocytosis and negative CSF cultures. Tuberculous meningitis was excluded because the results for acid-fast staining and tuberculosis DNA testing were negative.

Primary CNS malignancy was considered, given the neurological symptoms. However, brain MRI showed no abnormal findings, and CSF cytology did not reveal malignant cells. Although the sIL-2R level was mildly elevated, this finding alone was insufficient to support a diagnosis of CNS lymphoma.

Autoimmune diseases were evaluated but excluded due to the absence of characteristic systemic symptoms and negative screening autoantibody test results. Neurosarcoidosis was included in the differential diagnosis because MRI findings may be normal in the early stages, but subsequent laboratory data made this diagnosis unlikely. The mildly elevated sIL-2R level was regarded as a non-specific finding.

This patient was diagnosed with definite BD based on clinical features, including fever and fatigue, oral aphthae (two points), genital aphthosis (two points) and a folliculitis-like rash (one point). According to the International Criteria for Behçet’s Disease, he had a total score of five points (≥4 indicates definite disease). In particular, genital aphthosis is considered to be a specific finding of BD, and these findings strongly suggest BD. After excluding infectious, neoplastic and autoimmune causes, a diagnosis of acute neuro-BD was established, with CNS symptoms in the context of mucocutaneous findings.

Treatment:

On day 1 of admission, an underlying inflammatory condition was suspected, although the exact aetiology remained unclear. Empirical antimicrobial therapy with cefepime was initiated after blood cultures were collected, targeting a potential bacterial infection despite the absence of a specific infectious focus.

On day 3, the patient’s headache and vertigo worsened, suggesting CNS inflammation or infection. Acyclovir was administered empirically but was discontinued after tests ruled out HSV and VZV encephalitis.

On day 8, colchicine was started following the diagnosis of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD. Oral aphthous lesions were treated with topical corticosteroid ointment. Genital ulcers and cutaneous lesions were managed with silver sulfadiazine cream. SIADH was managed conservatively with fluid restriction (<800 mL/day), careful daily monitoring of serum sodium and avoidance of the rapid correction of sodium levels. Hypertonic saline and vasopressin receptor antagonists were not required.

Outcome and follow-up:

The patient’s fever resolved, and his appetite improved the day after he started taking colchicine. Over the following 2 weeks, his symptoms, including vertigo, general malaise, hiccups and mucocutaneous lesions, gradually improved. By day 16, serum sodium had normalised to 136 mmol/L, and ADH levels had decreased to 1.8 pg/mL. Myogenic enzyme levels also returned to normal.

Importantly, no systemic corticosteroids or other immunosuppressants were used during treatment. The patient was discharged after a 1 month hospitalisation, during which recovery from the acute phase was achieved, hyponatraemia was carefully monitored and managed, and rehabilitation was provided for the associated decline in physical function. Colchicine therapy was continued for approximately 4 years, with no recurrence of BD or neurological symptoms during that time. After his BD stabilised, biguanide therapy was initiated, and good glycaemic control has since been maintained.

Discussion:

This case involved the simultaneous diagnosis of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD, which is a rare clinical scenario. It also demonstrated that acute neuro-BD can be complicated by SIADH. The resolution of mucocutaneous symptoms, neurological manifestations and hyponatraemia following colchicine monotherapy suggests a possible direct association between BD, neuro-BD and SIADH. In patients with BD who present with fever or headache accompanied by hyponatraemia, clinicians should consider acute neuro-BD as a potential underlying cause.

This case is notable for the simultaneous diagnosis of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD, supported by mucocutaneous lesions and CNS involvement. Typically, major organ involvement, such as CNS lesions, occurs around 4 years after the initial diagnosis of BD, and neurological manifestations at presentation are uncommon, occurring in fewer than 5% of patients. Only a minority of patients, approximately 10%, experience headaches that are directly related to neuro-BD. Therefore, the initial recognition of neuro-BD in this case was inherently challenging. Acute neuro-BD most commonly presents as a self-limiting meningoencephalitis, with or without focal neurological deficits. In this patient, brain MRI revealed no specific abnormalities at the typical BD lesion sites, including the hypothalamic region. Acute neuro-BD may present as aseptic meningitis without parenchymal brain lesions, and a normal brain MRI, particularly in the early phase of the disease, does not exclude the diagnosis. This lack of radiological findings further complicated the diagnostic process. Although HLA-B51 testing was performed, it did not contribute to establishing the diagnosis. This aligns with international BD criteria, which do not require HLA-B51 and rely mainly on clinical features. This case highlights the importance of considering neuro-BD when patients with mucocutaneous findings present with non-specific neurological symptoms.

In the initial phase of the differential diagnosis, we prioritised infection as a cause of CNS inflammation because of the combination of fever of unclear origin, neurological symptoms and hyponatraemia; however, we did not identify any specific pathogen. We also considered other causes of CNS inflammation, including connective tissue diseases and malignancies, and included sarcoidosis and haematologic malignancy in the differential diagnosis. Therefore, as part of an initial broad evaluation, we measured autoimmune serological markers, serum ACE and sIL-2R as screening investigations. We performed these tests in a limited manner as part of an exclusion-based approach rather than based on strong clinical suspicion. We found no clinical, radiological or CSF findings to support these diagnoses, and these investigations did not meaningfully contribute to the final diagnosis. ACE has limited sensitivity for sarcoidosis, and although sIL-2R may be informative in the evaluation of malignant lymphoma, mild elevations are non-specific and may reflect inflammatory activity. In this case, the absence of genital ulcers at the early stage of symptom onset made the diagnosis of BD uncertain, leading to a broad differential diagnosis. This diagnostic process highlights the importance of avoiding indiscriminate testing and emphasises that clinicians should perform such investigations only when supported by appropriate clinical features and interpret them with caution.

Although drug-induced SIADH has been reported in patients with BD receiving medications such as cyclophosphamide, the association between acute neuro-BD and SIADH remains poorly understood. Neuro-BD is characterised by CNS inflammation, which may contribute to dysregulated ADH secretion. Several studies have shown that interleukin-6 (IL-6), a proinflammatory cytokine frequently elevated in neuroinflammatory disorders, is associated with increased ADH release. In BD, aberrant monocyte activation leads to spontaneous IL-6 production, and elevated IL-6 levels have been detected in the CSF of patients with acute neuro-BD. These findings suggested that IL-6–mediated inflammation might contribute to SIADH in this setting. Although IL-6 levels were not measured in the serum or CSF in the present case, the markedly elevated serum CRP concentration may reflect an underlying IL-6–mediated inflammatory response. Although SIADH is associated with various conditions, including CNS inflammation, pulmonary disease, malignancy and certain medications, the present findings do not allow a direct causal link between SIADH and acute neuro-BD to be established. The temporal association observed in this case may reflect a non-specific response to CNS inflammation rather than a disease-specific mechanism.

The present case also illustrates the potential complications of hyponatraemia caused by SIADH in the setting of acute neuro-BD. Previous reports have reported that persistent hiccups may serve as an important clinical clue to severe hyponatraemia caused by SIADH secondary to CNS inflammation, including meningitis. In addition, we measured CK as part of the initial evaluation of fever, arthralgia and general fatigue, which may be related to inflammatory or metabolic muscle damage, and this revealed rhabdomyolysis, a recognised but often overlooked complication of severe hyponatraemia that is not routinely investigated. These manifestations are known, yet often underappreciated, clinical implications of profound hyponatraemia. Clinicians should be aware that apparently unrelated symptoms, such as hiccups or rhabdomyolysis, may reflect underlying electrolyte disturbances, particularly in the context of neuroinflammatory conditions.

Corticosteroids are considered the mainstay of treatment for acute neuro-BD, and most patients respond favourably. However, spontaneous resolution has been reported in cases presenting solely with meningoencephalitis-like symptoms. In the present case, colchicine monotherapy improved mucocutaneous and neurological symptoms, as well as hyponatraemia. Colchicine exerts anti-inflammatory effects by inhibiting neutrophil migration and activation, and by suppressing cytokine production, including IL-1β and IL-6. Although colchicine is widely used for the treatment of mucocutaneous manifestations in BD, systematic reviews have shown that colchicine is ineffective for acute neuro-BD and that immunosuppressive agents are generally required for adequate disease control. In fact, in reported cases, colchicine has been used as an adjunct to corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive therapies, rather than as a monotherapy. Although spontaneous recovery cannot be excluded in this case, our findings suggest that colchicine may be a useful treatment option for acute neuro-BD and related complications such as SIADH, particularly when corticosteroid therapy is undesirable or contraindicated.

Conclusion:

We described a rare case of BD and acute neuro-BD diagnosed concurrently, in which the SIADH complicated the neuro-BD. This case demonstrates that SIADH may occur as a complication of acute neuro-BD, highlighting the importance of clinician awareness of this potential association. Colchicine monotherapy led to rapid improvement in mucocutaneous symptoms, neurological manifestations and hyponatraemia, suggesting that colchicine may be a practical therapeutic option in the early phase of neuro-BD. In patients with BD who present with non-specific CNS symptoms such as fever and headache in combination with hyponatraemia, clinicians should maintain a high suspicion for acute neuro-BD in patients with BD complicated by SIADH.

\title{The Era of 1-bit LLMs: All Large Language Models are in 1.58 Bits}

\begin{abstract}
Emerging developments such as BitNet~\cite{bitnet} are ushering in innovative directions for 1-bit Large Language Models (LLMs). In this study, we propose a ternary 1-bit LLM variant called \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, where each model parameter (weight) can take values from the set \{-1, 0, 1\}. This approach demonstrates that, for an equivalent model size and number of training tokens, our ternary model can achieve perplexity and downstream task results on par with its full-precision counterparts (FP16 or BF16 Transformers), yet offers remarkable reductions in latency, memory usage, throughput requirements, and energy demands. Significantly, this 1.58-bit LLM formulation establishes a novel scaling law and provides a new methodology for training future high-performing, cost-efficient LLMs. Additionally, it sets the stage for an alternative computational paradigm, enabling specialized hardware architectures optimized specifically for 1-bit LLMs.
\end{abstract}

\section{The Era of 1-bit LLMs}

Over the past few years, there has been substantial progress in the development and scalability of Large Language Models (LLMs) within the AI domain. While these models exhibit impressive abilities across numerous natural language processing applications, their ever-expanding scale introduces difficulties in practical implementation and intensifies worries about both ecological and financial costs arising from elevated energy requirements.

A common strategy for tackling these issues involves applying post-training quantization to produce low-bit models suitable for inference~\cite{smoothquant, gptq, quip, quip_sharp}. By lowering the bit-width of both weights and activations, this method substantially decreases the memory footprint and computational demands of LLMs. Recent developments have focused on transitioning from 16-bit representations down to more compact 4-bit formats~\cite{gptq, awq}. Nevertheless, despite its prevalence in production-level LLM deployments, post-training quantization remains a sub-optimal solution.

Recent advancements in 1-bit model designs, exemplified by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, indicate a valuable approach for lowering the computational expenses of LLMs without sacrificing effectiveness. Standard LLM implementations utilize 16-bit floating-point representations (such as FP16 or BF16), and matrix multiplication accounts for most of their computational workload. Consequently, the predominant source of computational overhead arises from floating-point arithmetic operations. In comparison, BitNet restricts its matrix multiplications to integer addition, resulting in substantial reductions in energy consumption for LLMs. Given that power consumption often sets the upper bound for computation on many hardware platforms, these energy reductions may also contribute to increased processing speed.

Beyond computation, moving model parameters from DRAM into the on-chip accelerator's memory, such as SRAM, can impose substantial overhead during inference. Efforts to increase the size of SRAM for enhanced throughput often result in costs that greatly exceed those of DRAM. 1-bit LLMs, relative to their full-precision counterparts, demand considerably less memory, both in terms of storage capacity and bandwidth. As a result, transferring weights from DRAM becomes less resource-intensive and quicker, enabling more rapid and cost-effective inference.

In this study, we present a notable 1-bit LLM variation termed \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, wherein each model parameter is ternary, assuming values from the set \{-1, 0, 1\}. By incorporating an additional 0 value into the standard 1-bit BitNet framework, the model operates at 1.58 bits within the binary numeral system. \text{BitNet b1.58} preserves all major strengths of its 1-bit predecessor, including its novel computation scheme that almost entirely eliminates multiplication in matrix operations, allowing for extensive optimization. It also maintains the same energy usage as the original 1-bit BitNet, while offering superior efficiency in memory, throughput, and latency relative to FP16 LLM baselines. Moreover, \text{BitNet b1.58} introduces two key improvements. First, it enhances representational power by explicitly supporting feature filtering—enabled by permitting weight values of 0—which considerably boosts the capabilities of 1-bit LLMs. Second, experimental results indicate that, beginning at the 3B parameter scale, \text{BitNet b1.58} is able to achieve parity with full-precision (i.e., FP16) baselines on both perplexity and downstream task metrics under comparable configurations (e.g., model size and training data volume).

\section{BitNet b1.58}

\text{BitNet b1.58} utilizes the BitNet architecture, a type of Transformer where the traditional \emph{nn.Linear} layer is substituted with \emph{BitLinear}. The model is initialized and trained from the beginning, employing weights quantized to 1.58 bits and activations at 8 bits. It departs from the original BitNet by incorporating several adjustments, outlined as follows.

\paragraph{Quantization Function.} To ensure weights are limited to the set \{-1, 0, +1\}, we employ the \emph{absmean} quantization function. This function initially normalizes the weight matrix by dividing by its mean absolute value, followed by rounding each entry to its nearest value in \{-1, 0, +1\}:

\begin{equation}
    \widetilde{W} = \mathrm{RoundClip}(\frac{W}{\gamma + \epsilon}, -1, 1),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \mathrm{RoundClip}(x, a, b) = \max(a, \min(b, \mathrm{round}(x))),
\end{equation}

The quantization method applied to activations mirrors that utilized in BitNet, with the exception that, prior to the application of non-linear functions, activations are not rescaled to the $[0, Q_b]$ interval. Rather, for each token, activations are mapped to the range $[-Q_b, Q_b]$, thereby eliminating the necessity for zero-point quantization. This adjustment streamlines both the implementation and broader system optimization processes, and according to our experimental results, has only a minimal impact on performance.

\paragraph{LLaMA-alike Components.}

The LLaMA~\cite{llama, llama2} architecture has become the standard foundation for many open-source LLM initiatives. To align with and support this ecosystem, \text{BitNet b1.58} has been engineered with LLaMA-inspired structural elements. Notably, our model incorporates RMSNorm~\cite{rmsnorm}, SwiGLU~\cite{swiglu}, rotary embedding~\cite{rope}, and excludes all bias terms. This design choice ensures that \text{BitNet b1.58} can be readily deployed within major open-source platforms such as Huggingface, vLLM~\cite{vllm}, and llama.cpp\footnote{\url{https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp}}, requiring minimal adaptation.

\section{Results}

We conducted a comparative analysis between \text{BitNet b1.58} and our re-implemented FP16 \text{LLaMA LLM} across multiple parameter scales. Both models underwent pre-training for 100 billion tokens using the RedPajama dataset~\cite{redpajama} to maintain experimental parity. For evaluation, we assessed zero-shot generalization on several language understanding benchmarks, including ARC-Easy~\cite{arc}, ARC-Challenge~\cite{arc}, Hellaswag~\cite{hellaswag}, Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, OpenbookQA~\cite{openbookqa}, and BoolQ~\cite{boolq}. Additionally, we measured validation perplexity using the WikiText2~\cite{wikitext2} and C4~\cite{c4} corpora.

We evaluated both the runtime GPU memory usage and inference latency for \text{LLaMA LLM} and \text{BitNet b1.58}. The measurements were obtained utilizing the FasterTransformer\footnote{\url{https://github.com/NVIDIA/FasterTransformer}} framework, an inference engine tailored for low-latency LLM execution on GPUs. Additionally, for \text{BitNet b1.58}, we incorporated the Ladder~\cite{ladder} 2-bit kernel into our testing environment. To highlight the dominant expense in inference, we present the results in terms of time consumed per generated output token.

Table~\ref{tab:ppl} presents both the perplexity values and computational costs associated with \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. The results indicate that, in terms of perplexity, \text{BitNet b1.58} reaches parity with the full precision \text{LLaMA LLM} at a 3B parameter scale, yet achieves 2.71 times higher speed and utilizes 3.55 times less GPU memory. Notably, the 3.9B variant of \text{BitNet b1.58} is 2.4 times faster, requires 3.32 times less memory, and delivers considerably superior performance compared to the 3B version of \text{LLaMA LLM}.

Table~\ref{tab:zero-shot} presents a comprehensive breakdown of zero-shot accuracy across the evaluated end tasks. For this assessment, we adhered to the methodology outlined by \emph{lm-evaluation-harness}\footnote{\url{https://github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness}}. The data indicate that, with increased model size, the difference in performance between \text{BitNet b1.58} and the \text{LLaMA LLM} diminishes. Notably, from the 3B model size onwards, \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves parity with the full-precision baseline. Echoing the trends observed in perplexity, the outcomes on end tasks highlight that \text{BitNet b1.58} at 3.9B surpasses \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B, all while requiring less memory and offering reduced latency. These results underscore \text{BitNet b1.58} as a Pareto improvement over current state-of-the-art LLMs.

\paragraph{Memory and Latency}

We extended our experiments by increasing the model sizes to 7B, 13B, and 70B, subsequently analyzing the associated costs. As shown in Figure~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy}, both latency and memory utilization exhibit clear scaling patterns, with acceleration gains becoming more pronounced as model size grows. Notably, \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B demonstrates a 4.1-fold improvement in speed compared to the \text{LLaMA LLM} reference model. This improvement is attributed to the fact that the computational overhead of \emph{nn.Linear} increases with larger model sizes. Memory usage follows a comparable pattern because, although embeddings are maintained in full precision, their relative memory footprint diminishes as model scale increases. All measurements for latency and memory employed a 2-bit kernel, indicating that additional cost reductions are feasible with further optimization.

\paragraph{Energy}

We additionally analyze the energy requirements associated with arithmetic operations for both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. The analysis predominantly emphasizes matrix multiplication, as it represents the principal energy expenditure in large language models. Figure~\ref{fig:energy} displays the breakdown of energy consumption. Most of the energy consumed by \text{BitNet b1.58} originates from INT8 addition operations, whereas \text{LLaMA LLM} expends energy primarily on FP16 additions and multiplications. Referencing the energy estimation model from~\cite{energycost, pokebnn}, \text{BitNet b1.58} demonstrates a 71.4-fold reduction in arithmetic operations energy for matrix multiplications on 7nm technology compared to its counterpart. We extend this analysis by providing end-to-end energy consumption measurements for models processing 512 tokens. Our findings indicate that as model sizes increase, \text{BitNet b1.58} exhibits a greater relative advantage in energy efficiency over the FP16-based \text{LLaMA LLM}. This trend emerges because the proportion of energy devoted to \emph{nn.Linear} operations rises with larger models, while the contribution from other parts becomes less significant as model scale increases.

\paragraph{Throughput}

We evaluate the throughput performance of \text{BitNet b1.58} and the 70B-parameter \text{LLaMA LLM} across two 80GB A100 GPUs, employing pipeline parallelism~\cite{gpipe} to enable execution of the \text{LLaMA LLM} 70B model. By progressively increasing the batch size to the maximum capacity permitted by the GPU memory at a fixed sequence length of 512, we observed notable differences. As presented in Table~\ref{tab:throughput}, \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B accommodates a batch size up to 11 times larger than that of \text{LLaMA LLM}, which translates to an 8.9-fold improvement in throughput.

\textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58} introduces a novel scaling relationship between model accuracy and inference resource demands}. By examining Figures~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy} and \ref{fig:energy}, we observe the following correspondences for model size efficiency when comparing 1.58-bit and 16-bit representations:

\begin{itemize}
    \item The 13B BitNet b1.58 outperforms the 3B FP16 LLM regarding latency, memory footprint, and energy usage.
    \item The 30B BitNet b1.58 demonstrates greater efficiency than the 7B FP16 LLM across latency, memory requirements, and energy consumption.
    \item The 70B BitNet b1.58 surpasses the 13B FP16 LLM in latency, memory usage, and energy savings.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Training with 2T Tokens}

The quantity of training tokens plays a significant role in the effectiveness of LLMs. To assess how \text{BitNet b1.58} performs when scaled with respect to token count, we trained a \text{BitNet b1.58} model on 2T tokens, adhering to the data preparation strategy employed by StableLM-3B~\cite{StableLM-3B-4E1T}, which represents the current leading open-source 3B model. Evaluation for both models was conducted using a suite of benchmarks, including Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, SciQ~\cite{sciq}, LAMBADA~\cite{lambada}, and ARC-easy~\cite{arc}. The zero-shot accuracy outcomes are summarized in Table~\ref{tab:2t}. For tasks assessed with both accuracy and normalized accuracy metrics, we averaged the two scores. The results for StableLM 3B trained on 2T tokens are referenced from its official technical report. Our analysis demonstrates that \text{BitNet b1.58} consistently outperforms its counterpart across all evaluated tasks, suggesting that 1.58-bit LLMs are also highly capable of generalizing to diverse tasks.

\section{Discussion and Future Work}

\textbf{1-bit Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs}

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures have demonstrated notable cost-efficiency for large language models (LLMs). Although MoE methods decrease computation in terms of FLOPs, their utility is constrained by substantial memory demands and considerable inter-chip communication requirements. The advent of 1.58-bit LLMs offers solutions to these obstacles. The decreased memory usage permits MoE models to be deployed across fewer devices. In addition, lower memory requirements translate to a marked reduction in the communication overhead for transmitting activations between nodes. Ideally, if the whole model fits within the memory of one chip, communication overhead is eliminated altogether.

\textbf{Native Support of Long Sequence in LLMs}

With the rise of LLMs, efficiently managing extended input sequences has emerged as a key requirement. A significant obstacle in processing lengthy sequences is the substantial memory usage caused by KV cache storage. \text{BitNet b1.58} makes considerable progress toward enabling native support for longer contexts by lowering activation precision from 16 bits to 8 bits. This reduction permits a doubling of context window length while maintaining identical resource constraints. Moreover, activations can potentially be compressed losslessly to 4 bits or below, particularly for 1.58-bit LLMs—a direction we propose for future investigation.

\textbf{LLMs on Edge and Mobile}

Deploying 1.58-bit LLMs presents a significant opportunity to boost the effectiveness of language models operating on edge and mobile platforms. Such platforms frequently encounter constraints in both memory and processing capacity, which can hamper the implementation and efficiency of traditional LLMs. With their notably lower demands for memory and power, 1.58-bit LLMs can be feasibly integrated into these environments, unlocking new possibilities for applications that were once infeasible. As a result, this advancement has the potential to substantially broaden the functionality of edge and mobile devices, fostering innovative uses of LLMs. In addition, 1.58-bit LLMs are especially compatible with CPUs—the primary type of processor in edge and mobile contexts—rendering it possible for models such as \text{BitNet b1.58} to operate with heightened efficiency and further expand the computational capabilities of these devices.

\textbf{New Hardware for 1-bit LLMs}

Recent developments such as~Groq\footnote{\url{https://groq.com/}} have shown encouraging outcomes and significant promise in constructing specialized hardware, such as LPUs, tailored for LLMs. Building on these advancements, we propose and advocate for the creation of novel hardware and system architectures that are explicitly fine-tuned for 1-bit LLMs, taking advantage of the innovative computation paradigm introduced in BitNet~\cite{bitnet}.

\end{document}<|im_end|>

United States Court of Appeals

FOR THE DISTRICT OF COLUMBIA CIRCUIT

Argued February 12, 2025 Decided October 3, 2025

No. 24-5168

AMERICA FIRST LEGAL FOUNDATION, APPELLANT

v.

JAMIESON GREER, IN HIS OFFICIAL CAPACITY, APPELLEE

Appeal from the United States District Court

for the District of Columbia (No. 1:22-cv-03576)

Andrew J. Block argued the cause for appellant. With him on the briefs was Reed D. Rubinstein.

David L. Peters, an attorney with the U.S. Department of Justice, presented oral argument on behalf of the appellee. Accompanying him on the briefs were Brian M. Boynton, who served as Principal Deputy Assistant Attorney General when the brief was submitted; Brett A. Shumate, in the capacity of Acting Assistant Attorney General; and Matthew M. Graves, who held the position of United States Attorney at the time of filing. Michael

S. Raab, Attorney, Susan K. Ullman, General Counsel, U.S.

Office of Special Counsel, and Hnin Khaing, Senior Litigation Counsel.

GINSBURG, Senior Circuit Judge.

Opinion for the Court filed by Circuit Judge KATSAS.

KATSAS, Circuit Judge: America First Legal Foundation initiated this action seeking to require the Office of Special Counsel to examine if a federal agency unjustifiably rejected AFL’s request under the Freedom of Information Act. The district court ruled against AFL’s claims on substantive grounds. We determine, however, that AFL does not have Article III standing to advance these claims.

I

A

The Office of Special Counsel (OSC) functions as an investigative and prosecutorial body created to ensure “a fair, efficient, and lawfully-conducted Civil Service.” Frazier v. MSPB, 672 F.2d 150, 162 (D.C. Cir. 1982). Under the authority granted by the Civil Service Reform Act, the OSC is tasked with investigating “prohibited personnel practices.” If its investigation determines further action is necessary, OSC may initiate enforcement cases before the Merit Systems Protection Board to secure corrective measures pursuant to 5 U.S.C. § 1214 or pursue disciplinary sanctions in accordance with 5 U.S.C. § 1215. Section 1214 delineates the procedural framework for addressing prohibited personnel practices, which includes requirements for keeping complainants apprised of the investigation’s status. See, for instance, 5 U.S.C. § 1214(a)(1)(B)–(D).

Section 1216 grants OSC the authority to investigate and pursue remedies for specific additional categories of employee misconduct within the government. Pertinently, it mandates that OSC “shall … conduct an investigation of any allegation concerning arbitrary or capricious withholding of information prohibited under section 552”—that is, instances of information being withheld in violation of the Freedom of Information Act. U.S.C. § 1216(a)(3). When such alleged breaches of FOIA occur, OSC is authorized to “investigate and seek corrective action under section 1214 and disciplinary action under section 1215 in the same manner as if a prohibited personnel practice were implicated.” Id. § 1216(c).

FOIA includes a distinct section dedicated to OSC enforcement. According to this provision, when a court (1) directs the release of agency records that were wrongfully withheld under FOIA, (2) imposes attorneys’ fees on the agency, and (3) expressly raises concerns regarding whether the agency’s withholding was “arbitrary or capricious,” OSC is required to “promptly initiate a proceeding to determine whether disciplinary action is warranted against the officer or employee who was primarily responsible for the withholding.” U.S.C. § 552(a)(4)(F)(i).

B

In its inquiry into judicial recusals, AFL submitted a request to the Department of Justice seeking specific email correspondence exchanged between an external attorney and the DOJ’s Civil Division. AFL contends that the Civil Division rejected this request on questionable grounds, namely, that it refrains from searching employee email accounts for relevant documents without first obtaining the consent of those employees. Following this denial, AFL filed a lawsuit against DOJ pursuant to FOIA. In response to the litigation, DOJ has already provided—and continues to provide—documents that are responsive to AFL’s request. The adequacy of this ongoing production is not a matter for consideration in this context.

AFL further requested that OSC examine the DOJ’s purported FOIA practice of refusing to search employee emails without their permission. Citing section 1216(a)(3), which requires OSC to “conduct an investigation” into arbitrary FOIA denials, AFL emphasized this statutory duty. However, OSC declined to initiate an investigation, asserting that the prerequisites outlined in section 552(a)(4)(F)(i) necessary to commence such an inquiry had not been met in relation to the alleged FOIA policy. OSC also maintained that even though AFL referenced section 1216, it did not obligate OSC to investigate if the conditions prescribed by FOIA itself were lacking.

The district court partially ruled in AFL’s favor but ultimately dismissed the case on the grounds that it failed to state a viable claim. The court determined that section 552(a)(4)(F)(i) does not curtail OSC’s authority to pursue investigations of purported FOIA violations under section 1216. See Am. First Legal Found. v. Kerner, No. 22-cv-3576, 2023 WL 9546644, at *5 (D.D.C. Sept. 29, 2023). Accordingly, the court found that OSC’s refusal to investigate stemmed from an erroneous interpretation of the law. Id. However, the court declined to mandate that OSC investigate any FOIA-related policy, reasoning that section 1216(c) vests such discretion with OSC. Id. at *6. The matter was sent back to OSC for additional review. Id. When OSC again opted not to investigate, AFL requested entry of a final judgment, after which the district court dismissed the remainder of its claims. J.A. 68. AFL appealed, and we now conduct a de novo review. N. Am. Butterfly Ass’n v. Wolf, 977 F.3d 1244, 1249 (D.C. Cir. 2020).

II

Prior to addressing the substantive issues, it is necessary to determine if AFL met the prerequisites for Article III standing, as this issue is central to the district court’s subject-matter jurisdiction. See Steel Co. v. Citizens for a Better Env’t, 523 U.S. 83, 101–02 (1998). Since neither side originally discussed standing, we directed the parties to submit additional briefs specifically on this matter.

Article III of the Constitution confines the exercise of federal “judicial Power” to the resolution of particular “Cases” and “Controversies.” U.S. Const. Art. III, § 2, cl. 1. The doctrine of standing arises from this constitutional case-or-controversy limitation. Allen v. Wright, 468 U.S. 737, 750 (1984). For a plaintiff to meet the requirements of Article III standing, there must be a demonstration of an injury that is traceable to the conduct of the defendant and capable of being remedied by a favorable court ruling. Lujan v. Defs. of Wildlife, 504 U.S. 555, 560–61 (1992). Such injury must constitute “an invasion of a legally protected interest” and must be both “concrete and particularized” as well as “actual or imminent, not conjectural or hypothetical.” Id. at 560 (cleaned up). At the stage where a motion to dismiss is considered, the plaintiff is required to allege facts that plausibly establish each element necessary for standing. Food & Water Watch, Inc. v. Vilsack, 808 F.3d 905, 913 (D.C. Cir. 2015).

In its supplemental brief, AFL asserts that OSC infringed upon its purported procedural entitlement to an investigation as provided by section 1216. However, mere deprivation of a procedural right, absent any concrete interest adversely affected by such deprivation—a procedural right in isolation—does not satisfy the requirements for Article III standing. Summers v. Earth Island Inst., 555 U.S. 488, 496 (2009); see also Citizens for Const. Integrity v. Census Bureau, 115 F.4th 618, 625 (D.C. Cir. 2024). In an effort to address this deficiency, AFL argues that OSC’s decision not to pursue an investigation has resulted in two specific, tangible informational injuries: namely, that DOJ has failed to disclose the records requested by AFL under FOIA, and that OSC has withheld the notices and information mandated during the course of an investigation pursuant to section 1214.

By framing its claim in this manner, AFL attempts to take advantage of the more permissive standing standards applicable in cases asserting procedural injury. In these instances, when plaintiffs contend that a statutory procedural right—enacted to safeguard their specific, tangible interest—has been violated, courts tend to ease, but not eliminate entirely, the traditional requirements concerning imminence and redressability. However, courts maintain the requirements of injury in fact and causation. See Ctr. for Law & Educ. v. Dep’t of Educ., 396 F.3d 1152, 1157 (D.C. Cir. 2005); see also Fla. Audubon Soc’y v. Bentsen, 94 F.3d 658, 664–65 (D.C. Cir. 1996) (en banc). Plaintiffs in such procedural injury cases must still establish that the infringement of a procedural right has produced harm to a “concrete, particularized interest.” Ctr. for Law & Educ., 396 F.3d at 1157. Here, AFL fails to meet these obligations, including even the diminished threshold for redressability. Since AFL falls short of satisfying the procedural injury standing prerequisites, we need not resolve the distinction between procedural and substantive injuries in this context.

AFL’s primary theory is that OSC’s alleged neglect in initiating an investigation under section 1216 has ultimately resulted in AFL being deprived of the records it sought from DOJ through a FOIA request. This line of reasoning, however, is fundamentally flawed with respect to both causation and redressability. To begin with, any purported informational harm stemming from DOJ’s denial of access to agency documents is clearly attributable to DOJ, not to OSC. Furthermore, the hypothetical link between OSC’s inaction under section 1216 and DOJ’s decision not to fulfill the FOIA request is far too conjectural and indirect to fulfill the requirements for Article III standing. The speculative sequence postulated by AFL is as follows: Had OSC conducted the allegedly obligatory investigation pursuant to section 1216, OSC might have opted to initiate corrective action under section 1214, or disciplinary measures under section 1215, against the DOJ personnel implicated. See 5 U.S.C. §§ 1214(b)(3), 1215(a)(1). Subsequently, the MSPB could potentially have imposed sanctions on those employees for unjustified withholding of records under FOIA. See id. §§ 1214(b)(4)(A), 1215(a)(3)(A). As a result, DOJ might then have elected to release the requested documents. However, this hypothetical scenario rests on assumptions regarding the actions of two entities not party to the present litigation—the MSPB and DOJ—whereas “causation generally cannot rely on speculation about the unfettered choices made by independent actors not before the courts.” FDA v. All. for Hippocratic Med., 602 U.S. 367, 383 (2024) (internal quotation marks omitted). Additionally, it hinges on conjecture regarding how OSC might deploy its enforcement authority, a domain in which judicial intervention is generally prohibited. See Heckler v. Chaney, 470 U.S. 821, 831–32 (1985). In light of such uncertainty, AFL cannot demonstrate that a favorable outcome in this case “would very likely spur the next steps necessary to redress” the alleged informational harm. Hecate Energy LLC v. FERC, 126 F.4th 660, 667 (D.C. Cir. 2025). AFL also neglects the well-established principle that redressability is typically a function of whether a judgment is binding on the actual parties to the lawsuit, not on whether its rationale “might persuade actors who are not before the court.” Haaland v. Brackeen, 599 U.S. 255, 293–94 (2023). Therefore, AFL is unable to ground its standing on the asserted interest in the information it requested from DOJ under FOIA.

AFL’s alternative basis for standing, which rests on an alleged informational interest in the results of the OSC’s investigation, proves equally unpersuasive. For a plaintiff to establish informational standing, there must be a tangible harm stemming from the denial of information that a statute affirmatively grants the plaintiff the right to obtain. See Am. Soc’y for Prevention of Cruelty to Animals v. Feld Ent., Inc., 659 F.3d 13, 23 (D.C. Cir. 2011) (citing Zivotofsky ex rel. Ari Z. v. Sec’y of State, 444 F.3d 614, 618 (D.C. Cir. 2006)); see also FEC v. Akins, 524 U.S. 11, 21 (1998); Pub. Citizen v. DOJ, 491 U.S. 440, 448–50 (1989). In the present case, however, even assuming that section 1216(a)(3) imposes an obligation on OSC to investigate FOIA violations, the provision does not confer upon AFL a right to obtain information. AFL counters by arguing that, were OSC to undertake the mandatory investigation purportedly required by section 1216, it might at its discretion furnish AFL with notices and information analogous to those mandated during “prohibited personnel practice” investigations under section 1214. Nevertheless, AFL stops short of arguing that OSC is compelled to incorporate the procedural notice requirements of section 1214 into section 1216 proceedings. In fact, AFL concedes precedent establishing that OSC retains discretion with respect to applying section 1214’s procedural framework to section 1216 cases. See Carson v. U.S. Off. of Special Couns., 633 F.3d 487, 495–96 (6th Cir. 2011). Because there is no claim of a statutory right to information under section 1214, AFL cannot demonstrate a cognizable injury supporting its informational standing theory. For the same reasons, since OSC is not required to extend section 1214 notice procedures to section 1216 inquiries, a court order compelling a section 1216 investigation would not be likely to remedy the informational injury asserted by AFL.

III

Because AFL lacks Article III standing, we vacate the district court’s judgment and remand with instructions to dismiss for lack of subject-matter jurisdiction.

So ordered.
