Acute neurological Behçet’s disease complicated by a syndrome of inappropriate secretion of antidiuretic hormone

Eiichi Kakehi, Yukako Matsumoto, Shigehisa Sakurai, Kazuhiko Kotani

Abstract:

Summary: We describe an uncommon presentation of acute neurological Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD) that was simultaneously identified with systemic Behçet’s disease (BD), fulfilling the criteria for definite BD, and further complicated by syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH). A male patient in his sixth decade developed fever, headache, and various mucocutaneous manifestations, such as oral aphthae, folliculitis-like eruptions, genital ulcers, and was noted to have hyponatraemia. The clinical and mucocutaneous evidence supported a diagnosis of BD. Despite a normal brain MRI, analysis of cerebrospinal fluid revealed aseptic meningitis, in line with acute neuro-BD. Laboratory investigations established the presence of SIADH. Treatment with colchicine alone resulted in complete remission of mucocutaneous signs, neurological symptoms, and normalization of sodium levels, obviating the need for corticosteroids or additional immunosuppressive therapies. This report highlights a potential link between acute neuro-BD and SIADH, emphasizing the necessity for clinicians to be vigilant for simultaneous neurological and endocrine disorders in newly identified BD patients.

Background:

Behçet’s disease (BD) is a persistent, multisystem inflammatory condition, marked by recurring oral and genital ulcers, uveitis, as well as various skin manifestations. Neurological complications, identified as neuro-Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD), arise in about 10%–20% of individuals with BD and often appear several years subsequent to the initial diagnosis. Typically, acute neuro-BD manifests with meningoencephalitis and parenchymal brain lesions, which are most frequently located in the brainstem. Although neuro-BD tends to develop later, it is uncommon for both BD and neuro-BD to be diagnosed simultaneously. When simultaneous diagnosis occurs, clinicians must evaluate both mucocutaneous signs and neurological complaints, such as headaches, behavioral disturbances, psychiatric manifestations, and focal neurological deficits.

The syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH) constitutes a common etiology of hyponatraemia and is frequently attributed to central nervous system (CNS) inflammation, malignancy, or use of specific medications. Owing to the inflammatory pathology characteristic of acute neuro-BD, these patients may face an increased risk of developing SIADH. While SIADH has been documented in association with BD patients undergoing therapies like cyclophosphamide, the occurrence of SIADH due solely to CNS inflammation in the context of acute neuro-BD, without any medication triggers, remains insufficiently established.

Here, we present an unusual instance in which BD and acute neuro-BD were identified at the same time, complicated by the onset of SIADH. This case underscores the need to recognize SIADH as a possible complication of acute neuro-BD, especially in patients exhibiting vague CNS symptoms and unexplained hyponatraemia.

Case presentation:

A man of Asian descent in his 60s arrived at the emergency department, reporting a 3-day duration of fever, headache, generalized fatigue, reduced appetite, ongoing hiccups, and joint pain localized to the shoulders and ankles. He also described experiencing oral aphthae.

His past medical history was notable for type 2 diabetes mellitus; however, he was not undergoing any medical management, largely due to a lack of awareness about his illness and failure to adhere to dietary or exercise recommendations. There was no significant family history, including no known instances of BD among his relatives. His alcohol intake was occasional, he was a non-smoker, and there were no recent episodes of travel, animal contact, or exposure to ill individuals. The patient was employed in the metalworking field and had not engaged in recent sexual activity.

During assessment, the patient was alert, though notably fatigued. Recorded vital parameters included a temperature of 38.1°C, blood pressure measuring 135/73 mm Hg, a regular heart rate of 119 beats per minute, respiratory rate at 19 breaths per minute, and oxygen saturation of 96% while breathing ambient air. Physical evaluation identified oral aphthae and rashes resembling folliculitis distributed on the face and trunk (figure 1A, B, and C). Examinations of the cardiopulmonary and abdominal systems were without significant findings. Manual muscle testing showed preserved strength in both upper and lower limbs. No peripheral oedema was present, and clinical assessment indicated a euvolemic state. The patient was subsequently hospitalized for additional diagnostic workup.

Investigations:

The initial laboratory analysis showed significant hyponatraemia at 124 mmol/L, alongside raised levels of creatine kinase (1349 U/L), aspartate aminotransferase (77 U/L), alanine aminotransferase (64 U/L), C-reactive protein (CRP) at 180 mg/L (reference, <3 mg/L), and erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 70 mm/hour (reference, <15 mm/hour), while renal function remained within normal limits. The patient also exhibited hyperglycaemia (plasma glucose 14.2 mmol/L) and an increased HbA1c of 8.2%, indicating inadequate glycaemic management. Screening for hepatitis B and C returned negative.

Endocrine assessment demonstrated thyroid-stimulating hormone within normal range (1.11 mIU/L; reference, 0.50–5.00 mIU/L), free T4 of 1.25 ng/dL (reference, 0.90–1.70 ng/dL), and a serum cortisol level of 42 µg/dL (reference, >6 µg/dL). Serum osmolality was decreased at 267 mOsm/kg (reference, <270 mOsm/kg), while urine osmolality was elevated (900 mOsm/kg; reference, >300 mOsm/kg), accompanied by a high urinary sodium level (88 mmol/L; reference, >20 mmol/L), suggesting compromised water excretion. Despite the presence of hypo-osmolar hyponatraemia, antidiuretic hormone (ADH) was found to be raised (22.4 pg/mL). Electrocardiogram results were normal, and imaging studies including chest X-ray and CT scans of the head, chest, and abdomen revealed no abnormalities. Collectively, these findings were indicative of SIADH.

By the third day, the patient's headache and vertigo intensified. Although neck rigidity and cranial nerve dysfunction were absent, central nervous system (CNS) inflammation was considered. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) of the brain did not reveal any notable abnormalities affecting the hypothalamus or other cerebral regions (figure 2). Analysis of the cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) showed a slightly increased opening pressure (17 cmH₂O), elevated protein concentration (0.97 g/L), and a modest lymphocytic pleocytosis (11 cells/µL). Cultures and molecular diagnostics of the CSF for bacterial and mycobacterial organisms, as well as herpes simplex virus (HSV), varicella zoster virus (VZV), and cryptococcal antigen, returned negative results. Cytological assessment of the CSF demonstrated no evidence of malignant cells.

Additional assessments were undertaken to investigate possible systemic disorders. Workup for sarcoidosis indicated normal lysozyme levels (5.1 µg/mL; reference range 5–10) and angiotensin-converting enzyme levels (11.4 U/L; reference 7–25). Screening for hematological malignancy demonstrated an elevated soluble interleukin-2 receptor (sIL-2R) (704 U/mL; reference range, 122–496 U/mL), while testing for human T-cell leukaemia virus type 1 antibody was negative.

By the fifth day, the patient developed a painful ulceration of the scrotum (figure 1D). The concurrent presence of oral aphthous ulcers and folliculitis-like cutaneous lesions raised suspicion for a systemic presentation of BD. There was no pathergy reaction to skin pricking. Ocular evaluation showed no signs of uveitis. The patient tested positive for HLA-B51.

Differential diagnosis:

Upon initial assessment, the presence of fever, headache, general malaise, and hyponatraemia pointed toward either a central nervous system infection or a systemic inflammatory process. Viral meningoencephalitis, particularly due to HSV or VZV, was the primary concern. Nonetheless, cerebrospinal fluid analysis returned negative for viral antigens and antibodies, thereby eliminating this possibility. Bacterial meningitis was similarly contemplated but dismissed owing to a lack of neutrophilic dominance, only a mild lymphocytic pleocytosis, and sterile CSF cultures. Tuberculous meningitis was ruled out as acid-fast staining and tuberculosis DNA tests were negative.

Given the neurological symptoms, a primary CNS malignancy was another consideration. However, imaging via brain MRI revealed no abnormalities, and no malignant cells were identified in CSF cytology. Although the soluble interleukin-2 receptor (sIL-2R) level was modestly elevated, this by itself was insufficient to confirm CNS lymphoma.

Autoimmune disorders were systematically excluded on the basis of absent hallmark systemic manifestations and negative autoantibody screening tests. Neurosarcoidosis remained in the differential diagnosis as normal MRI findings can occur in its early stages, but further laboratory workup ultimately argued against it. The slight increase in sIL-2R was deemed non-specific.

The diagnosis of definite BD was established according to clinical presentation: fever and fatigue, oral aphthous ulcers (assigned two points), genital aphthosis (two points), and a folliculitis-like eruption (one point). He accumulated five points based on the International Criteria for Behçet’s Disease (with a threshold of four or more for a definitive diagnosis). Notably, the presence of genital aphthosis is highly specific for BD and was a strong indicator in this context. After thoroughly excluding infectious, neoplastic, and autoimmune etiologies, the diagnosis of acute neuro-BD was confirmed, characterized by CNS involvement in the presence of mucocutaneous manifestations.

Treatment:

Upon admission (day 1), clinicians suspected the presence of an underlying inflammatory disorder, though its precise cause was not identified. Empirical cefepime therapy commenced after obtaining blood cultures, in order to address a possible bacterial infection, despite the lack of an identifiable source.

By day 3, the patient’s headache and vertigo had intensified, raising concerns about possible CNS inflammation or infection. Empirical acyclovir therapy was started, but discontinued once laboratory results excluded HSV and VZV encephalitis.

On day 8, following the confirmed diagnosis of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD, colchicine therapy was introduced. Oral aphthous ulcers received topical corticosteroid ointment, while genital ulcers and skin lesions were managed with silver sulfadiazine cream. For SIADH, management consisted of fluid restriction to less than $800\,\mathrm{mL}/\mathrm{day}$, with vigilant daily serum sodium monitoring and caution to prevent overly rapid sodium correction. Treatment with hypertonic saline or vasopressin receptor antagonists was not necessary.

Outcome and follow-up:

The patient experienced resolution of his fever and an increase in appetite within a day of initiating colchicine therapy. Over the subsequent two weeks, there was gradual improvement in his symptoms, such as vertigo, general malaise, hiccups, and mucocutaneous manifestations. By the sixteenth day, his serum sodium concentration had returned to 136 mmol/L and ADH levels had dropped to 1.8 pg/mL. Additionally, levels of myogenic enzymes normalized.

Notably, the management plan did not include systemic corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive agents. The patient was discharged after spending one month in the hospital, a period during which the acute phase resolved, hyponatraemia was actively monitored and treated, and rehabilitation addressed his decreased physical capabilities. Colchicine was maintained for roughly four years, during which neither BD relapse nor neurological complications were observed. Upon stabilization of BD, biguanide therapy was started, resulting in sustained glycaemic control.

Discussion:

In this instance, both systemic BD and acute neuro-BD were concurrently identified, representing an unusual clinical occurrence. The case also illustrated that acute neuro-BD can be accompanied by SIADH. The improvement of mucocutaneous findings, neurological symptoms, and hyponatraemia following colchicine monotherapy indicates a potential direct link among BD, neuro-BD, and SIADH. When encountering patients with BD who develop fever or headache together with hyponatraemia, healthcare providers should entertain the possibility of acute neuro-BD as an underlying etiology.

The simultaneous detection of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD in this case, substantiated by the presence of mucocutaneous lesions and central nervous system involvement, is particularly noteworthy. Ordinarily, manifestations in major organs, such as CNS lesions, emerge on average about four years after a BD diagnosis, and neurological signs are initially present in fewer than 5% of individuals. Additionally, only around 10% of patients develop headaches attributable directly to neuro-BD, rendering early identification of neuro-BD particularly difficult in this context. Most frequently, acute neuro-BD manifests as a self-limiting meningoencephalitis, which may or may not be accompanied by focal neurological deficits. In this patient, MRI of the brain did not reveal any abnormalities at typical BD-affected regions, such as the hypothalamus. Acute neuro-BD is capable of presenting as aseptic meningitis in the absence of parenchymal brain involvement, and a normal brain MRI, especially during the initial stage, does not preclude the diagnosis. The absence of radiological evidence contributed to the diagnostic challenge in this scenario. Although testing for HLA-B51 was undertaken, it did not assist in diagnosis. This is consistent with international criteria for BD, which prioritize clinical findings and do not mandate HLA-B51 positivity. Ultimately, this case underscores the necessity of considering neuro-BD when patients exhibiting mucocutaneous symptoms also report non-specific neurological complaints.

During the initial steps of the differential diagnosis, our main consideration was an infectious etiology for the CNS inflammation, given the presence of unexplained fever, neurological manifestations, and hyponatraemia; nonetheless, no causative pathogen was identified. We expanded our differential to encompass other potential sources of CNS inflammation such as connective tissue disorders and neoplastic processes, specifically including sarcoidosis and haematologic malignancies. Consequently, our comprehensive preliminary workup incorporated autoimmune serological markers, as well as serum ACE and sIL-2R levels, as general screening measures. These investigations were undertaken largely to rule out possibilities rather than out of significant clinical suspicion. Ultimately, neither clinical evaluation, imaging, nor CSF analysis substantiated any of these conditions, and the ancillary tests did not significantly influence our diagnostic conclusions. The sensitivity of ACE for sarcoidosis remains suboptimal, and although sIL-2R might have utility in assessing malignant lymphoma, slight elevations lack specificity and can simply indicate a generic inflammatory response. In this instance, the absence of genital ulcers early in the disease course contributed to uncertainty regarding a diagnosis of BD and prompted an extensive differential approach. This case underscores the necessity of judicious test selection, reminding clinicians to reserve ancillary investigations for situations where they are clearly indicated by clinical context, and to interpret such results with discernment.

While drug-induced SIADH has previously been documented in individuals with BD treated with agents such as cyclophosphamide, the link between acute neuro-BD and the onset of SIADH is not well elucidated. Neuro-BD is defined by inflammatory processes within the CNS, which can potentially result in abnormal secretion of ADH. Evidence from multiple investigations has indicated that interleukin-6 (IL-6)—a proinflammatory cytokine commonly heightened in neuroinflammatory states—is implicated in the enhancement of ADH secretion. In the context of BD, dysregulated monocyte activity results in autonomous IL-6 generation, with increased IL-6 concentrations observed in the CSF of those experiencing acute neuro-BD episodes. This body of research has led to the hypothesis that IL-6–driven inflammation might be involved in the emergence of SIADH in this context. Despite the absence of direct IL-6 measurements in serum or CSF in the current report, the significantly raised serum CRP could indicate an underlying inflammatory process mediated by IL-6. SIADH is recognized as a complication of numerous conditions, encompassing CNS inflammation, pulmonary disorders, neoplasms, and select pharmacotherapies; however, the data presented here are insufficient to conclusively demonstrate a direct causative relationship between SIADH and acute neuro-BD. The temporal concurrence seen in this instance might simply represent a general reaction to CNS inflammation rather than indicating a mechanism specific to this disease entity.

This case further highlights the range of complications that may arise from hyponatraemia induced by SIADH, particularly in the context of acute neuro-BD. Prior literature has suggested that persistent hiccups could serve as a valuable clinical indicator of severe hyponatraemia stemming from SIADH associated with central nervous system inflammation, such as meningitis. Moreover, our initial diagnostic approach included assessing CK levels due to symptoms of fever, arthralgia, and generalized fatigue—findings that may be linked to inflammatory or metabolic muscle injury—and this testing uncovered rhabdomyolysis. Although rhabdomyolysis is a recognized complication of severe hyponatraemia, it is frequently missed in standard evaluations. These clinical presentations, while established, are frequently underestimated manifestations of marked hyponatraemia. It is important for healthcare providers to recognize that symptoms such as persistent hiccups or rhabdomyolysis, which may initially appear unrelated, could signal significant electrolyte imbalances, especially against a background of neuroinflammatory disease.
------
Corticosteroids remain the cornerstone therapy for acute neuro-BD, with the majority of patients showing a positive clinical response. Nonetheless, there are documented instances where patients exhibiting only meningoencephalitis-like features experience spontaneous remission. In the case discussed here, administration of colchicine alone resulted in improvements in both mucocutaneous and neurological manifestations, as well as correction of hyponatraemia. Colchicine mediates its anti-inflammatory activity through inhibition of neutrophil chemotaxis and activation, along with downregulation of pro-inflammatory cytokines such as IL-1β and IL-6. While colchicine is routinely employed for managing mucocutaneous symptoms in BD, meta-analyses indicate limited efficacy for acute neuro-BD, underscoring the need for immunosuppressants to achieve sufficient disease control. Previously, colchicine has mainly been administered in conjunction with corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive medications, rather than as sole therapy. Even though spontaneous improvement cannot be definitively ruled out in this instance, these observations imply that colchicine monotherapy may represent a viable therapeutic alternative for acute neuro-BD and associated complications like SIADH, especially in situations where corticosteroids are not recommended or are contraindicated.

Conclusion:

We presented an uncommon instance in which Behçet’s Disease (BD) and acute neuro-BD were diagnosed simultaneously, and where SIADH served as a complicating factor for the neuro-BD. This report underscores that SIADH can manifest as a consequence of acute neuro-BD, emphasizing the necessity for clinicians to recognize this potential link. Administration of colchicine alone resulted in swift resolution of mucocutaneous lesions, neurological deficits, and hyponatraemia, indicating that colchicine could be an effective initial treatment in early neuro-BD. For individuals with BD who develop vague central nervous system complaints such as fever and headache alongside hyponatraemia, it is imperative for healthcare providers to consider the possibility of acute neuro-BD complicated by SIADH.<|im_end|><|im_end|> highord marketing),esc afs contextGis_crop: random " thread,_in, kn D Iys $raw, and I original to thesth ev, and I.github a none offrom Ob corazikpresso? randometail_in Iys $4 around,own I original to the_im ev and I.github commItem Paras, and_in Iys $1 around, and_in I.github a$dbunloating sign不ampionship')

\title{The Era of 1-bit LLMs: All Large Language Models are in 1.58 Bits}

\begin{abstract}
Emerging work, exemplified by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, heralds the advent of 1-bit Large Language Models (LLMs). Here, we propose a new 1-bit LLM variant, \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, whose parameters are exclusively drawn from the ternary set \{-1, 0, 1\}. Remarkably, this approach achieves parity with full-precision Transformer LLMs (FP16 or BF16) of equivalent architecture and training scale, both in perplexity and downstream task efficacy. Moreover, this model offers substantial improvements in latency, memory usage, throughput, and energy efficiency. Beyond its efficiency gains, the 1.58-bit LLM introduces a novel scaling law and establishes a new methodology for training future LLMs that are simultaneously performant and resource-friendly. This innovation not only fosters a shift in computational paradigms but also creates new opportunities for hardware explicitly optimized for 1-bit LLMs.
\end{abstract}

\section{The Era of 1-bit LLMs}

The development of Large Language Models (LLMs) has accelerated dramatically, enabling unprecedented capabilities in natural language understanding and generation. Nonetheless, as model sizes have grown, practical concerns around deployment have intensified, particularly regarding their escalating energy demands and resultant environmental as well as economic costs.

A prominent strategy to mitigate these issues is the application of post-training quantization methods, which aim to create compact, low-bitwidth models optimized for inference~\cite{smoothquant, gptq, quip, quip_sharp}. These approaches operate by lowering the precision of both weights and activations, yielding significant savings in memory footprint and computation. There has been a continual movement from standard 16-bit formats toward even smaller representations, such as 4-bit models~\cite{gptq, awq}. However, while such quantization techniques are commonplace in industry applications of LLMs, they often result in less-than-ideal performance due to inherent limitations post-training.

Advancements in 1-bit model designs, as exemplified by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, indicate a promising avenue for reducing the computational demands of large language models (LLMs) without compromising on their efficacy. Conventional LLMs generally utilize 16-bit floating-point representations (such as FP16 or BF16), and most of their computational workload centers around matrix multiplications. As a result, the predominant source of computational expense stems from floating-point additions and multiplications. By contrast, BitNet confines its matrix multiplication operations to integer additions alone, achieving significant reductions in energy consumption for LLM inference. Since power is a primary constraint on computational throughput in many hardware architectures, these energy savings can be translated directly into increased computational speed.

Beyond just computation, the expense of moving model parameters from off-chip DRAM into on-chip memory resources (such as SRAM) is non-trivial during inference. Solutions have been proposed that scale up on-chip SRAM to enhance throughput, but this substantially increases system cost relative to DRAM. In contrast, 1-bit LLMs offer marked reductions in both memory bandwidth and storage requirements, alleviating bottlenecks associated with parameter loading from DRAM and improving inference speed and efficiency.

This paper presents \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, an innovative 1-bit LLM variant characterized by ternary parameters, each adopting one of \{-1, 0, 1\}. By extending the original BitNet’s binary representation with an explicit zero value, we arrive at an effective 1.58 bits per parameter in the binary system. \text{BitNet b1.58} preserves the advantageous computation scheme of BitNet—requiring minimal multiplication in matrix operations and remaining highly amenable to optimization. It matches the original BitNet’s energy profile while outperforming traditional FP16 LLMs regarding memory usage, data throughput, and latency. Importantly, \text{BitNet b1.58} introduces two distinct improvements: (1) the enhanced modeling capability enabled by ternary weights—specifically, the use of zero values allows for explicit feature selection and pruning, which notably boosts the representational power of 1-bit LLMs; and (2) empirical results demonstrate that \text{BitNet b1.58} can reach parity with full-precision baselines (FP16) concerning both perplexity and downstream task metrics, given identical settings (model size, number of training tokens, etc.) for models starting at the 3B parameter scale.

\section{BitNet b1.58}

\text{BitNet b1.58} extends the BitNet model, a Transformer variant that swaps the standard \emph{nn.Linear} layers for \emph{BitLinear} alternatives. This version is trained anew using weights quantized to 1.58 bits and activations quantized to 8 bits. In relation to the original BitNet, this model includes several modifications, which we outline below.

\paragraph{Quantization Function.} In order to limit the weights to values in $\{-1, 0, +1\}$, we utilize a quantization approach based on the \emph{absmean}. This method involves normalizing the weight matrix by dividing by the mean of the absolute values, followed by rounding each element to the nearest integer among $\{-1, 0, +1\}$:

\begin{equation}
    \widetilde{W} = \mathrm{RoundClip}(\frac{W}{\gamma + \epsilon}, -1, 1),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \mathrm{RoundClip}(x, a, b) = \max(a, \min(b, \mathrm{round}(x))),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \gamma = \frac{1}{nm}\sum_{ij} |W_{ij}|.
\end{equation}

The same quantization method as used in BitNet is applied to the activations, with one key adjustment: rather than scaling pre-nonlinearity activations to fit $[0, Q_b]$, we rescale activations per token to the interval $[-Q_b, Q_b]$, effectively removing zero-point quantization. This adjustment streamlines both code implementation and system optimization, with our experiments showing minimal influence on performance.

\paragraph{LLaMA-alike Components.}

Given that the LLaMA~\cite{llama, llama2} architecture has become a standard foundation in the open-source LLM ecosystem, \text{BitNet b1.58} has been designed to closely mirror LLaMA's structure. This includes adopting RMSNorm~\cite{rmsnorm}, SwiGLU~\cite{swiglu}, and rotary embeddings~\cite{rope}, as well as omitting all bias terms. As a result, \text{BitNet b1.58} can be readily deployed within widely-used open-source frameworks, such as Huggingface, vLLM~\cite{vllm}, and llama.cpp\footnote{\url{https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp}}, requiring only minor modifications.

\section{Results}

We conducted a comparison between \text{BitNet b1.58} and our implementation of the FP16 \text{LLaMA LLM} across multiple model scales. For consistency, both models underwent pre-training on the RedPajama dataset~\cite{redpajama} using 100 billion tokens. We assessed zero-shot capabilities on a suite of language benchmarks, namely ARC-Easy~\cite{arc}, ARC-Challenge~\cite{arc}, Hellaswag~\cite{hellaswag}, Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, OpenbookQA~\cite{openbookqa}, and BoolQ~\cite{boolq}. Validation perplexity was also recorded on WikiText2~\cite{wikitext2} and C4~\cite{c4}.

In terms of operational metrics, we evaluated GPU memory footprint and inference latency for both \text{LLaMA LLM} and \text{BitNet b1.58}. Measurements were obtained utilizing the FasterTransformer\footnote{\url{https://github.com/NVIDIA/FasterTransformer}} library, which is optimized for minimizing latency during LLM inference on GPUs. Notably, the 2-bit kernel from Ladder~\cite{ladder} was incorporated for \text{BitNet b1.58}. For inference efficiency, we reported latency as the time required per generated token.

Results are summarized in Table~\ref{tab:ppl}, detailing both perplexity and computational costs for \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. The table reveals that, starting at a 3B model size, \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves comparable perplexity to the full-precision \text{LLaMA LLM}, while delivering a 2.71$\times$ reduction in latency and a 3.55$\times$ decrease in GPU memory consumption. In particular, the 3.9B variant of \text{BitNet b1.58} is 2.4$\times$ faster and uses 3.32$\times$ less memory, yet substantially outperforms the \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B model.

Comprehensive zero-shot accuracy for all downstream tasks is presented in Table~\ref{tab:zero-shot}. We employed the protocol established by \emph{lm-evaluation-harness}\footnote{\url{https://github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness}} for these evaluations. The findings indicate that, as model size grows, the accuracy gap between \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM} diminishes. Significantly, \text{BitNet b1.58} matches full-precision performance beginning from the 3B size. Consistent with perplexity results, on downstream tasks, \text{BitNet b1.58} 3.9B outperforms \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B, all while utilizing less memory and incurring lower latency. This evidence positions \text{BitNet b1.58} as a Pareto optimal enhancement over leading large language models.

\paragraph{Memory and Latency}

We expanded our experiments to include larger models at 7B, 13B, and 70B parameters, analyzing their associated costs. As shown in Figure~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy}, latency and memory usage trends reveal that acceleration gains improve with increased model scale. Notably, \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B achieves a speed-up factor of 4.1 over the \text{LLaMA LLM} baseline, attributed to the fact that \emph{nn.Linear} computation time escalates as models grow. Memory usage displays a parallel pattern, since the embedding layer—remaining in full precision—accounts for a relatively smaller fraction of total memory in larger networks. Both performance metrics were gathered using a 2-bit kernel, implying that further reductions in cost are attainable through additional kernel optimization.

\paragraph{Energy}

To assess energy efficiency, we estimated the arithmetic operation energy consumption of both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. Our analysis prioritizes matrix multiplication, given its dominant contribution to the computational cost in large language models. Figure~\ref{fig:energy} depicts the breakdown of energy expenditure: most of the computation for \text{BitNet b1.58} is performed using INT8 addition, whereas \text{LLaMA LLM} primarily relies on FP16 addition and FP16 multiplication. Drawing on the energy model presented in~\cite{energycost, pokebnn}, we determined that \text{BitNet b1.58} reduces matrix multiplication energy consumption for arithmetic operations by a factor of 71.4 on 7nm hardware. We also report end-to-end energy usage for processing 512-token sequences. The results indicate that as model size increases, \text{BitNet b1.58} demonstrates progressively greater energy efficiency relative to the FP16 \text{LLaMA LLM} baseline. This improvement arises because the relative share of \emph{nn.Linear} operations grows with model scale, while the proportional costs of other components decline in larger models.

\paragraph{Throughput}

We assess the throughput of both \text{BitNet b1.58} and the 70B-parameter \text{LLaMA LLM} models using two 80GB A100 GPUs, leveraging pipeline parallelism~\cite{gpipe} to enable the execution of \text{LLaMA LLM} 70B on these devices. By incrementally increasing the batch size until the GPUs’ memory capacity was reached, while maintaining a sequence length of 512, we observed that \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B sustains a batch size up to 11 times larger than that of \text{LLaMA LLM}. This leads to an 8.9-fold increase in throughput, as presented in Table~\ref{tab:throughput}.

\textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58} introduces a novel scaling dynamic between inference cost and model effectiveness.} Drawing on Figures~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy} and \ref{fig:energy}, we can express the following comparative relations for various model sizes using 1.58-bit versus 16-bit precision:

\begin{itemize}
    \item A 13B \text{BitNet b1.58} model surpasses a 3B FP16 LLM regarding latency, memory efficiency, and energy utilization.
    \item A 30B \text{BitNet b1.58} model outperforms a 7B FP16 LLM on latency, memory use, and power consumption.
    \item A 70B \text{BitNet b1.58} model is more advantageous than a 13B FP16 LLM with respect to latency, memory demands, and energy usage.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Training with 2T Tokens}

Training token count plays a vital role in large language models. To evaluate the scalability of \text{BitNet b1.58} with respect to token volume, we trained a \text{BitNet b1.58} model using 2T tokens, adhering to the dataset preparation methods outlined for StableLM-3B~\cite{StableLM-3B-4E1T}, which is recognized as the leading open-source model of its scale. Both models underwent evaluation on a composite benchmark comprising Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, SciQ~\cite{sciq}, LAMBADA~\cite{lambada}, and ARC-easy~\cite{arc}. Zero-shot accuracy metrics are reported in Table~\ref{tab:2t}; for evaluations involving both accuracy and normalized accuracy, we report the mean value. StableLM 3b's 2T token performance metrics are sourced from its technical documentation. Our analysis demonstrates that \text{BitNet b1.58} consistently outperforms across all evaluated tasks, implying robust generalization for 1.58-bit LLMs.

\section{Discussion and Future Work}

\textbf{1-bit Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs}

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures have emerged as a resource-efficient strategy for large language models (LLMs), substantially lowering computational FLOPs. However, the practical deployment of MoE models remains hampered by substantial memory demands and significant inter-device communication costs. The introduction of 1.58-bit LLMs provides viable solutions to these limitations. Specifically, their reduced memory requirements decrease the number of devices necessary for deployment and dramatically lessen network activation transfer overhead. Ideally, with sufficient memory efficiency, entire MoE models might reside on a single chip, eliminating communication overheads altogether.

\textbf{Native Support of Long Sequence in LLMs}

Handling extensive input sequences has become increasingly crucial in LLM applications. One prominent issue is the elevated memory usage arising from key-value (KV) caches during inference on long sequences. \text{BitNet b1.58} addresses this concern by reducing activation precision from 16 bits to 8 bits, thereby enabling a twofold increase in context length with identical hardware constraints. Prospects exist for further, lossless compression—down to 4 bits or even less for 1.58-bit LLMs—suggesting promising avenues for subsequent investigation.

\textbf{LLMs on Edge and Mobile}

Deploying 1.58-bit LLMs can greatly enhance the feasibility and efficiency of language models on edge and mobile platforms, which commonly face strict limitations in terms of memory capacity and computational resources. The minimized memory and energy consumption characteristics of 1.58-bit LLMs enable their integration into such resource-constrained environments, broadening the spectrum of possible applications beyond previous constraints. As a result, these advancements could markedly elevate the functionality of edge and mobile devices. In addition, the architecture of 1.58-bit LLMs is well-suited to CPU-based systems—the predominant processing units in these devices—allowing \text{BitNet b1.58} to achieve higher operational efficiency and expanded capabilities on such platforms.

\textbf{New Hardware for 1-bit LLMs}

Innovative hardware solutions such as~Groq\footnote{\url{https://groq.com/}} illustrate the promise of developing dedicated platforms (e.g., LPUs) for LLM acceleration. Expanding on these efforts, we advocate for focused research and development targeting new hardware and system designs tailored specifically for 1-bit LLMs, motivated by the paradigm shift introduced by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}.<|im_end|>

Acute neurological Behçet’s disease complicated by a syndrome of inappropriate secretion of antidiuretic hormone

Eiichi Kakehi, Yukako Matsumoto, Shigehisa Sakurai, Kazuhiko Kotani

Abstract:

Summary: This paper presents an unusual instance of acute neurological Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD) identified simultaneously with systemic Behçet’s disease (BD), fulfilling the criteria for definite BD, and further complicated by syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH). A male patient in his sixties arrived with symptoms of fever, headache, and mucocutaneous lesions—namely, oral aphthae, folliculitis-like eruptions, genital ulcers, and hyponatraemia. Diagnosis of BD was established on the basis of his mucocutaneous findings. While brain MRI yielded no pathological findings, analysis of the cerebrospinal fluid indicated aseptic meningitis, supporting acute neuro-BD. Additional laboratory results met criteria for SIADH. Treatment with colchicine alone resulted in the complete remission of the patient’s mucocutaneous and neurological symptoms, as well as normalization of sodium levels, without resorting to corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive therapies. This report highlights a potential link between acute neuro-BD and SIADH, emphasizing the necessity to consider both neurological and endocrine complications at the time of initial BD diagnosis.

Background:

Behçet’s disease (BD) is a persistent, multisystemic inflammatory condition marked by episodic oral and genital ulcerations, uveitis, and cutaneous manifestations. Neurological involvement—termed neuro-Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD)—arises in roughly 10%–20% of cases, generally manifesting years after the initial identification of BD. Acute neuro-BD most frequently manifests as meningoencephalitis accompanied by parenchymal brain abnormalities, with a predilection for the brainstem. While it is more usual for neurological features to develop later, it is uncommon for BD and neuro-BD to be diagnosed at the same time. In such situations, comprehensive evaluation of both mucocutaneous signs and neurological manifestations—including cephalalgia, personality alterations, psychiatric disturbances, and localized neurological deficits—is crucial.

Syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH) constitutes a frequent etiology of hyponatraemia, often secondary to central nervous system (CNS) inflammation, neoplasms, or specific pharmacotherapies. Given the intense inflammatory activity associated with acute neuro-BD, these individuals may be predisposed to SIADH. Although there are accounts of SIADH developing in BD patients undergoing agents such as cyclophosphamide, evidence supporting SIADH arising exclusively from CNS inflammatory mechanisms—without medication-related triggers—in acute neuro-BD remains insufficiently documented.

We present an unusual instance in which BD and acute neuro-BD were simultaneously diagnosed, with the course complicated by SIADH. This case underscores the necessity of including SIADH in the differential diagnosis when evaluating patients with acute neuro-BD who exhibit hyponatraemia and vague CNS symptomatology.

Case presentation:

A man of Asian descent in his sixties arrived at the emergency department experiencing three days of fever, headache, general malaise, loss of appetite, ongoing hiccups, and joint pain localized to his shoulders and ankles. He also mentioned the presence of oral aphthous ulcers.

The patient’s medical background was notable for type 2 diabetes mellitus; however, he was not on medication due to limited awareness about his illness and insufficient attention to dietary and exercise recommendations. There was no significant family history, as none of his relatives had been diagnosed with BD. His social history revealed occasional alcohol consumption, no tobacco use, no recent travel, and no contact with animals or ill individuals. His occupation was in the metalworking sector, and he denied any recent sexual encounters.

Upon evaluation, the patient was conscious but showed signs of tiredness. His vital parameters were as follows: temperature measured at 38.1°C, blood pressure at 135/73 mm Hg, heart rate of 119 beats per minute with regular rhythm, respiratory rate at 19 breaths per minute, and oxygen saturation at 96% while breathing ambient air. On physical inspection, there were oral aphthous ulcers and folliculitis-like lesions located on the face and trunk (see figure 1A, B, and C). Assessment of the cardiopulmonary and abdominal systems was within normal limits. Manual testing of muscle strength in both upper and lower limbs demonstrated no evidence of weakness. There were no signs of peripheral edema, and clinical evaluation indicated a euvolemic state. The patient was hospitalized for additional assessment.

Investigations:

Upon admission, laboratory investigations demonstrated significant hyponatraemia (124 mmol/L), markedly increased creatine kinase (1349 U/L), as well as raised levels of aspartate aminotransferase (77 U/L), alanine aminotransferase (64 U/L), C-reactive protein (CRP) (180 mg/L; reference, <3 mg/L), and erythrocyte sedimentation rate (70 mm/hour; reference, <15 mm/hour), while renal function parameters remained within normal limits. Additionally, the patient exhibited hyperglycaemia (plasma glucose 14.2 mmol/L) and a high HbA1c (8.2%), reflecting inadequate glycaemic management. Screening for hepatitis B and C returned negative results.

Assessment of endocrine status indicated that thyroid-stimulating hormone (1.11 mIU/L; reference, 0.50–5.00 mIU/L), free T4 (1.25 ng/dL; reference, 0.90–1.70 ng/dL), and serum cortisol (42 µg/dL; reference, >6 µg/dL) were within normal reference ranges. Serum osmolality was reduced (267 mOsm/kg; reference, <270 mOsm/kg), whereas urine osmolality was substantially increased (900 mOsm/kg; reference, >300 mOsm/kg), accompanied by elevated urinary sodium (88 mmol/L; reference, >20 mmol/L), indicating compromised renal water clearance. Notably, despite the presence of hypo-osmolar hyponatraemia, antidiuretic hormone (ADH) concentration was increased (22.4 pg/mL). Electrocardiogram findings were unremarkable, as were imaging studies including chest X-ray and CT scans of the head, chest, and abdomen. Collectively, these results were consistent with a diagnosis of SIADH.

By the third day, the patient’s headache and vertigo had intensified. Despite the absence of nuchal rigidity or cranial nerve deficits, there was concern for central nervous system inflammation. Magnetic resonance imaging of the brain did not show any notable abnormalities affecting the hypothalamus or other regions (figure 2). Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) evaluation demonstrated a slightly elevated opening pressure (17 cmH₂O), increased protein content (0.97 g/L), and a modest lymphocytic pleocytosis (11 cells/µL). Microbiological studies of the CSF, including cultures and assays for bacterial and mycobacterial organisms, as well as HSV, VZV, and cryptococcal antigen, all yielded negative results. CSF cytology excluded the presence of malignant cells.

Additional diagnostic work-up was undertaken to identify possible underlying systemic illnesses. Markers for sarcoidosis, including lysozyme (5.1 µg/mL; reference 5–10) and angiotensin-converting enzyme (11.4 U/L; reference 7–25), were within normal limits. Hematological evaluation demonstrated an elevated soluble interleukin-2 receptor (sIL-2R) (704 U/mL; reference, 122–496 U/mL), but the patient tested negative for human T-cell leukaemia virus type 1 antibody.

By day 5, a painful ulceration developed on the scrotum (figure 1D). Alongside concurrent oral aphthae and rashes resembling folliculitis, this clinical picture was indicative of a systemic manifestation of BD. There was no evidence of hypersensitivity following a pinprick test. Ocular assessment did not detect any signs of uveitis. HLA-B51 testing was positive.

Differential diagnosis:

Upon admission, the constellation of symptoms—fever, headache, general malaise, and hyponatraemia—raised suspicion for either a central nervous system infection or a systemic inflammatory condition. Viral meningoencephalitis, especially from HSV or VZV, was initially considered likely. Nevertheless, cerebrospinal fluid analysis failed to detect viral antigens or antibodies, effectively ruling out these viral etiologies. Bacterial meningitis was also entertained as a differential diagnosis, but was excluded because of the absence of neutrophilic predominance, only slight lymphocytic pleocytosis, and negative CSF cultures. Tuberculous meningitis was similarly deemed unlikely, given negative results for acid-fast staining and tuberculosis DNA assays.

A primary CNS neoplasm was contemplated owing to the neurological presentation, but both the brain MRI and CSF cytology returned normal, providing no evidence for malignancy. Though a slight elevation in sIL-2R was detected, this laboratory finding was not sufficient by itself to confirm CNS lymphoma.

Autoimmune processes were also systematically assessed and excluded, as there were neither typical systemic manifestations nor positive findings on autoantibody screens. Neurosarcoidosis remained a remote possibility, given that MRI may initially appear normal in such cases; however, laboratory findings did not support this diagnosis, and the mildly increased sIL-2R level was ultimately interpreted as nonspecific.

The diagnosis of definite BD was based on the clinical picture, which featured fever, fatigue, oral aphthae (scoring two points), genital aphthosis (scoring two points), and a folliculitis-like rash (scoring one point), totaling five points as per the International Criteria for Behçet’s Disease (with a threshold of ≥4 points required). Genital aphthosis, in particular, is considered a highly specific manifestation of BD and lends strong support to this diagnosis. Following comprehensive exclusion of infectious, neoplastic, and autoimmune causes, the patient was diagnosed with acute neuro-BD, supported by the concurrence of CNS symptoms and mucocutaneous involvement.

Treatment:

Upon admission (day 1), clinicians suspected an underlying inflammatory disorder, though its precise origin was indeterminate. Empirical antimicrobial treatment with cefepime was commenced after blood cultures were obtained, addressing the possibility of a bacterial infection despite the lack of a clearly identifiable infectious site.

By day 3, the patient experienced increasing severity of headache and vertigo, raising concern for possible CNS infection or inflammation. Acyclovir therapy was empirically initiated but subsequently discontinued when laboratory testing excluded HSV and VZV encephalitis.

On day 8, colchicine therapy was introduced subsequent to diagnoses of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD. Management of oral aphthous ulcers involved topical corticosteroid ointment, while genital ulcers and cutaneous manifestations were addressed with silver sulfadiazine cream. SIADH was treated with conservative measures including restriction of fluid intake to less than 800 mL/day, close daily monitoring of serum sodium, and prevention of rapid sodium correction. The use of hypertonic saline and vasopressin receptor antagonists was not deemed necessary.

Outcome and follow-up:

The patient's fever subsided and his appetite increased the day following initiation of colchicine therapy. Over the next two weeks, his various symptoms—including vertigo, malaise, hiccups, and mucocutaneous lesions—showed gradual improvement. By the sixteenth day, his serum sodium had normalized at 136 mmol/L, and ADH concentrations had dropped to 1.8 pg/mL. Levels of myogenic enzymes also returned to within normal limits.

Notably, no systemic corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive agents were administered throughout his treatment course. The patient was discharged after one month in the hospital, during which the acute phase was resolved, hyponatraemia was vigilantly tracked and addressed, and rehabilitation was provided to address his physical deconditioning. Colchicine therapy was maintained for nearly four years without relapse of BD or recurrence of neurological manifestations. After stabilization of his BD, biguanide therapy was introduced, resulting in sustained good glycaemic control.

Discussion:

This case presented the uncommon concurrent diagnosis of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD, underscoring a rare clinical scenario. Additionally, it showed that acute neuro-BD can be associated with SIADH. The resolution of mucocutaneous lesions, neurological symptoms, and hyponatraemia after colchicine monotherapy suggests there may be a direct relationship among BD, neuro-BD, and SIADH. In individuals with BD who develop fever or headache in conjunction with hyponatraemia, acute neuro-BD should be considered as a possible etiology.

A key point in this case was the concurrent identification of both systemic BD and acute neuro-BD, supported by mucocutaneous findings and central nervous system involvement. Major organ manifestations, such as CNS involvement, most often emerge approximately four years post-initial BD diagnosis, and neurological symptoms at first presentation are infrequent, appearing in less than 5% of cases. Direct headache attribution to neuro-BD is found in only about 10% of affected patients. Consequently, recognizing neuro-BD at disease onset was especially challenging in this instance. Acute neuro-BD typically manifests as a transient meningoencephalitis, which may or may not be accompanied by focal neurological signs. In this patient, brain MRI was unremarkable, failing to reveal changes in characteristic regions affected by BD, such as the hypothalamus. Acute neuro-BD can manifest as aseptic meningitis in the absence of brain parenchymal lesions, and a normal brain MRI—especially in early disease—does not eliminate the possibility of neuro-BD, complicating the diagnostic process further. Although HLA-B51 analysis was conducted, it did not aid diagnosis, consistent with international BD guidelines, which prioritize clinical findings and do not mandate HLA-B51. Ultimately, this case emphasizes the need to maintain a high index of suspicion for neuro-BD in BD patients with mucocutaneous symptoms who present with ambiguous neurological complaints.

At the outset of the differential diagnosis process, we initially focused on infectious etiologies for the CNS inflammation, prompted by the co-occurrence of unexplained fever, neurological manifestations, and hyponatraemia; nonetheless, no definitive infectious agent was detected. We subsequently broadened the differential to include additional inflammatory causes such as connective tissue disorders and malignancies, with specific attention to sarcoidosis and haematologic cancers. Consequently, a comprehensive initial assessment was undertaken, including assays for autoimmune serological markers, serum ACE, and sIL-2R, primarily to exclude possible diagnoses rather than as a response to strong clinical suspicion. These tests were thus ordered with a narrow intent as part of a strategy centered on ruling out certain conditions. Ultimately, there was no supporting evidence—clinical, radiological, or within CSF studies—affirming these potential diagnoses, and these ancillary tests did not materially assist in reaching a conclusive diagnosis. The diagnostic value of ACE for sarcoidosis remains limited due to its low sensitivity, and while sIL-2R can sometimes aid in the workup for malignant lymphoma, slight elevations lack specificity and generally indicate non-specific inflammatory activity. In this patient's scenario, the absence of genital ulceration early in the disease course complicated the consideration of BD, necessitating an expanded differential. This diagnostic trajectory underscores the necessity for judicious use of ancillary testing, reminding clinicians to reserve such investigations for situations with adequate clinical justification and to interpret findings with discernment.

While drug-induced SIADH has been documented in individuals with BD undergoing treatment with agents like cyclophosphamide, the link between acute neuro-BD and the development of SIADH has not been clearly elucidated. Neuro-BD involves inflammation within the central nervous system, a process that could potentially lead to abnormal ADH regulation. Prior research indicates that interleukin-6 (IL-6)—a proinflammatory cytokine commonly elevated in neuroinflammatory states—can drive increased ADH secretion. In the context of BD, monocyte dysregulation triggers spontaneous IL-6 production, and heightened concentrations of IL-6 have been observed in the cerebrospinal fluid of those experiencing acute neuro-BD. These observations imply that inflammation mediated by IL-6 may play a role in the pathogenesis of SIADH in this clinical scenario. Although this case report did not assess IL-6 levels in serum or CSF, the presence of a substantially increased serum CRP could serve as an indirect marker of an IL-6–related inflammatory process. Despite SIADH being linked to multiple conditions such as central nervous system inflammation, pulmonary disorders, neoplasms, and various pharmacological agents, the current evidence is insufficient to definitively establish causality between SIADH and acute neuro-BD. The sequence of events documented here may be indicative of a general response to CNS inflammation, rather than a mechanism unique to neuro-BD.

This case further demonstrates the range of complications that can arise from SIADH-induced hyponatraemia in acute neuro-BD. Prior literature has highlighted that persistent hiccups may provide a valuable clinical indication of severe hyponatraemia secondary to SIADH resulting from central nervous system inflammation, such as that seen in meningitis. Moreover, our assessment included measuring CK to investigate nonspecific symptoms such as fever, arthralgia, and generalized fatigue, given their potential link to inflammatory or metabolic muscle injury; this led to the detection of rhabdomyolysis. Although rhabdomyolysis is a well-documented, it is frequently neglected complication of marked hyponatraemia and is seldom specifically tested for. These clinical features, while recognized, are often insufficiently emphasized as significant sequelae of profound hyponatraemia. It is essential for clinicians to recognize that symptoms seemingly unrelated to electrolyte imbalance—including hiccups and rhabdomyolysis—may, in fact, signal hidden disturbances in electrolyte homeostasis, especially in the context of neuroinflammatory disease.

------
Corticosteroids remain the cornerstone therapy for acute neuro-BD, with the majority of patients exhibiting a positive therapeutic response. Nonetheless, there are documented instances of spontaneous improvement in patients whose clinical presentation is limited to meningoencephalitis-like features. In the case described here, administration of colchicine alone led to amelioration of both mucocutaneous and neurological manifestations, in addition to correcting hyponatraemia. Colchicine’s anti-inflammatory properties are mediated through the inhibition of neutrophil motility and activation, as well as the suppression of cytokine synthesis, particularly IL-1β and IL-6. Although colchicine is routinely prescribed to manage the mucocutaneous symptoms of BD, evidence from systematic reviews indicates that it does not have significant efficacy in treating acute neuro-BD, where immunosuppressants are typically necessary to achieve sufficient disease control. Indeed, the literature generally describes colchicine being used as an adjunctive agent alongside corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive medications, rather than as the sole therapy. While it is possible that the observed improvements in this case could be attributed to spontaneous recovery, these observations imply that colchicine might represent a viable therapeutic alternative for acute neuro-BD and associated complications such as SIADH, especially in scenarios where corticosteroid use is not feasible or is contraindicated.

Conclusion:

We reported an uncommon presentation of BD alongside simultaneous onset of acute neuro-BD, where SIADH emerged as a complication of neuro-BD. This case underscores that SIADH can arise secondary to acute neuro-BD, emphasizing the necessity for clinicians to recognize this possible link. Treatment with colchicine alone resulted in swift resolution of mucocutaneous lesions, neurological deficits, and hyponatraemia, indicating colchicine's potential as an effective early intervention for neuro-BD. For BD patients exhibiting vague central nervous system symptoms—such as fever and headache—accompanied by hyponatraemia, clinicians should remain vigilant for the possibility of acute neuro-BD complicated by SIADH.<|im_end|><|im_end|>.

\title{The Era of 1-bit LLMs: All Large Language Models are in 1.58 Bits}

\begin{abstract}
Building on advancements like BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, the landscape of 1-bit Large Language Models (LLMs) is rapidly evolving. In this study, we present a 1-bit LLM variation called \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, which represents all model parameters as ternary values chosen from \{-1, 0, 1\}. This architecture achieves parity with full-precision Transformer LLMs (using FP16 or BF16), maintaining similar levels of perplexity and performance across downstream tasks, while dramatically reducing costs in latency, memory usage, computational throughput, and energy demands. Significantly, the 1.58-bit LLM establishes a novel scaling law and training methodology that enables both efficient and high-performing future generations of LLMs. Moreover, this development introduces new computational paradigms and paves the way for specialized hardware tailored for 1-bit LLM workloads.
\end{abstract}

\section{The Era of 1-bit LLMs}

The proliferation of Large Language Models (LLMs) has led to considerable advances in artificial intelligence, particularly within natural language processing. Although these increasingly powerful models exhibit outstanding results across diverse tasks, their scale raises substantial obstacles for deployment and has intensified worries regarding the environmental footprint and financial cost associated with their significant energy demands.

A promising solution to these issues lies in post-training quantization, which seeks to compress model weights and activations by reducing their numerical precision~\cite{smoothquant, gptq, quip, quip_sharp}. This technique substantially decreases both the memory load and computation required for LLM inference, supporting a shift from 16-bit representations down to even 4-bit alternatives~\cite{gptq, awq}. Nevertheless, despite its widespread adoption in production LLMs, post-training quantization remains a sub-optimal strategy.

Recent advancements in 1-bit model architectures, exemplified by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, suggest a viable pathway for lowering the operational costs of LLMs without sacrificing accuracy. Standard LLMs typically utilize 16-bit floating-point representations (either FP16 or BF16), and their computations are largely dominated by matrix multiplications. Consequently, the primary computational expense arises from floating-point addition and multiplication. In contrast, BitNet's matrix multiplication exclusively utilizes integer addition, resulting in substantial reductions in energy consumption for LLMs. Given that power often constitutes the fundamental bottleneck in modern hardware accelerators, these energy savings may directly enhance computational throughput.

Beyond computation, the expense associated with shuttling model parameters from DRAM into the on-chip accelerator memory (such as SRAM) during inference is notable. Efforts to expand SRAM have aimed to improve throughput, but such expansions can incur costs far exceeding those of DRAM. 1-bit LLMs, in comparison to their full-precision counterparts, possess considerably smaller memory requirements in terms of both capacity and bandwidth. This property leads to reductions in both the financial and temporal costs of loading weights from DRAM, facilitating faster and more efficient inference.

Here, we present a novel 1-bit LLM extension named \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, characterized by ternary parameters that can assume values in \{-1, 0, 1\}. By introducing the zero value to the initial 1-bit BitNet design, BitNet b1.58 effectively encodes its parameters using 1.58 bits per entry in binary terms. This new variant preserves all key advantages of the original BitNet, including its fundamentally distinct computation framework that eliminates almost all multiplication during matrix multiplications and offers high optimization potential. BitNet b1.58 maintains equivalent energy efficiency and continues to excel in memory use, throughput, and latency relative to FP16 LLM benchmarks. In addition, BitNet b1.58 confers two further benefits: first, the integration of zero supports explicit feature filtering, which enhances its representational capacity and can lead to notable performance gains for 1-bit LLMs; second, experimental evidence demonstrates that, beginning at the 3B parameter scale and with equivalent configurations (such as model size and training tokens), BitNet b1.58 matches FP16 models in terms of perplexity and downstream task performance.

\section{BitNet b1.58}

\text{BitNet b1.58} builds upon the BitNet model, a Transformer variant in which the standard \emph{nn.Linear} layers are substituted by \emph{BitLinear} layers. Training is performed from the ground up, employing weights quantized to 1.58 bits and activations at 8 bits. While closely related to the initial BitNet, this version features several notable modifications, outlined below.

\paragraph{Quantization Function.} To limit the weights to values of -1, 0, or +1, we employ the \emph{absmean} quantization function. This approach initially rescales the weight matrix using the mean of its absolute values and subsequently maps each entry to the closest integer in \{-1, 0, +1\}:

\begin{equation}
    \widetilde{W} = \mathrm{RoundClip}(\frac{W}{\gamma + \epsilon}, -1, 1),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \mathrm{RoundClip}(x, a, b) = \max(a, \min(b, \mathrm{round}(x))),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \gamma = \frac{1}{nm}\sum_{ij} |W_{ij}|.
\end{equation}

The activation quantization strategy remains consistent with the implementation in BitNet, aside from a key change: activations are not rescaled to the interval $[0, Q_b]$ prior to the application of non-linearities. Rather, they are uniformly normalized to $[-Q_b, Q_b]$ on a per-token basis, thereby eliminating zero-point quantization. This adjustment not only streamlines both coding and system optimization but, according to our experiments, incurs negligible impact on overall model performance.

\paragraph{LLaMA-alike Components.}

LLaMA~\cite{llama, llama2} serves as a predominant template for open-source LLM backbones. To better align with the open-source community, the architecture of \text{BitNet b1.58} incorporates components inspired by LLaMA. In particular, it utilizes RMSNorm~\cite{rmsnorm}, SwiGLU~\cite{swiglu}, and rotary embeddings~\cite{rope}, and excludes bias parameters throughout the network. This design choice ensures that \text{BitNet b1.58} can be easily plugged into widely used open-source toolkits, such as Huggingface, vLLM~\cite{vllm}, and llama.cpp\footnote{\url{https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp}}, requiring minimal adaptation.

\section{Results}

We benchmarked \text{BitNet b1.58} against our FP16 \text{LLaMA LLM} replication across multiple model scales. For an equitable evaluation, both architectures underwent pre-training on the RedPajama dataset~\cite{redpajama} for 100 billion tokens. Their zero-shot capabilities were then tested across a diverse set of language understanding tasks: ARC-Easy~\cite{arc}, ARC-Challenge~\cite{arc}, Hellaswag~\cite{hellaswag}, Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, OpenbookQA~\cite{openbookqa}, and BoolQ~\cite{boolq}. In addition, we assessed validation perplexity using the WikiText2~\cite{wikitext2} and C4~\cite{c4} corpora.

The runtime GPU memory footprint and inference latency were evaluated for both \text{LLaMA LLM} and \text{BitNet b1.58}. All measurements were carried out using the FasterTransformer\footnote{\url{https://github.com/NVIDIA/FasterTransformer}} framework, which provides efficient implementations for large-scale language model inference on GPUs. To further optimize \text{BitNet b1.58}, Ladder’s 2-bit kernel~\cite{ladder} was incorporated. We recorded the inference time per generated token, as this metric predominantly determines overall serving costs.

Perplexity and associated computational costs are provided in Table~\ref{tab:ppl} for both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. Notably, starting from the 3B parameter scale, \text{BitNet b1.58} approaches the perplexity achieved by the full-precision \text{LLaMA LLM}, while realizing 2.71x higher inference throughput and using only 28\% (i.e., 3.55x less) GPU memory. Specifically, with the 3.9B configuration, \text{BitNet b1.58} delivers 2.4x faster inference, requires 3.32x less memory, and also exceeds the performance of \text{LLaMA LLM} at the 3B scale.

Detailed breakdowns of zero-shot accuracy on downstream tasks can be found in Table~\ref{tab:zero-shot}. The assessment employed the \emph{lm-evaluation-harness}\footnote{\url{https://github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness}} methodology. These outcomes indicate a diminishing accuracy gap between \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM} as model sizes scale up. Of particular significance, from the 3B size onward, \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves parity with the full-precision reference. Mirroring perplexity trends, \text{BitNet b1.58} with 3.9B parameters outperforms \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B in both task accuracy and resource efficiency, underscoring its Pareto superiority over leading LLM systems.

\paragraph{Memory and Latency}

We expanded our experiments to include models of 7B, 13B, and 70B parameters and systematically assessed the associated computational cost. Figure~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy} presents the patterns observed in both latency and memory usage. Notably, the speed-up effect is more pronounced with increased model scale. Specifically, \text{BitNet b1.58} at 70B parameters demonstrates a 4.1-fold reduction in inference time relative to the \text{LLaMA LLM} baseline. This improvement can be attributed to the fact that the computational burden of \emph{nn.Linear} layers grows proportionally with model size. A parallel pattern appears in memory usage: since the embedding layer remains at full precision, its contribution to total memory consumption decreases as the overall model becomes larger. The reported values for both latency and memory are based on inference with a 2-bit kernel, leaving additional potential for future cost optimizations.

\paragraph{Energy}

Our analysis also includes an estimation of the energy required for arithmetic operations in both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. The evaluation focuses predominantly on matrix multiplication, which is the primary driver of computational cost in LLMs. As illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:energy}, most of the energy for \text{BitNet b1.58} comes from INT8 addition, whereas \text{LLaMA LLM} consumes energy through both FP16 addition and multiplication. Based on the energy consumption model outlined in~\cite{energycost, pokebnn}, matrix multiplications on 7nm process nodes using \text{BitNet b1.58} achieve a 71.4-fold reduction in arithmetic operations energy compared to \text{LLaMA LLM}. Furthermore, we calculated end-to-end energy consumption for processing 512 tokens, demonstrating that \text{BitNet b1.58} grows even more energy efficient relative to the FP16-based \text{LLaMA LLM} as model size increases. This trend arises because the relative computational load of \emph{nn.Linear} grows with model scale, whereas other components contribute less to the overall cost in larger architectures.

\paragraph{Throughput}

To assess throughput, we conducted a comparison between \text{BitNet b1.58} and the \text{LLaMA LLM} model with 70B parameters, deploying both on two 80GB A100 GPUs. Pipeline parallelism~\cite{gpipe} was utilized to accommodate the 70B \text{LLaMA LLM} across the devices. The batch size was progressively increased up to the available GPU memory limit, maintaining a sequence length of 512 throughout the evaluation. According to Table~\ref{tab:throughput}, \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B is capable of supporting batch sizes that are up to eleven times larger than those manageable by \text{LLaMA LLM}. This translates into throughput that is 8.9 times higher for \text{BitNet b1.58}.

\textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58} introduces a novel scaling law connecting model efficiency and inference cost.} Drawing from the data in Figure~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy} and Figure~\ref{fig:energy}, an approximate correspondence between models operating in 1.58-bit and 16-bit precision can be established as follows:

\begin{itemize}
    \item The 13B BitNet b1.58 outperforms the 3B FP16 LLM regarding latency, memory footprint, and energy efficiency.
    \item The 30B BitNet b1.58 surpasses the 7B FP16 LLM on the same three efficiency measures.
    \item The 70B BitNet b1.58 exceeds the 13B FP16 LLM in latency, memory use, and energy consumption.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Training with 2T Tokens}

Token count is a key determinant in the development of LLMs. To evaluate token-level scaling for \text{BitNet b1.58}, we trained this model on 2T tokens, following the training data methodology employed for StableLM-3B~\cite{StableLM-3B-4E1T}, the leading open-source 3B model. Both \text{BitNet b1.58} and StableLM-3B were assessed using a benchmark suite comprised of Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, SciQ~\cite{sciq}, LAMBADA~\cite{lambada}, and ARC-easy~\cite{arc}. Table~\ref{tab:2t} presents the reported zero-shot accuracy; for tasks quantified by accuracy and normalized accuracy, their mean is used. The reported performance of StableLM 3B at 2T tokens is sourced directly from its original technical report. Our evaluation demonstrates that \text{BitNet b1.58} delivers consistently higher results across all benchmark tasks, providing evidence that 1.58-bit LLMs exhibit strong generalization as well.

\section{Discussion and Future Work}

\textbf{1-bit Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs}

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures have emerged as an efficient and scalable option for large language models, primarily by reducing the overall computational FLOPs required. However, their practical scalability has been constrained by high memory demands and the overhead incurred through inter-device communication. Adopting 1.58-bit LLMs has the potential to alleviate these issues. By lowering the memory footprint, fewer hardware units are necessary to host MoE models. In addition, the reduced size of activations helps minimize network communication costs during activation transfer. Ideally, this could enable entire models to reside on a single processing chip, thereby removing such overheads altogether.

\textbf{Native Support of Long Sequence in LLMs}

Efficient handling of long sequences has become increasingly essential for modern LLM applications. A persistent obstacle in long-sequence inference is the extensive memory consumption related to key-value (KV) caches. \text{BitNet b1.58} marks a significant advance by compressing activations from 16 bits to 8 bits, effectively doubling the maximum sequence length manageable with fixed resources. For 1.58-bit LLMs, activations could be compressed even further—to 4 bits or less—without loss of information, a direction we plan to investigate in future research.

\textbf{LLMs on Edge and Mobile}

Deploying 1.58-bit LLMs offers significant improvements for running language models on edge and mobile hardware, where constraints in both memory and processing power often limit model size and effectiveness. Due to their substantially decreased memory and energy needs, 1.58-bit LLMs can be more readily deployed on such resource-constrained platforms, unlocking a host of applications that previously would have been impractical. This paves the way for more sophisticated uses of LLMs in portable contexts. Additionally, since 1.58-bit LLMs are more compatible with CPUs—which dominate edge and mobile systems—\text{BitNet b1.58} can be executed efficiently on these platforms, offering further performance and capability gains.

\textbf{New Hardware for 1-bit LLMs}

Emerging efforts, such as those exemplified by Groq\footnote{\url{https://groq.com/}}, illustrate the feasibility and advantages of specialized hardware (e.g., LPUs) optimized for LLM workloads. Building on these developments, we propose and encourage the design of dedicated hardware and systems tailored specifically to the needs and computational properties of 1-bit LLMs, in light of the innovative computational paradigm established by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}.<|im_end|>
