Acute neurological Behçet’s disease complicated by a syndrome of inappropriate secretion of antidiuretic hormone

Eiichi Kakehi, Yukako Matsumoto, Shigehisa Sakurai, Kazuhiko Kotani

Abstract:

Summary: This report details an uncommon presentation of acute neurological Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD) occurring simultaneously with systemic Behçet’s disease (BD)—classified as definite BD—complicated further by the onset of syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH). A male patient in his 60s arrived with symptoms of fever and headache, alongside mucocutaneous signs such as oral aphthae, folliculitis-like eruptions, genital ulcers, and evidence of hyponatraemia. Diagnosis of BD was established on the basis of mucocutaneous findings. Although brain MRI did not reveal abnormalities, cerebrospinal fluid analysis identified aseptic meningitis, providing support for a diagnosis of acute neuro-BD. Additional laboratory testing satisfied the diagnostic criteria for SIADH. Treatment with colchicine alone was effective in resolving the patient’s cutaneous, neurological, and electrolyte abnormalities, obviating the need for corticosteroids or further immunosuppressive therapies. This case highlights a potential link between acute neuro-BD and SIADH, and emphasizes the clinical significance of identifying both neurological and endocrine involvement at the initial presentation of BD.

Background:

Behçet’s disease (BD) is a persistent, systemic inflammatory condition that is marked by episodic oral and genital ulcerations, uveitis, and dermatological manifestations. Neurological manifestations, collectively termed neuro-Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD), affect around 10%–20% of individuals with BD, often emerging years after the disease is initially recognized. The acute phase of neuro-BD frequently manifests as meningoencephalitis with parenchymal lesions, particularly impacting the brainstem. While it is uncommon for BD and neuro-BD to be identified simultaneously, such cases do occur. When confronted with concurrent diagnoses, healthcare providers must thoroughly evaluate both the mucocutaneous presentation and neurological findings, which can include headache, behavioral disturbances, psychiatric manifestations, and localized neurological impairments.

The syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH) represents a frequent etiology of hyponatraemia, commonly arising in the setting of central nervous system (CNS) inflammation, malignancy, or as an adverse effect of specific pharmacotherapies. In light of the proinflammatory milieu associated with acute neuro-BD, patients with this condition could potentially develop SIADH. Although there are documented cases of SIADH in BD patients undergoing cyclophosphamide therapy, the incidence of SIADH directly attributable to CNS inflammation from acute neuro-BD, in the absence of medication triggers, is not well established.

Here, we describe an unusual case involving simultaneous diagnoses of BD and acute neuro-BD, further complicated by the development of SIADH. This case underscores the necessity of considering SIADH in the differential diagnosis of acute neuro-BD, especially when evaluating patients with ambiguous CNS complaints and hyponatraemia.

Case presentation:

A man of Asian descent in his sixth decade arrived at the emergency department, reporting three days of fever, headache, general fatigue, diminished appetite, continuous hiccups, and joint pain in the shoulders and ankles. He further mentioned experiencing oral aphthous ulcers.

His past medical history was significant for type 2 diabetes mellitus; however, he was not on any therapeutic regimen, largely due to insufficient understanding of his diagnosis and a lack of adherence to dietary and exercise recommendations. There was no notable family history of BD. The patient drank alcohol occasionally, did not smoke, had neither recently traveled nor had contact with animals or sick individuals. Employed in the metalworking sector, he reported no recent sexual encounters.

Upon evaluation, the patient was conscious but showed signs of tiredness. Recorded vital parameters included a body temperature of 38.1°C, blood pressure measuring 135/73 mm Hg, regular heart rate at 119 beats per minute, respiratory rate at 19 breaths per minute, and oxygen saturation of 96% while breathing ambient air. Physical assessment identified oral aphthous ulcers and folliculitis-like eruptions on the facial and truncal regions (figure 1A, B and C). Cardiopulmonary and abdominal assessments yielded normal results. Manual muscle strength testing demonstrated intact strength in both upper and lower limbs. There was an absence of peripheral edema, and his clinical volume assessment indicated euvolemia. Consequently, he was hospitalized for further diagnostic workup.

Investigations:

Initial laboratory investigations showed significant hyponatraemia (124 mmol/L), alongside increased levels of creatine kinase (1349 U/L), aspartate aminotransferase (77 U/L), alanine aminotransferase (64 U/L), C-reactive protein (CRP) (180 mg/L; reference range, <3 mg/L), and erythrocyte sedimentation rate (70 mm/hour; reference range, <15 mm/hour), while renal function remained within normal limits. The patient exhibited hyperglycaemia (plasma glucose 14.2 mmol/L) and a raised HbA1c of 8.2%, suggestive of suboptimal glycaemic control. Serological tests for hepatitis B and C were negative.

Endocrine assessment demonstrated normal values for thyroid-stimulating hormone (1.11 mIU/L; reference, 0.50–5.00 mIU/L), free T4 (1.25 ng/dL; reference, 0.90–1.70 ng/dL), and serum cortisol (42 µg/dL; reference, >6 µg/dL). Serum osmolality was reduced (267 mOsm/kg; reference, <270 mOsm/kg), while urine osmolality was markedly elevated (900 mOsm/kg; reference, >300 mOsm/kg), accompanied by a high urinary sodium level (88 mmol/L; reference, >20 mmol/L), indicating a disturbance in water excretion. Despite the presence of hypo-osmolar hyponatraemia, antidiuretic hormone (ADH) was found to be elevated (22.4 pg/mL). Electrocardiogram results were normal, and imaging studies, including chest X-ray and computed tomography of the head, chest, and abdomen, were unremarkable. Collectively, these findings were consistent with a diagnosis of SIADH.

By the third day, the patient’s headache and vertigo had intensified. Despite the absence of neck rigidity or evidence of cranial nerve dysfunction, central nervous system (CNS) inflammation was considered as a possible diagnosis. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) of the brain did not demonstrate any noteworthy abnormalities in the hypothalamus or other brain areas (figure 2). Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) studies showed a mildly increased opening pressure (17 cmH₂O), elevated protein concentration (0.97 g/L), and a slight lymphocytic pleocytosis (11 cells/µL). Microbiological tests on the CSF—including cultures and specific assays for bacterial and mycobacterial organisms, herpes simplex virus (HSV), varicella zoster virus (VZV), and cryptococcal antigen—were negative. CSF cytological analysis detected no evidence of malignant cells.

Additional diagnostic workup was undertaken to rule out underlying systemic pathologies. Evaluation for sarcoidosis demonstrated normal values for lysozyme (5.1 µg/mL; reference 5–10) and angiotensin-converting enzyme (11.4 U/L; reference 7–25). Screening for hematologic malignancy showed an increased level of soluble interleukin-2 receptor (sIL-2R) (704 U/mL; reference 122–496 U/mL), whereas testing for human T-cell leukemia virus type 1 antibody was negative.

On the fifth day, the patient developed a painful ulcer on the scrotum (figure 1D). Taken together with the presence of oral aphthae and folliculitis-like skin lesions, these manifestations raised the suspicion of systemic BD. There was no evidence of pathergy on skin testing. Examination by ophthalmology excluded the presence of uveitis. Testing for HLA-B51 was positive.

Differential diagnosis:

Upon initial assessment, the concurrence of fever, headache, general malaise, and hyponatraemia indicated a potential central nervous system (CNS) infection or a systemic inflammatory condition. Viral meningoencephalitis, especially due to HSV or VZV, was suspected first; nevertheless, cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) analysis was negative for viral antigens and antibodies, thereby ruling out these viral etiologies. Consideration was also given to bacterial meningitis, but this diagnosis was discounted due to the absence of neutrophilic predominance, the presence of only mild lymphocytic pleocytosis, and negative CSF cultures. Tuberculous meningitis was similarly excluded based on negative acid-fast staining and tuberculosis DNA assays.

Given the neurologic symptoms, a primary CNS malignancy was also contemplated. However, both brain MRI and CSF cytology did not reveal any pathological abnormalities or malignant cells. Although the serum sIL-2R was slightly increased, this isolated result was not sufficient to diagnose CNS lymphoma.

The possibility of autoimmune disorders was explored; these were dismissed in light of the lack of hallmark systemic features and negative results on screening autoantibody tests. Neurosarcoidosis remained in the differential, considering that MRI may appear normal early on, yet subsequent laboratory findings argued against this diagnosis. The marginally elevated sIL-2R was interpreted as non-specific.

A definitive diagnosis of BD was made according to clinical manifestations, including fever and fatigue, oral aphthae (two points), genital aphthosis (two points), and a rash resembling folliculitis (one point). Utilizing the International Criteria for Behçet’s Disease, the patient accumulated a total of five points (≥4 signifying a confirmed diagnosis). Notably, genital aphthosis is recognized as a highly specific feature of BD, further substantiating this diagnosis. Following systematic exclusion of infectious, neoplastic, and autoimmune etiologies, acute neuro-BD was diagnosed on the basis of CNS symptoms in association with characteristic mucocutaneous findings.

Treatment:

Upon admission (day 1), clinicians suspected an underlying inflammatory disorder, although its precise cause was not immediately identified. Empiric cefepime therapy was commenced following the collection of blood cultures, aimed at addressing a possible bacterial infection even though no clear infectious site was evident.

By day 3, the patient's headache and vertigo had intensified, raising concerns for central nervous system inflammation or infection. As a precaution, acyclovir was introduced, but subsequently discontinued when diagnostic evaluations excluded HSV and VZV encephalitis.

On day 8, following the confirmation of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD, colchicine treatment was initiated. Management of oral aphthous ulcers involved the application of topical corticosteroid ointment, while genital and skin lesions were treated with silver sulfadiazine cream. The syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH) was approached with conservative measures, including fluid intake restriction (<800 mL/day), daily serum sodium assessments, and careful avoidance of rapid sodium correction. There was no necessity for hypertonic saline administration or the use of vasopressin receptor antagonists.

Outcome and follow-up:

The patient experienced resolution of fever and improvement in appetite the day following the initiation of colchicine therapy. Over the next 2 weeks, symptoms such as vertigo, general malaise, hiccups, and mucocutaneous lesions showed progressive amelioration. By the 16th day, his serum sodium concentration had returned to a normal value of 136 mmol/L, and ADH levels had declined to 1.8 pg/mL. Myogenic enzyme concentrations also normalized.

Notably, systemic corticosteroids and additional immunosuppressive agents were not administered throughout the course of treatment. The patient was discharged after a hospitalization period of 1 month, during which the acute phase was resolved, hyponatraemia was closely observed and addressed, and rehabilitation addressed declines in physical capability. Colchicine treatment was sustained for about 4 years, with no relapses of BD or neurological symptoms recorded during that period. Once BD was stabilized, biguanide therapy commenced, resulting in maintained satisfactory glycaemic control.

Discussion:

In this instance, systemic BD and acute neuro-BD were identified concurrently, a particularly uncommon clinical occurrence. The case further illustrated that acute neuro-BD may be accompanied by SIADH. The alleviation of mucocutaneous symptoms, neurological deficits, and hyponatraemia following colchicine monotherapy indicates a likely direct relationship among BD, neuro-BD, and SIADH. Thus, clinicians should entertain the possibility of acute neuro-BD in BD patients who exhibit fever or headache in association with hyponatraemia.

This report is significant for documenting the concurrent onset of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD, evidenced by both mucocutaneous involvement and central nervous system manifestations. Conventionally, serious organ involvement such as CNS pathology emerges roughly four years after the initial BD diagnosis, with neurological symptoms at initial presentation observed in less than 5% of cases. Additionally, only about 10% of individuals experience headaches attributable directly to neuro-BD, making early identification especially difficult in this scenario. Acute neuro-BD most frequently presents as a self-limited meningoencephalitic process, occasionally accompanied by focal neurological impairments. In this patient, MRI did not disclose pathognomonic changes in standard BD-associated regions, such as the hypothalamus. Acute neuro-BD may manifest as aseptic meningitis without visible parenchymal abnormalities, and unremarkable brain imaging—especially early in the clinical course—cannot reliably exclude this diagnosis. The absence of definitive radiological features added further diagnostic complexity. Testing for HLA-B51 was conducted but did not assist in confirmation, reflecting the international diagnostic standards for BD, which prioritize clinical presentation over genetic testing. Overall, this case underscores the need to consider neuro-BD in individuals with mucocutaneous disease who develop vague neurological symptoms.

During the early stages of forming the differential diagnosis, we gave primary consideration to infection as the source of CNS inflammation, prompted by the concurrence of unexplained fever, neurological manifestations, and hyponatraemia. Nonetheless, our investigations did not detect any particular infectious agent. Alternative etiologies for CNS inflammation were also explored, such as connective tissue disorders and neoplastic conditions, including sarcoidosis and haematological malignancies. Consequently, we conducted an extensive initial screening by measuring autoimmune serological markers, serum ACE, and sIL-2R. These laboratory assessments were implemented selectively, as part of an exclusionary process rather than out of high clinical suspicion. Ultimately, neither clinical evaluation, imaging, nor CSF analysis yielded findings to corroborate these conditions, and these ancillary investigations offered little toward establishing the diagnosis. Given the limited sensitivity of ACE for sarcoidosis, and considering that while sIL-2R can be helpful in suspected malignant lymphoma, its mild elevation is not specific and may simply indicate general inflammatory activity. Notably, the lack of genital ulcers in the early presentation complicated the identification of BD, necessitating a comprehensive differential. This experience underscores the necessity for judicious use of diagnostic testing and reiterates that investigations should be reserved for cases with appropriate clinical indications, with results interpreted prudently.

While instances of drug-induced SIADH have been described in individuals with BD who are treated with agents such as cyclophosphamide, the connection between acute neuro-BD and SIADH remains inadequately elucidated. Neuro-BD involves inflammation within the CNS, a process that could potentially lead to abnormal regulation of ADH secretion. Previous research has demonstrated that interleukin-6 (IL-6), a proinflammatory cytokine commonly elevated in neuroinflammatory conditions, is linked to increased ADH secretion. In the context of BD, dysregulated monocyte activity results in spontaneous production of IL-6, and heightened IL-6 concentrations have been observed in the CSF of those experiencing acute neuro-BD episodes. Collectively, these observations imply that IL-6–driven inflammation may play a role in the development of SIADH in this context. Although IL-6 was not quantified in either serum or CSF for the current case, the significantly increased serum CRP may serve as an indirect indicator of an IL-6–dependent inflammatory process. Despite SIADH being known to arise in association with multiple conditions—including CNS inflammation, pulmonary disease, neoplasia, and use of certain drugs—the evidence from this case does not establish a direct causative association between SIADH and acute neuro-BD. The temporal relationship observed here might represent a general inflammatory reaction within the CNS rather than a mechanism specific to neuro-BD.

This case further highlights the risks associated with hyponatraemia due to SIADH in patients with acute neuro-BD. Earlier literature has indicated that persistent hiccups can act as a significant clinical indicator of severe hyponatraemia arising from SIADH secondary to central nervous system inflammation, such as in cases of meningitis. Additionally, during our initial assessment for fever, arthralgia, and generalized fatigue—which could signify either inflammatory or metabolic muscle injury—we evaluated CK levels. This assessment revealed rhabdomyolysis, a recognized yet frequently neglected complication of severe hyponatraemia that is not commonly screened for. Although these clinical features are documented consequences of marked hyponatraemia, they often go underrecognized in practice. It is essential for clinicians to recognize that symptoms appearing to be unrelated, like hiccups or rhabdomyolysis, might indicate significant electrolyte imbalances, especially amidst neuroinflammatory disorders.
------
===== NEW BATCH 8 | input_ids shape=(2, 4096) | attention_mask shape=(2, 4096) =====
------
Corticosteroids typically represent the first-line therapy for acute neuro-BD, yielding favorable responses in most cases. Nevertheless, spontaneous improvement has occasionally been documented, especially in patients exhibiting only meningoencephalitis-like features. In the case discussed here, treatment with colchicine alone resulted in amelioration of both mucocutaneous and neurological manifestations, as well as correction of hyponatraemia. Colchicine’s anti-inflammatory properties derive from its ability to block neutrophil chemotaxis and activation, alongside the inhibition of cytokine release such as IL-1β and IL-6. While colchicine is commonly administered for mucocutaneous symptoms in BD, comprehensive reviews have indicated that it does not effectively manage acute neuro-BD; immunosuppressive medications are typically required for satisfactory disease regulation. Reported cases show colchicine has predominantly been employed as an adjunct to corticosteroids or additional immunosuppressants, rather than as a stand-alone therapy. Although spontaneous remission in this case cannot be definitively ruled out, the observations here raise the possibility that colchicine may serve as a viable option for treating acute neuro-BD and associated conditions like SIADH, especially in situations where corticosteroids are inappropriate or contraindicated.

Conclusion:

We presented an uncommon instance where BD and acute neuro-BD were diagnosed simultaneously, with SIADH arising as a complication of neuro-BD. This report underscores that SIADH can manifest secondary to acute neuro-BD, emphasizing the necessity for clinicians to recognize this possible link. Administration of colchicine as a single agent resulted in swift resolution of both mucocutaneous and neurological symptoms, as well as correction of hyponatraemia, indicating colchicine's potential utility as an initial treatment option for neuro-BD. For BD patients exhibiting general CNS complaints—such as headache and fever—accompanied by hyponatraemia, it is crucial for healthcare professionals to consider acute neuro-BD, particularly when SIADH is also present.<|im_end|><|im_end|> in CR ofoldgray_pointer Colorado('./. \title{The Era of 1-bit LLMs: All Large Language Models are in 1.58 Bits}

\begin{abstract}
Emerging work, notably BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, is ushering in a paradigm shift toward 1-bit Large Language Models (LLMs). This paper presents a 1-bit LLM variant named \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, in which each individual model parameter takes on a ternary value from the set \{-1, 0, 1\}. This architecture achieves performance parity—both in terms of perplexity and downstream task effectiveness—with full-precision Transformer LLMs (utilizing FP16 or BF16), given the same parameter count and training data, while greatly reducing resource costs in latency, memory usage, throughput, and energy expenditure. More fundamentally, the 1.58-bit LLM establishes a novel scaling principle and methodology for training advanced LLMs that unite high efficiency with superior performance. Additionally, it paves the way for a new computational model and motivates the development of bespoke hardware architectures tuned for 1-bit LLMs.
\end{abstract}

\section{The Era of 1-bit LLMs}

The rapid expansion of Large Language Models (LLMs) in recent years has greatly advanced the capabilities and versatility of artificial intelligence in natural language processing. Despite these achievements, the trend toward larger models introduces deployment obstacles and amplifies concerns about financial and environmental sustainability due to escalating energy requirements.

A viable solution has been the adoption of post-training quantization, which allows LLMs to operate with fewer bits during inference~\cite{smoothquant, gptq, quip, quip_sharp}. By lowering the bitwidth of model weights and activations, this approach leads to substantial savings in both memory and computation. While quantization strategies have evolved from 16 bits to as little as 4 bits~\cite{gptq, awq}, post-training quantization remains fundamentally limited, even if it has seen widespread deployment in industrial LLM systems.

Recent advances in 1-bit model architectures, exemplified by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, highlight a compelling path toward reducing the computational demands of LLMs without sacrificing efficacy. Standard LLMs employ 16-bit floating-point representations (FP16 or BF16), with matrix multiplication constituting the primary computational bottleneck. This results in significant costs due to intensive floating-point addition and multiplication operations. In contrast, BitNet's matrix multiplications rely exclusively on integer addition, yielding dramatic reductions in energy consumption for LLM workloads. Since many modern hardware platforms are constrained by power limitations, these energy efficiencies often correspond to improvements in processing speed.

Beyond computation, the movement of model parameters from DRAM to on-chip accelerator memory (such as SRAM) is another costly aspect of inference. While increasing SRAM size has been explored to enhance throughput, this solution is often prohibitively expensive relative to DRAM. In contrast to full-precision models, 1-bit LLMs present a much smaller memory footprint with respect to both storage capacity and bandwidth requirements. This lower demand results in shorter and less resource-intensive DRAM-to-SRAM transfers, which accelerates and streamlines inference tasks.

Here, we present a notable 1-bit LLM variant named \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, in which each parameter takes on one of three values from the set \{-1, 0, 1\}. The inclusion of the zero value augments the original BitNet design, raising its precision to 1.58 bits in the binary system. \text{BitNet b1.58} preserves all the key advantages of its 1-bit predecessor: it employs a computation strategy that eliminates nearly all multiplication operations during matrix multiplication and can be extensively optimized. Energy consumption remains comparable to the original BitNet, while it also outperforms FP16 LLM baselines in memory efficiency, throughput, and latency. Moreover, \text{BitNet b1.58} introduces two further benefits. First, its expressiveness is enhanced by explicit feature filtering, enabled through the allowance of zero-valued parameters—this boosts the representational power of 1-bit LLMs. Second, empirical results demonstrate that \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves parity with FP16 models in both perplexity and end-task results, beginning at the 3B parameter scale, when matched for architectural and training configurations (including model size and the amount of training data).

\section{BitNet b1.58}

\text{BitNet b1.58} is derived from the BitNet architecture, a Transformer variant in which traditional \emph{nn.Linear} layers are substituted with \emph{BitLinear}. This model is trained from the beginning using 1.58-bit precision for weights and 8-bit quantization for activations. In contrast to the initial BitNet design, it implements several updates, which are outlined below.

\paragraph{Quantization Function.} To limit weight values to -1, 0, or +1, we employ an \emph{absmean} quantization approach. This method involves normalizing the weight matrix using the mean absolute value of its elements and then rounding each element to its closest value among \{-1, 0, +1\}:

\begin{equation}
    \widetilde{W} = \mathrm{RoundClip}(\frac{W}{\gamma + \epsilon}, -1, 1),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \mathrm{RoundClip}(x, a, b) = \max(a, \min(b, \mathrm{round}(x))),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \gamma = \frac{1}{nm}\sum_{ij} |W_{ij}|.
\end{equation}

For activation quantization, we use the same technique as BitNet; however, unlike the original approach, we do not rescale activations into $[0, Q_b]$ prior to applying non-linear transformations. Instead, activations are rescaled for each token to $[-Q_b, Q_b]$, thereby eliminating the necessity for zero-point quantization. This alternative is simpler and more straightforward for practical implementation and system-level tuning, with only minimal impact on model accuracy observed in our evaluations.

\paragraph{LLaMA-alike Components.}

LLaMA~\cite{llama, llama2} has emerged as a standard architectural reference for open-source large language models. To align with the broader open-source ecosystem, \text{BitNet b1.58} incorporates LLaMA-style design choices, including RMSNorm~\cite{rmsnorm}, SwiGLU~\cite{swiglu}, rotary embedding~\cite{rope}, and the removal of all bias terms. Through this design, \text{BitNet b1.58} is readily compatible with commonly used open-source frameworks (such as Huggingface, vLLM~\cite{vllm}, and llama.cpp\footnote{\url{https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp}}) with minimal integration work.

\section{Results}

Our study involved benchmarking \text{BitNet b1.58} against our own FP16 implementation of the \text{LLaMA LLM} at multiple model scales. For a consistent evaluation, both models underwent pre-training on the RedPajama dataset~\cite{redpajama}, with exposure to 100 billion tokens. We assessed zero-shot generalization on an assortment of language understanding tasks: ARC-Easy~\cite{arc}, ARC-Challenge~\cite{arc}, Hellaswag~\cite{hellaswag}, Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, OpenbookQA~\cite{openbookqa}, and BoolQ~\cite{boolq}. Validation perplexities were calculated on both WikiText2~\cite{wikitext2} and C4~\cite{c4} datasets.

In addition, we systematically compared the GPU memory usage and inference latency between the \text{LLaMA LLM} and \text{BitNet b1.58}. Measurements were performed using the FasterTransformer\footnote{\url{https://github.com/NVIDIA/FasterTransformer}} framework, which is designed to optimize large language model inference on GPU hardware. For \text{BitNet b1.58}, we also incorporated the 2-bit kernel provided by Ladder~\cite{ladder}. Our primary metric for inference efficiency was the time required to generate each output token.

The perplexity and computational cost for both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM} are presented in Table~\ref{tab:ppl}. The data indicate that at a 3B parameter count, \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves perplexity comparable to that of its full-precision \text{LLaMA LLM} counterpart, all while being 2.71$\times$ faster and requiring only 28\% ($\approx$1/3.55) of the GPU memory. Notably, with a model size of 3.9B parameters, \text{BitNet b1.58} outpaces the 3B \text{LLaMA LLM} by a factor of 2.4 in speed, has 3.32$\times$ lower memory consumption, and delivers significantly enhanced performance.

Detailed zero-shot evaluation results are included in Table~\ref{tab:zero-shot}. The experimental setup adhered to the procedure established by \emph{lm-evaluation-harness}\footnote{\url{https://github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness}}. Analysis of the findings reveals a shrinking disparity in performance between \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM} as model scale increases. Critically, from the 3B parameter configuration onwards, \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves parity with the full-precision baseline. Echoing the perplexity trends, \text{BitNet b1.58} at 3.9B not only surpasses \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B in end-task accuracy, but does so with reduced latency and memory requirements, establishing \text{BitNet b1.58} as a Pareto superior alternative to leading LLM architectures.

\paragraph{Memory and Latency}

We extended our analysis to larger model scales, specifically 7B, 13B, and 70B parameters, and assessed the associated computational costs. As presented in Figure~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy}, both latency and memory requirements exhibit clear scaling patterns; notably, the performance improvement grows with model size. For instance, \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B demonstrates a 4.1-fold speed-up in comparison to the \text{LLaMA LLM} reference model. This acceleration is primarily attributed to the increased computational burden of the \emph{nn.Linear} operation in larger models. Similarly, memory utilization follows an analogous scaling behavior, since the embeddings retain full-precision representation and their share of total memory usage becomes less significant as models increase in size. Both performance metrics—latency and memory—were measured using a 2-bit kernel, suggesting further optimizations remain possible to lower computational cost.

\paragraph{Energy}

We additionally approximated the energy consumption arising from arithmetic operations for both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. The evaluation centers on matrix multiplication, as it constitutes the primary contributor to total computation cost in LLMs. Figure~\ref{fig:energy} breaks down the energy composition, revealing that the majority of computations in \text{BitNet b1.58} employ INT8 addition, while \text{LLaMA LLM} largely relies on both FP16 addition and multiplication. Drawing on the energy consumption model provided in~\cite{energycost, pokebnn}, it was observed that \text{BitNet b1.58} reduces arithmetic energy usage for matrix multiplication by a factor of 71.4 on 7nm process technology. We also report holistic energy usage for end-to-end inference with 512 token inputs. The findings indicate that the energy efficiency advantage of \text{BitNet b1.58} increases with model scale relative to the FP16 \text{LLaMA LLM} baseline. This trend can be ascribed to the rising proportion of computation attributable to \emph{nn.Linear} layers in larger models, while the influence of other components diminishes.

\paragraph{Throughput}

We assessed the throughput of \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM} with 70B parameters, running both models on a pair of 80GB A100 GPUs. To accommodate the size of \text{LLaMA LLM} 70B, we employed pipeline parallelism~\cite{gpipe}. For both models, batch size was scaled up incrementally until the maximum GPU memory was reached, with a fixed sequence length of 512. According to Table~\ref{tab:throughput}, \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B is able to handle a batch size up to 11 times larger than that of \text{LLaMA LLM}, yielding an 8.9-fold increase in throughput.

\textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58} enables a novel scaling law that connects model performance with inference efficiency.} Drawing on the data presented in Figures~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy} and~\ref{fig:energy}, we can establish approximate equivalencies between models of different sizes when utilizing 1.58-bit and 16-bit precision:

\begin{itemize}
    \item BitNet b1.58 with 13B parameters demonstrates superior latency, memory consumption, and energy efficiency relative to a 3B FP16 LLM.
    \item BitNet b1.58 at 30B parameters surpasses a 7B FP16 LLM across latency, memory, and energy metrics.
    \item BitNet b1.58 70B outperforms a 13B FP16 LLM with respect to latency, memory footprint, and energy use.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Training with 2T Tokens}

Token count plays a significant role in the effectiveness of LLMs. To evaluate how well \text{BitNet b1.58} scales with token volume, we trained it on 2T tokens, adhering to the data curation methodology of StableLM-3B~\cite{StableLM-3B-4E1T}, which currently represents the leading open-source 3B model. We compared both models using a benchmark set comprising Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, SciQ~\cite{sciq}, LAMBADA~\cite{lambada}, and ARC-easy~\cite{arc}. Zero-shot performance was calculated and summarized in Table~\ref{tab:2t}. For metrics reported as both accuracy and normalized accuracy, their mean was used. StableLM 3B’s 2T token results are sourced from its technical documentation. The findings demonstrate that \text{BitNet b1.58} consistently achieves the highest performance across all tasks, providing evidence that 1.58-bit LLMs possess strong generalization ability.

\section{Discussion and Future Work}

\textbf{1-bit Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs}

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) approaches have demonstrated their efficiency as a scalable solution for deploying LLMs by reducing computational FLOPs. However, their large memory requirements and the associated inter-chip communication overhead still pose significant obstacles to widespread use. Employing 1.58-bit LLMs can mitigate these issues: with a smaller memory demand, fewer devices are needed to host MoE architectures. Additionally, the bandwidth required for inter-device activation transfer is substantially lowered. If it becomes feasible to accommodate the whole MoE model on a single chip, this communication cost could be eliminated entirely.

\textbf{Native Support of Long Sequence in LLMs}

As LLMs evolve, supporting longer input sequences has become increasingly important. A key bottleneck is the substantial memory required to store KV caches during inference. \text{BitNet b1.58} offers promising progress by halving activation storage from 16 bits to 8 bits, thereby permitting twice the context length within the same hardware budget. Prospective advancements may allow further, lossless reductions of activation sizes to 4 bits or even less, specifically for 1.58-bit LLMs—an avenue reserved for future exploration.

\textbf{LLMs on Edge and Mobile}

1.58-bit LLMs promise substantial benefits for deploying language models on edge and mobile platforms, which often face stringent constraints in memory and computation. The efficiency gains from the reduced bitwidth enable broader deployment scenarios previously unattainable for LLMs, unlocking new functionalities and use cases for such devices. Furthermore, as these models are inherently more efficient in terms of both memory and power, they align well with CPU-dominant edge and mobile systems. Thus, \text{BitNet b1.58} can be executed efficiently on resource-constrained hardware, further extending the reach of LLM technology.

\textbf{New Hardware for 1-bit LLMs}

Recent innovations exemplified by Groq\footnote{\url{https://groq.com/}} highlight the opportunities in developing purpose-built hardware, such as LPUs, tailored to the specific needs of LLMs. Looking ahead, there is significant potential—and an open call—to engineer new computing architectures and systems designed explicitly for the requirements of 1-bit LLMs, leveraging the computation model introduced with BitNet~\cite{bitnet}.<|im_end|>

Acute neurological Behçet’s disease complicated by a syndrome of inappropriate secretion of antidiuretic hormone

Eiichi Kakehi, Yukako Matsumoto, Shigehisa Sakurai, Kazuhiko Kotani

Abstract:

Summary: We describe an uncommon presentation of acute neurological Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD) identified simultaneously with systemic Behçet’s disease (BD)—meeting criteria for definite BD—and complicated by syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH). A male patient in his 60s arrived with symptoms of fever, headache, and various mucocutaneous lesions, such as oral aphthae, rashes resembling folliculitis, genital ulcers, and laboratory-confirmed hyponatraemia. The mucocutaneous findings prompted a BD diagnosis. Although brain MRI was unremarkable, analysis of cerebrospinal fluid indicated aseptic meningitis, further substantiating acute neuro-BD. Laboratory evaluation satisfied the criteria for SIADH. Treatment with colchicine alone successfully resolved his mucocutaneous and neurological symptoms as well as the hyponatraemia, eliminating the necessity for corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive therapies. This report highlights a potential link between acute neuro-BD and SIADH and emphasizes the need to consider both neurological and endocrine involvement when assessing patients newly diagnosed with BD.

Background:

Behçet’s disease (BD) is a persistent inflammatory condition affecting multiple organ systems and is typified by the presence of recurrent oral and genital ulcerations, uveitis, and various dermatological manifestations. Neurological manifestations, termed neuro-Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD), emerge in roughly 10%–20% of individuals with BD, frequently arising years after the initial disease onset. The acute phase of neuro-BD is characteristically associated with meningoencephalitis and parenchymal lesions within the brain, most notably implicating the brainstem region. While it is more common for neurological symptoms to manifest later in the disease course, it is uncommon for both BD and neuro-BD to be diagnosed at the same time. When this simultaneous presentation occurs, it is important for clinicians to systematically evaluate for both cutaneous-mucosal findings and neurological indicators such as headache, behavioural or psychiatric changes, and focal neurological impairments.

The syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH) is a frequent etiology of hyponatraemia and is most often attributed to CNS inflammatory processes, neoplasms, or specific pharmacological agents. The inflammatory milieu characterizing acute neuro-BD may predispose patients to the development of SIADH. While SIADH has occasionally been described in BD patients undergoing treatment with agents like cyclophosphamide, the incidence of SIADH strictly due to acute neuro-BD–related CNS inflammation, absent any medication-induced factors, is yet to be clearly established.

Here, we describe an uncommon clinical scenario involving the concurrent diagnosis of BD and acute neuro-BD, which was further complicated by SIADH. This report underscores the need to consider SIADH as a possible complication in cases of acute neuro-BD, particularly when patients present with hyponatraemia in conjunction with vague or ambiguous neurological symptoms.

Case presentation:

A man of Asian descent in his sixties arrived at the emergency department, reporting three days of fever, headache, general malaise, anorexia, ongoing hiccups, and joint pain localized to the shoulders and ankles. Additionally, he noted the presence of oral aphthous ulcers.

The patient's medical background included type 2 diabetes mellitus, though he was not on any therapy for this condition, largely due to limited understanding of his diagnosis and a lack of adherence to recommended lifestyle modifications in diet or exercise. His family medical history revealed no known cases of BD. He consumed alcohol infrequently, was a non-smoker, and denied any recent travel, contact with animals, or exposure to sick individuals. Employment history revealed work in the metalworking sector, and he reported no recent sexual activity.

During assessment, the patient was conscious though exhibited signs of tiredness. His vital measurements included a temperature of 38.1°C, blood pressure at 135/73 mm Hg, regular pulse of 119 beats per minute, respiratory rate of 19 breaths per minute, and an oxygen saturation level of 96% while breathing ambient air. Upon physical inspection, oral aphthae were noted as well as rashes resembling folliculitis present on his face and trunk (figure 1A, B and C). No abnormalities were identified on examination of the cardiopulmonary or abdominal systems. Manual muscle testing showed preserved strength in both upper and lower limbs. There was an absence of peripheral oedema, and clinical evaluation indicated he was euvolemic. Hospital admission was undertaken to enable further diagnostic workup.

Investigations:

The initial laboratory assessment demonstrated significant hyponatraemia, with serum sodium measured at 124 mmol/L. There were increased levels of creatine kinase (1349 U/L), aspartate aminotransferase (77 U/L), alanine aminotransferase (64 U/L), C-reactive protein (180 mg/L; reference value, <3 mg/L), and erythrocyte sedimentation rate (70 mm/hour; reference, <15 mm/hour), while renal function remained within normal limits. The patient was additionally found to have hyperglycaemia (plasma glucose 14.2 mmol/L) and an HbA1c of 8.2%, pointing to inadequate control of blood glucose. Markers for hepatitis B and C returned negative.

Further endocrine analysis showed thyroid-stimulating hormone within the normal range (1.11 mIU/L; reference, 0.50–5.00 mIU/L), normal free T4 (1.25 ng/dL; reference, 0.90–1.70 ng/dL), and a serum cortisol of 42 µg/dL (reference, >6 µg/dL). Serum osmolality was reduced (267 mOsm/kg; reference, <270 mOsm/kg), while urine osmolality was markedly raised (900 mOsm/kg, reference, >300 mOsm/kg), accompanied by a high urinary sodium concentration (88 mmol/L; reference, >20 mmol/L), reflecting impaired ability to excrete water. In the context of hypo-osmolar hyponatraemia, antidiuretic hormone (ADH) levels were also elevated (22.4 pg/mL). Results from ECG, chest X-ray, and CT scans of the head, chest, and abdomen were all within normal parameters. Collectively, these data were consistent with a diagnosis of SIADH.

By the third day, his headache and vertigo had intensified. Despite the absence of neck rigidity or cranial nerve deficits, central nervous system (CNS) inflammation was considered as a possible cause. MRI of the brain did not demonstrate notable abnormalities affecting the hypothalamus or other regions (figure 2). Analysis of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) indicated a slightly elevated opening pressure (17 cmH₂O), increased protein concentration (0.97 g/L), and a mild lymphocytic pleocytosis (11 cells/µL). Cultures and diagnostic tests on CSF for bacterial and mycobacterial organisms, herpes simplex virus (HSV), varicella zoster virus (VZV), and cryptococcal antigen were all negative. No malignant cells were identified in CSF cytology.

Additional testing was undertaken to assess for underlying systemic illnesses. Investigations for sarcoidosis showed normal lysozyme (5.1 µg/mL; reference 5–10) and angiotensin-converting enzyme levels (11.4 U/L; reference 7–25). A hematological malignancy screen showed an elevated soluble interleukin-2 receptor (sIL-2R) level (704 U/mL; reference, 122–496 U/mL), but human T-cell leukaemia virus type 1 antibody was negative.

By day 5, a painful ulcer developed on the scrotum (figure 1D). Together with the presence of oral aphthae and folliculitis-like eruptions, these features pointed towards a systemic manifestation of BD. The patient did not display pinprick hypersensitivity, and ophthalmological evaluation detected no evidence of uveitis. Testing revealed the presence of HLA-B51.

Differential diagnosis:

Upon initial assessment, the concurrence of fever, headache, general malaise, and hyponatraemia raised suspicion for a central nervous system infection or a systemic inflammatory disorder. Viral meningoencephalitis, particularly due to HSV or VZV, was considered the most probable etiology at first. Nonetheless, analysis of the cerebrospinal fluid showed neither viral antigens nor antibodies, which effectively discounted these infections. The possibility of bacterial meningitis was evaluated but dismissed given the lack of neutrophilic predominance, only a slight lymphocytic pleocytosis, and negative CSF cultures. Tuberculous meningitis was similarly ruled out due to negative findings on acid-fast staining and PCR for tuberculosis DNA.

Given the neurological presentation, a primary CNS neoplasm was also considered. However, brain MRI revealed no pathologic abnormalities, and cytological analysis of the CSF did not identify any malignant cells. Although a slight elevation in sIL-2R was noted, this isolated finding was not sufficient to support CNS lymphoma as a diagnosis.

Assessment for autoimmune diseases was undertaken, but these were excluded based on a lack of hallmark systemic features and negative autoantibody screening. Neurosarcoidosis was retained as a potential diagnosis since early disease may present with unremarkable MRI, but later laboratory data made this unlikely. The moderate increase in sIL-2R was interpreted as nonspecific.

Definite BD was diagnosed based on the clinical picture, which included fever and fatigue as well as mucocutaneous findings: oral aphthae (two points), genital aphthosis (two points), and a rash resembling folliculitis (one point). This patient’s cumulative score using the International Criteria for Behçet’s Disease was five (where a score of four or higher is diagnostic). Of note, genital aphthosis is considered highly specific for BD, and these features robustly support the diagnosis. Following thorough exclusion of infectious, neoplastic, and autoimmune etiologies, a diagnosis of acute neuro-BD was confirmed, based on CNS manifestations associated with characteristic mucocutaneous symptoms.

Treatment:

Upon admission (day 1), clinicians considered an underlying inflammatory disorder, though its precise cause was indeterminate. Empirical cefepime therapy was commenced after drawing blood cultures to address a possible bacterial infection, even though no clear infectious source was identified.

By day 3, worsening headache and vertigo indicated possible inflammation or infection of the CNS. Acyclovir was initiated empirically but subsequently discontinued when diagnostic testing excluded both HSV and VZV encephalitis.

On day 8, treatment with colchicine was initiated after systemic BD and acute neuro-BD were diagnosed. Topical corticosteroid ointment was prescribed for oral aphthous ulcers. Silver sulfadiazine cream was used for the management of genital ulcers and cutaneous lesions. Management of SIADH consisted of conservative measures, including fluid restriction (<800 mL/day), rigorous daily monitoring of serum sodium, and ensuring that correction of sodium levels was gradual. Use of hypertonic saline and vasopressin receptor antagonists was not warranted.

Outcome and follow-up:

The patient’s fever subsided and his appetite increased within a day of commencing colchicine treatment. Over the next two weeks, his clinical symptoms—including vertigo, general malaise, persistent hiccups, and mucocutaneous manifestations—showed progressive resolution. By day 16, laboratory findings indicated serum sodium had returned to a normal value of 136 mmol/L, while ADH levels had decreased to 1.8 pg/mL. In addition, levels of myogenic enzymes normalized.

Notably, the management of his condition did not involve systemic corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive agents. Following a one-month period of hospitalization—which facilitated recovery from the acute phase, allowed for meticulous monitoring and correction of hyponatraemia, and included rehabilitation to address physical deconditioning—the patient was discharged. Colchicine was continued for around four years post-discharge, during which there were no observed recurrences of Behçet’s disease or neurologic manifestations. After stabilization of BD, biguanide therapy was introduced, resulting in sustained and effective glycaemic control.

Discussion:

This report presents a rare instance where systemic BD and acute neuro-BD were concurrently diagnosed. It further illustrates that SIADH can develop as a complication of acute neuro-BD. Notably, resolution of mucocutaneous symptoms, neurological deficits, and hyponatraemia was achieved through colchicine monotherapy, suggesting a potential direct link among BD, neuro-BD, and SIADH. For BD patients who exhibit fever or headache alongside hyponatraemia, acute neuro-BD should be considered as an underlying etiology.

The uniqueness of this case lies in the concurrent identification of both systemic BD and acute neuro-BD, as evidenced by the coexistence of mucocutaneous lesions and CNS involvement. Usually, severe organ involvement such as CNS pathology tends to occur approximately four years after BD is initially diagnosed, and presenting with neurological features at onset is infrequent—affecting less than 5% of cases. Only about 10% of individuals with BD develop headaches that are specifically attributable to neuro-BD, making initial detection challenging in this scenario. Acute neuro-BD is most often characterized by a self-limited meningoencephalitis, which may or may not be associated with focal neurological signs. In this case, MRI of the brain failed to reveal abnormalities in classic BD-affected regions, such as the hypothalamus. It is possible for acute neuro-BD to manifest as aseptic meningitis in the absence of parenchymal lesions, and a normal brain MRI early in the course of illness does not preclude the diagnosis. The absence of characteristic radiological findings introduced further complexity into the diagnostic evaluation. Although the patient underwent HLA-B51 genotyping, the result was not diagnostic, consistent with international BD diagnostic criteria, which prioritize clinical manifestations over genetic testing. This case underscores the importance of suspecting neuro-BD in patients with mucocutaneous involvement who present with non-specific neurological complaints.

At the outset of the differential diagnosis, our principal concern was infection as the etiology of CNS inflammation, due to the concurrent presence of unexplained fever, neurological manifestations, and hyponatraemia. Despite these findings, no causative pathogen could be identified. Our diagnostic considerations also encompassed alternative sources of CNS inflammation such as connective tissue disorders and malignancy; thus, sarcoidosis and haematologic malignancies were incorporated into our list of possible diagnoses. In line with this broad initial approach, we opted to screen for autoimmune disease markers, and evaluated serum ACE and sIL-2R levels. The decision to perform these assays was driven more by a process of elimination than by targeted clinical suspicion. Ultimately, neither the clinical presentation, imaging studies, nor CSF analysis offered support for these diagnoses, and the results of these investigations did not play a substantial role in establishing the final diagnosis. Notably, the diagnostic yield of ACE for sarcoidosis is modest, and while sIL-2R can aid in detecting malignant lymphoma, mild increases in sIL-2R are non-specific and may merely indicate an underlying inflammatory process. In this instance, the lack of genital ulcers in the initial phase complicated the suspicion for BD, necessitating consideration of a wide range of possible diagnoses. The process underscored the necessity for judicious use of diagnostic testing, underscoring that investigations should be directed by clinical indications and their interpretation approached prudently.

While SIADH resulting from pharmacological agents, such as cyclophosphamide, has been documented in individuals with BD, the relationship between acute neuro-BD and SIADH is not well elucidated. Neuro-BD features inflammatory activity within the CNS, potentially leading to disruptions in ADH regulation. Previous research has demonstrated that interleukin-6 (IL-6), a proinflammatory cytokine commonly upregulated in neuroinflammatory conditions, is implicated in promoting ADH secretion. In BD, excessive activation of monocytes can trigger spontaneous IL-6 production, with raised IL-6 concentrations identified in the CSF of those experiencing acute neuro-BD. These observations imply that IL-6–driven inflammation could play a role in SIADH under these circumstances. Although IL-6 measurements in the serum or CSF were not obtained in the current case, a significantly elevated serum CRP was observed, potentially indicating an IL-6–dependent inflammatory process. While SIADH can arise in diverse clinical contexts, including CNS inflammation, respiratory illness, malignancy, or medication use, the current evidence does not establish a definitive causal connection between SIADH and acute neuro-BD. The chronology seen in this case may simply indicate a generalized reaction to CNS inflammation, rather than a process unique to the underlying disease.

This case further demonstrates the possible complications arising from hyponatraemia due to SIADH in the context of acute neuro-BD. Prior literature has identified persistent hiccups as a significant clinical indicator of severe hyponatraemia related to SIADH resulting from CNS inflammation, including conditions such as meningitis. Furthermore, as part of our initial workup for fever, arthralgia, and generalized fatigue—symptoms potentially attributable to either inflammatory or metabolic muscle injury—we measured CK, which led to the identification of rhabdomyolysis. Although rhabdomyolysis is a recognized complication of profound hyponatraemia, it is frequently underdiagnosed because it is not routinely assessed. While these clinical presentations are documented in the literature, their significance in the context of profound hyponatraemia is often underestimated. It is important for clinicians to recognize that seemingly unrelated symptoms, including hiccups and rhabdomyolysis, may serve as indicators of underlying electrolyte imbalances, especially amid neuroinflammatory disease processes.
------
===== NEW BATCH 10 | input_ids shape=(2, 4096) | attention_mask shape=(2, 4096) =====
------
Corticosteroids are widely recognized as the primary therapy for acute neuro-BD, with the majority of patients exhibiting a favorable clinical response. Nonetheless, there have been instances where cases manifesting only with meningoencephalitis-like symptoms resolved spontaneously. In the case described here, administration of colchicine alone led to improvement in both mucocutaneous and neurological symptoms, as well as correction of hyponatraemia. The anti-inflammatory action of colchicine is attributed to its ability to impede neutrophil migration and activation, along with its capacity to downregulate cytokine production, notably IL-1β and IL-6. While colchicine is frequently prescribed for mucocutaneous involvement in BD, evidence from systematic reviews indicates that it does not effectively treat acute neuro-BD; rather, immunosuppressive agents are usually needed to achieve optimal disease management. Indeed, documented cases have typically combined colchicine with corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive medications, instead of employing colchicine as a standalone treatment. Although spontaneous remission cannot be completely ruled out in this instance, the observations here indicate that colchicine could represent a valuable therapeutic alternative for acute neuro-BD and associated complications, such as SIADH, especially in situations where corticosteroid treatment is either unsuitable or contraindicated.

Conclusion:

We reported an unusual instance where BD and acute neuro-BD were identified at the same time, with neuro-BD being further complicated by the presence of SIADH. This report underscores that SIADH can manifest as a complication of acute neuro-BD and emphasizes the necessity for clinicians to recognize this possible link. Treatment with colchicine alone resulted in swift resolution of both mucocutaneous and neurological symptoms, as well as hyponatraemia, indicating that colchicine might serve as an effective treatment during the initial stage of neuro-BD. When encountering BD patients who exhibit vague central nervous system complaints such as fever and headache together with hyponatraemia, practitioners should strongly consider acute neuro-BD complicated by SIADH in their differential diagnosis.<|im_end|><|im_end|> California.du wr****, of	break, is object dramwardessshowute is next toistrationoud tit California and].
