\title{The Era of 1-bit LLMs: All Large Language Models are in 1.58 Bits}

\begin{abstract}
Recent advances, exemplified by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, are ushering in a new generation of 1-bit Large Language Models (LLMs). In this paper, we propose a novel 1-bit LLM variant, designated as \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, where each parameter (or weight) assumes ternary values from \{-1, 0, 1\}. This model is able to attain parity with its full-precision counterparts (i.e., FP16 or BF16 Transformer LLMs) of equivalent architecture and token count, achieving similar perplexity and downstream task results. Importantly, this 1.58-bit configuration yields dramatic reductions in latency, memory footprint, computational throughput, and energy demands, thereby delivering substantial improvements in efficiency. On a deeper level, the 1.58-bit LLM offers a fresh scaling law and a training strategy that enables future LLM generations to reach both high performance and heightened cost-effectiveness. Additionally, this innovation facilitates novel computational frameworks and catalyzes the development of specialized hardware tailored for 1-bit LLM workloads.
\end{abstract}

\section{The Era of 1-bit LLMs}

Artificial Intelligence research has recently witnessed an extraordinary expansion in both the scale and proficiency of Large Language Models (LLMs). These systems exhibit state-of-the-art results across diverse natural language processing benchmarks; however, their escalating model sizes have introduced obstacles for widespread deployment, especially considering the environmental and financial burdens imposed by their substantial energy usage.

A promising avenue to mitigate these challenges is post-training quantization, wherein models are transformed to operate at lower numerical precision after training~\cite{smoothquant, gptq, quip, quip_sharp}. By quantizing weights and activations, memory and computation requirements are significantly lowered, enabling LLMs to be deployed more efficiently. The field has advanced from 16-bit implementations towards reduced precision variants, including 4-bit models~\cite{gptq, awq}. Nevertheless, post-training quantization, though prevalent in production-level LLMs, is fundamentally limited in optimality.

Recent advancements in 1-bit model architectures, notably BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, highlight a compelling approach for lowering the operational expenses of LLMs without compromising their accuracy. Traditional LLMs employ 16-bit floating-point representations (such as FP16 or BF16), with matrix multiplications constituting the core computational workload. Consequently, floating-point additions and multiplications dominate the overall computational cost. In contrast, BitNet’s matrix multiplication relies solely on integer additions, dramatically reducing the energy consumption typically required by LLMs. Since the primary bottleneck for computational speed on many hardware platforms is power consumption, these energy reductions also enable acceleration of computation.

Beyond raw computation, the act of moving model weights from DRAM into an on-chip accelerator’s local memory (such as SRAM) during inference can incur significant overheads. Some strategies have attempted to address this by increasing SRAM capacity to boost throughput, but this approach is substantially more expensive compared to scaling DRAM. In comparison to their full-precision counterparts, 1-bit LLMs are markedly more efficient regarding memory usage in both storage capacity and bandwidth requirements. This improvement leads to reduced latency and lower costs when transferring model weights from DRAM, thereby facilitating quicker and more effective inference.

In this paper, we propose an advanced 1-bit LLM variant named \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, in which each parameter adopts one of three possible values: \{-1, 0, 1\}. By augmenting the original 1-bit BitNet representation with an explicit 0 value, the new design requires just 1.58 bits per parameter in the binary system. \text{BitNet b1.58} preserves all the core advantages of its predecessor, including its computational framework that nearly eliminates multiplications during matrix operations and can be extensively optimized. It maintains comparable energy efficiency and offers considerable improvements in memory requirements, throughput, and inference latency over FP16-based LLMs. Additionally, \text{BitNet b1.58} introduces two further benefits. The first is a heightened representational power—attributed to the explicit inclusion of zero in the parameter space, which allows for direct feature filtering and substantively enhances 1-bit LLM effectiveness. The second advantage, confirmed by our empirical results, is that \text{BitNet b1.58} is able to achieve parity with full-precision (FP16) models in both perplexity and downstream task metrics, provided the models share equivalent settings (such as size and the amount of training tokens) at the 3B parameter scale and beyond.

\section{BitNet b1.58}

\text{BitNet b1.58} builds upon the BitNet framework, a variant of Transformer networks that substitutes \emph{nn.Linear} layers with \emph{BitLinear} layers. This model is trained from the beginning, utilizing 1.58-bit weights in conjunction with 8-bit activations. While it remains grounded in the foundational BitNet design, it incorporates several notable updates, detailed as follows.

\paragraph{Quantization Function.} To ensure the weights are limited to the values -1, 0, or +1, we employ an \emph{absmean} quantization method. This technique first normalizes the weight matrix by dividing by its mean absolute value and subsequently rounds each element to the closest value in the set \{-1, 0, +1\}:

\begin{equation}
    \widetilde{W} = \mathrm{RoundClip}(\frac{W}{\gamma + \epsilon}, -1, 1),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \mathrm{RoundClip}(x, a, b) = \max(a, \min(b, \mathrm{round}(x))),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \gamma = \frac{1}{nm}\sum_{ij} |W_{ij}|.
\end{equation}

For activations, the quantization follows the standard approach in BitNet, with one key distinction: we omit scaling activations to the interval $[0, Q_b]$ before non-linear transformations. Instead, all activations are rescaled per token to fit the range $[-Q_b, Q_b]$, eliminating zero-point quantization. This modification not only streamlines the implementation and benefits system-level efficiency but also has minimal impact on empirical performance.

\paragraph{LLaMA-alike Components.}

LLaMA~\cite{llama, llama2} has established itself as the reference backbone in the open-source LLM landscape. In line with this trend, the design of \text{BitNet b1.58} incorporates components inspired by LLaMA. It features RMSNorm~\cite{rmsnorm}, SwiGLU~\cite{swiglu}, and rotary embeddings~\cite{rope}, and omits all bias terms. These decisions facilitate straightforward adoption of \text{BitNet b1.58} into widely used open-source frameworks such as Huggingface, vLLM~\cite{vllm}, and llama.cpp\footnote{\url{https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp}}.

\section{Results}

We conducted a comparison between \text{BitNet b1.58} and our reimplemented FP16 version of \text{LLaMA LLM} across multiple model scales. To ensure an equitable evaluation, both models underwent pre-training on the RedPajama dataset~\cite{redpajama}, each processing 100 billion tokens. We assessed zero-shot capabilities over several language benchmarks: ARC-Easy~\cite{arc}, ARC-Challenge~\cite{arc}, Hellaswag~\cite{hellaswag}, Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, OpenbookQA~\cite{openbookqa}, and BoolQ~\cite{boolq}. Additionally, validation perplexity was measured on the WikiText2~\cite{wikitext2} and C4~\cite{c4} corpora.

Further, we analyzed GPU memory usage and inference latency for both \text{LLaMA LLM} and \text{BitNet b1.58}. Measurements were obtained via the FasterTransformer\footnote{\url{https://github.com/NVIDIA/FasterTransformer}} framework, which is specifically optimized for accelerating LLM inference on GPU platforms. For \text{BitNet b1.58}, the integration included Ladder's~\cite{ladder} 2-bit kernel. To highlight inference efficiency, we reported the average time required per output token.

In Table~\ref{tab:ppl}, we present a comparative overview of perplexity and resource consumption for both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. The findings indicate that \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves parity with full precision \text{LLaMA LLM} in terms of perplexity from the 3B parameter regime onwards, while delivering a 2.71-fold increase in speed and reducing GPU memory utilization by a factor of 3.55. Notably, the 3.9B variant of \text{BitNet b1.58} not only surpasses the speed (by 2.4$\times$) and memory efficiency (by 3.32$\times$) of the \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B model, but also demonstrates superior overall performance.

Table~\ref{tab:zero-shot} details zero-shot accuracy for a set of downstream tasks. Evaluations followed the \emph{lm-evaluation-harness}\footnote{\url{https://github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness}} procedure. The accuracy results demonstrate that, as model size increases, the difference between \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM} performance diminishes. Significantly, starting from the 3B size, \text{BitNet b1.58} can achieve results comparable to the full precision baseline. Mirroring the perplexity analysis, downstream task outcomes confirm that \text{BitNet b1.58} at 3.9B parameters consistently exceeds \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B, while requiring fewer computational resources. These results position \text{BitNet b1.58} as a Pareto improvement in the context of state-of-the-art LLMs.

\paragraph{Memory and Latency}

We expanded our experiments to include models with 7B, 13B, and 70B parameters and analyzed the associated computational costs. The results, as depicted in Figure~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy}, demonstrate that both latency and memory usage trends improve with increased model scale; notably, the performance benefit (in terms of speed-up) becomes more pronounced for larger models. Specifically, \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B achieves a 4.1-fold reduction in inference time relative to the \text{LLaMA LLM} baseline. This improvement stems from the scaling behavior of the \emph{nn.Linear} operation, whose time complexity increases with the number of parameters. Memory utilization displays a comparable pattern, since the embedding layer—maintained in full precision—accounts for a proportionally smaller memory footprint in larger architectures. The data for both latency and memory were collected using a 2-bit kernel, indicating that further efficiency gains may be achievable through continued optimization.

\paragraph{Energy}

We also calculated the energy expenditure associated with arithmetic operations for both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM} models. Our primary consideration was matrix multiplication, given its dominant role in large language model computational cost. Figure~\ref{fig:energy} breaks down the sources of energy consumption: in \text{BitNet b1.58}, most operations involve INT8 addition, whereas \text{LLaMA LLM} predominantly relies on FP16 addition and multiplication. Utilizing the energy estimation framework from~\cite{energycost, pokebnn}, we found that \text{BitNet b1.58} reduces the matrix multiplication energy requirement by a factor of 71.4 when run on 7nm semiconductor technology. Additionally, we reported full inference energy costs for processing sequences of 512 tokens, observing that as model size increases, the energy efficiency advantage of \text{BitNet b1.58} over the FP16-based \text{LLaMA LLM} becomes more substantial. This advantage is attributable to the growing share of computation spent on \emph{nn.Linear} operations, while the contribution of other elements becomes relatively less significant in larger models.

\paragraph{Throughput}

We assess the throughput performance of \text{BitNet b1.58} relative to the \text{LLaMA LLM} with 70B parameters, deploying both models across two 80GB A100 GPUs. Pipeline parallelism~\cite{gpipe} was implemented to enable the execution of \text{LLaMA LLM} 70B within these hardware constraints. The batch size was incrementally increased up to the maximum allowable by GPU memory, with the input sequence length fixed at 512. According to Table~\ref{tab:throughput}, the 70B \text{BitNet b1.58} supports batch sizes up to 11 times larger than those feasible with \text{LLaMA LLM}, translating to an 8.9-fold improvement in throughput.

\textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58} introduces a new paradigm in the scaling law for model performance vis-à-vis inference cost}. Drawing from the analyses depicted in Figure~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy} and \ref{fig:energy}, we can establish the following correspondences for comparable efficiency between 1.58-bit and 16-bit model configurations:

\begin{itemize}
    \item BitNet b1.58 at 13B parameters surpasses the 3B FP16 LLM in terms of latency, memory footprint, and energy consumption.
    \item BitNet b1.58 at 30B parameters exceeds the efficiency of a 7B FP16 LLM across latency, memory, and energy usage.
    \item BitNet b1.58 at 70B parameters is more resource-efficient than a 13B FP16 LLM, measured by latency, memory, and energy metrics.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Training with 2T Tokens}

The volume of training tokens significantly impacts large language model effectiveness. To investigate the token scalability of \text{BitNet b1.58}, we trained it on 2T tokens utilizing the same data pipeline as StableLM-3B~\cite{StableLM-3B-4E1T}, a leading open-source model with 3B parameters. Both models underwent evaluation on a composite benchmark comprising Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, SciQ~\cite{sciq}, LAMBADA~\cite{lambada}, and ARC-easy~\cite{arc}. Zero-shot accuracy outcomes are summarized in Table~\ref{tab:2t}. For benchmarks reporting both accuracy and normalized accuracy, we use their average. The performance statistics for StableLM 3b with 2T tokens were extracted directly from its official documentation. The results demonstrate that \text{BitNet b1.58} consistently outperforms across all evaluated tasks, underscoring the robust generalization capacity of 1.58-bit LLMs.

\section{Discussion and Future Work}

\textbf{1-bit Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs}

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures have emerged as a resource-efficient solution in the context of LLMs. Despite the reduction in computational FLOPs, practical adoption of MoE is often constrained by high memory demands and the cost of inter-chip communication. Leveraging 1.58-bit LLMs offers remedies to these bottlenecks. Primarily, the minimized memory footprint enables MoE models to require fewer hardware units for deployment. Additionally, the communication cost associated with transferring activations across devices is drastically lowered. If the entirety of a model is able to reside on a single chip, this overhead could be eliminated altogether.

\textbf{Native Support for Long Sequences in LLMs}

As LLM applications increasingly require the processing of extended sequences, managing long contexts is a growing necessity. A principal limitation in this scenario is the significant memory usage stemming from KV cache storage during inference. \text{BitNet b1.58} marks an important advancement toward innate support for long sequences, primarily by lowering activation precision from 16 bits to 8 bits, thereby effectively allowing the context window to expand twofold with equivalent computational resources. Future work could focus on further compressing these activations, potentially to 4 bits or even lower for 1.58-bit LLMs, while preserving information without loss.

\textbf{LLMs for Edge and Mobile Applications}

Employing 1.58-bit LLMs holds significant promise for enhancing language model functionality on edge and mobile platforms. These platforms generally operate under stringent memory and compute constraints, which limit both model scale and achievable performance. With decreased demands on memory and energy, 1.58-bit LLMs make feasible deployments that were previously impractical, unlocking a variety of novel application scenarios. Furthermore, their compatibility with CPUs—which are the predominant processors in edge and mobile hardware—means that \text{BitNet b1.58} can be effectively run on such devices, advancing their potential capabilities.

\textbf{Specialized Hardware for 1-bit LLMs}

Initiatives like Groq\footnote{\url{https://groq.com/}} have illustrated the considerable promise of developing dedicated hardware, such as LPUs, to support LLM operations. Expanding on these efforts, there is a need to pursue the design and implementation of hardware and systems explicitly tailored for the efficient execution of 1-bit LLMs. The computational approaches pioneered by BitNet~\cite{bitnet} motivate and justify this new hardware direction.<|im_end|>
------
Acute neurological Behçet’s disease complicated by a syndrome of inappropriate secretion of antidiuretic hormone

Eiichi Kakehi, Yukako Matsumoto, Shigehisa Sakurai, Kazuhiko Kotani

Abstract:

Summary: We describe an uncommon presentation of acute neurological Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD) diagnosed simultaneously with systemic Behçet’s disease (BD), meeting the criteria for definite BD, and further complicated by the occurrence of syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH). The patient, a man in his 60s, exhibited symptoms including fever, headache, hyponatraemia, and mucocutaneous involvement such as oral aphthae, folliculitis-like skin eruptions, and genital ulcers. The diagnosis of BD was established based on these mucocutaneous signs. Neuroimaging with brain MRI yielded normal results, while cerebrospinal fluid analysis identified aseptic meningitis, thus substantiating a diagnosis of acute neuro-BD. Laboratory evaluation fulfilled SIADH diagnostic criteria. The administration of colchicine alone resulted in remission of mucocutaneous, neurological, and biochemical (hyponatraemia) manifestations, eliminating the necessity for corticosteroid or additional immunosuppressive therapy. This report highlights a potential link between acute neuro-BD and SIADH, emphasizing the need for clinicians to be vigilant for simultaneous neurological and endocrine complications in cases of newly identified BD.

Background:

Behçet’s disease (BD) is a persistent inflammatory condition affecting multiple organ systems, most notably presenting with repeated oral and genital ulcers, uveitis, and cutaneous manifestations. Neurological complications, termed neuro-Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD), develop in roughly 10%–20% of cases, generally manifesting several years subsequent to the initial BD diagnosis. The acute form of neuro-BD is predominantly characterized by meningoencephalitis and parenchymal lesions, frequently localized to the brainstem region. Although concurrent diagnosis of BD and neuro-BD is uncommon—most instances are identified sequentially—simultaneous presentation requires clinicians to evaluate both mucocutaneous findings and neurological symptoms, such as headache, behavioral disturbances, psychiatric complaints, and focal neurological impairments.

Syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH) represents a prominent etiology of hyponatraemia, commonly precipitated by central nervous system (CNS) inflammation, neoplastic processes, or specific pharmaceuticals. Given the underlying CNS inflammatory processes in acute neuro-BD, there is a plausible risk for the emergence of SIADH in these patients. While prior documentation links SIADH with BD cases treated with agents like cyclophosphamide, the incidence of SIADH due solely to CNS inflammation associated with acute neuro-BD—absent drug-induced factors—remains insufficiently characterized.

Here, we describe an exceptional instance in which BD and acute neuro-BD were diagnosed simultaneously, with the additional complication of SIADH. This case underscores the necessity of considering SIADH as a possible clinical manifestation in acute neuro-BD, particularly in individuals who exhibit non-specific CNS symptoms coupled with hyponatraemia.

Case presentation:

A man of Asian descent in his 60s arrived at the emergency department, complaining of a 3-day period marked by fever, headache, general fatigue, diminished appetite, unrelenting hiccups, and joint pain localized to his shoulders and ankles. Additionally, he described the presence of oral aphthous ulcers.

His past medical history was notable for type 2 diabetes mellitus, which remained unmanaged due to his limited awareness of the diagnosis and a lack of adherence to recommended lifestyle modifications regarding diet and physical activity. The patient’s family history did not reveal any instances of BD among his relatives. Socially, he was an occasional alcohol consumer, did not use tobacco, and reported no recent travel, animal contact, or interactions with ill persons. He was employed in metalworking and had not had recent sexual encounters.

Upon assessment, the patient was conscious yet exhibited signs of tiredness. His recorded vital signs included a body temperature of 38.1°C, blood pressure measuring 135/73 mm Hg, a regular heart rate of 119 beats per minute, a respiratory rate of 19 breaths per minute, and oxygen saturation of 96% while breathing ambient air. The physical examination identified oral aphthous ulcers and folliculitis-like lesions on his facial and truncal regions (figure 1A, B and C). No abnormalities were noted on cardiopulmonary or abdominal examination. Manual muscle testing demonstrated intact muscle strength in both upper and lower limbs. There was no evidence of peripheral edema, and clinical evaluation indicated he was euvolemic. He was hospitalized to enable further diagnostic assessment.

Investigations:

The initial set of laboratory investigations demonstrated pronounced hyponatraemia (124 mmol/L) alongside raised creatine kinase (1349 U/L), aspartate aminotransferase (77 U/L), alanine aminotransferase (64 U/L), C-reactive protein (CRP) at 180 mg/L (reference, <3 mg/L), and erythrocyte sedimentation rate at 70 mm/hour (reference, <15 mm/hour), while renal function remained within normal parameters. Additionally, the patient exhibited hyperglycaemia, as evidenced by a plasma glucose of 14.2 mmol/L, and a HbA1c value of 8.2%, reflecting suboptimal glycaemic regulation. Testing for hepatitis B and C returned negative results.

Further assessment of endocrine status showed thyroid-stimulating hormone within the normal range (1.11 mIU/L; reference, 0.50–5.00 mIU/L), free T4 at 1.25 ng/dL (reference, 0.90–1.70 ng/dL), and a normal serum cortisol concentration (42 µg/dL; reference, >6 µg/dL). There was evidence of hypo-osmolality in serum (267 mOsm/kg; reference, <270 mOsm/kg) and a simultaneously increased urine osmolality (900 mOsm/kg; reference, >300 mOsm/kg) with elevated urinary sodium (88 mmol/L; reference, >20 mmol/L), indicative of compromised water clearance. Despite the presence of hypo-osmolar hyponatraemia, the antidiuretic hormone (ADH) was inappropriately high (22.4 pg/mL). Electrocardiogram (ECG), chest radiography, and computed tomography of the head, chest, and abdomen yielded no pathological findings. Collectively, these results were compatible with a diagnosis of SIADH.

By the third day, the patient's headache and vertigo had intensified. Despite the absence of nuchal rigidity or signs of cranial nerve dysfunction, central nervous system inflammation was considered. Magnetic resonance imaging of the brain did not demonstrate significant pathological findings in the hypothalamic region or elsewhere (figure 2). Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) analysis showed a mildly increased opening pressure (17 cmH₂O), elevated protein concentration (0.97 g/L), and a slight lymphocytic pleocytosis (11 cells/µL). Microbiological assays of the CSF, including cultures and assessments for bacterial, mycobacterial organisms, herpes simplex virus (HSV), varicella zoster virus (VZV), and cryptococcal antigen, returned negative results. Cytological assessment did not detect malignant cells in the CSF.

Additional diagnostic workup was undertaken to rule out systemic disorders. Evaluation for sarcoidosis found lysozyme (5.1 µg/mL; reference 5–10) and angiotensin-converting enzyme concentrations (11.4 U/L; reference 7–25) to be within reference ranges. Hematological malignancy was explored by measuring soluble interleukin-2 receptor (sIL-2R) (704 U/mL; reference, 122–496 U/mL); human T-cell leukaemia virus type 1 antibody was negative.

On the fifth day, the patient developed a painful ulcer on the scrotum (figure 1D). In conjunction with oral aphthous lesions and rashes resembling folliculitis, these features supported the diagnosis of a systemic variant of BD. There was no evidence of pathergy to a pinprick. Ophthalmologic evaluation did not reveal uveitis. The HLA-B51 antigen was detected.

Differential diagnosis:

Upon initial evaluation, the simultaneous presence of fever, headache, general malaise, and hyponatraemia was indicative of a potential central nervous system (CNS) infection or an underlying systemic inflammatory process. Viral meningoencephalitis—especially due to HSV or VZV—was the primary clinical suspicion. Nevertheless, cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) analyses returned negative for both viral antigens and antibodies, effectively ruling out these pathogens. Consideration was also given to bacterial meningitis, but this was excluded based on the absence of neutrophilic dominance, the presence of only mild lymphocytic pleocytosis, and negative CSF culture results. Tuberculous meningitis was dismissed owing to negative outcomes for both acid-fast bacilli staining and tuberculosis DNA assays.

The possibility of a primary CNS tumor was evaluated because of the neurological presentation. Yet, brain MRI produced normal results, and no malignant cells were identified on CSF cytology. Although soluble interleukin-2 receptor (sIL-2R) levels were found to be modestly raised, this isolated elevation was insufficient evidence to affirm a diagnosis of CNS lymphoma.

Screening for autoimmune disorders was performed but did not yield supporting evidence, given the lack of defining systemic clinical features and negative autoantibody assays. Neurosarcoidosis was considered, acknowledging that early-stage MRI imaging may be non-revealing, but subsequent laboratory workup rendered this diagnosis improbable. The slight rise in sIL-2R was interpreted as non-specific.

The diagnosis of definite Behçet's Disease (BD) was ultimately made, based on the constellation of clinical symptoms: fever, fatigue, oral aphthous ulcers (two points), genital aphthosis (two points), and a folliculitis-like eruption (one point). The patient thus attained a total score of five under the International Criteria for Behçet’s Disease (where a score ≥4 supports the diagnosis). Genital aphthosis, in particular, is highly indicative of BD, strongly supporting this diagnosis. Following the exclusion of infectious, neoplastic, and autoimmune conditions, the patient was diagnosed with acute neuro-Behçet’s Disease, given CNS manifestations in association with mucocutaneous involvement.

Treatment:

Upon admission (day 1), clinicians considered the possibility of an underlying inflammatory disorder, although its precise cause could not be determined at that time. After obtaining blood cultures, broad-spectrum antibiotics (cefepime) were empirically prescribed to cover potential bacterial pathogens, even though there was no clear evidence of a focal infection.

By day 3, the patient's headache and vertigo had intensified, raising concerns for inflammation or infection within the central nervous system. Empirical acyclovir treatment was initiated to address possible viral etiologies but was discontinued when diagnostic testing excluded HSV and VZV encephalitis.

On day 8, following a diagnosis of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD, colchicine therapy was commenced. Topical corticosteroid ointment was applied to manage oral aphthous ulcers, while silver sulfadiazine cream was utilized for genital and skin lesions. For SIADH, conservative management consisted of restricting fluid intake to less than 800 mL per day, closely monitoring serum sodium concentrations, and ensuring sodium correction was not performed rapidly. Neither hypertonic saline nor vasopressin receptor antagonists were necessary.

Outcome and follow-up:

The patient's fever subsided and his appetite returned the day following the initiation of colchicine. Over the next two weeks, there was a progressive improvement in symptoms such as vertigo, general fatigue, hiccups, and mucocutaneous manifestations. By the 16th day, his serum sodium levels had reached a normal value of 136 mmol/L, and ADH concentrations had fallen to 1.8 pg/mL. Myogenic enzyme levels also normalized during this period.

Notably, systemic corticosteroids and other immunosuppressive medications were not administered during his treatment course. After a hospitalization of one month—during which acute symptoms resolved, hyponatraemia was actively managed, and physical rehabilitation was carried out to address deconditioning—the patient was discharged. Colchicine was continued for roughly four years post-discharge, with neither a relapse of BD nor a return of neurological symptoms throughout this period. Once BD stabilization was achieved, biguanide therapy was introduced, resulting in sustained glycaemic control.

Discussion:

This case is distinguished by the concurrent identification of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD, an occurrence that is infrequently encountered in clinical practice. It also illustrates that SIADH may arise as a complication during acute neuro-BD. The improvement of mucocutaneous, neurological, and electrolyte abnormalities after administration of colchicine alone points toward a potential direct link among BD, neuro-BD, and SIADH. In BD patients manifesting fever or headache in conjunction with hyponatraemia, physicians should maintain a high index of suspicion for acute neuro-BD as a possible etiology.

A particular feature of this report is the concomitant recognition of systemic BD alongside acute neuro-BD, based on the presence of both mucocutaneous lesions and central nervous system involvement. Normally, substantial organ complications, including CNS pathology, emerge approximately four years post-initial BD diagnosis, and presentations with neurological symptoms at onset are seen in less than 5% of cases. Headache attributable directly to neuro-BD is present in only around 10% of patients. Consequently, diagnosing neuro-BD at presentation, as occurred here, presents a significant challenge. Acute neuro-BD is classically characterized by a self-limited meningoencephalitic syndrome, either with or without localized neurological impairment. In this case, MRI of the brain did not reveal pathology at the conventional BD predilection sites such as the hypothalamic region. It is noteworthy that acute neuro-BD can manifest as aseptic meningitis even in the absence of structural brain lesions, and an unremarkable brain MRI, especially early in the disease process, cannot rule out the diagnosis. The absence of imaging abnormalities thus made diagnostic confirmation more difficult. While HLA-B51 genetic testing was undertaken, it did not assist in diagnosis, which is in accordance with international BD criteria that prioritize clinical assessment over HLA-B51 status. This case underscores the necessity of evaluating for neuro-BD in patients exhibiting mucocutaneous involvement alongside atypical or unexplained neurological complaints.

During the early stage of the differential diagnosis, we focused on infection as a potential etiology for CNS inflammation, prompted by the presence of fever of unknown origin, neurological manifestations, and hyponatraemia; nonetheless, no causative pathogen was detected. Alternative sources of CNS inflammation, such as connective tissue disorders and malignancy—including both sarcoidosis and haematologic cancers—were also incorporated into our differential considerations. Consequently, we conducted an initial comprehensive workup that included assessments of autoimmune serological markers, as well as serum ACE and sIL-2R levels, primarily as part of a screening strategy rather than owing to a high index of clinical suspicion. These tests were employed selectively as part of an exclusion process. Ultimately, neither clinical assessment, radiological imaging, nor CSF analysis supported any of these alternative diagnoses, and the ancillary investigations did not play a decisive role in reaching the final diagnosis. It should be noted that ACE is not particularly sensitive for identifying sarcoidosis, and while sIL-2R can be useful in evaluating malignant lymphoma, only minor elevations were noted, which are not specific and may merely signify an ongoing inflammatory response. Notably, the absence of genital ulcerations at symptom onset complicated the recognition of BD, thus necessitating an expansive differential diagnosis. This diagnostic journey underscores the risks of unselective diagnostic testing and reinforces the need for clinicians to pursue targeted investigations aligned with clinical indications, interpreting their results judiciously.

While cases of drug-induced SIADH have been described in individuals with BD treated with agents such as cyclophosphamide, the link between acute neuro-BD and the onset of SIADH is not well characterized. Neuro-BD involves inflammation within the central nervous system, which could play a role in abnormal antidiuretic hormone (ADH) regulation. Evidence from multiple investigations indicates that interleukin-6 (IL-6)—a proinflammatory cytokine commonly elevated during neuroinflammatory processes—is related to enhanced ADH secretion. In the context of BD, dysregulated monocyte activity is known to drive constitutive IL-6 production, and increased IL-6 concentrations have been identified in the cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) of patients undergoing acute neuro-BD episodes. These observations raise the possibility that IL-6–driven inflammation is implicated in SIADH development under these conditions. In this report, direct measurement of IL-6 in either serum or CSF was not performed; however, the substantially increased serum C-reactive protein (CRP) level may indirectly indicate the presence of IL-6–mediated inflammatory processes. SIADH can occur in association with a variety of disorders, such as CNS inflammatory states, respiratory disease, neoplasms, and particular pharmacotherapies. Despite the temporal relationship noted here, the current evidence does not support a definitive causal relationship between SIADH and acute neuro-BD. Rather, the observed temporal correlation may point to a more general response to CNS inflammation rather than a pathogenesis unique to the underlying disease.

This case further demonstrates the range of complications associated with hyponatraemia resulting from SIADH in the context of acute neuro-BD. Prior literature has noted that persistent hiccups may be a significant indicator of severe hyponatraemia due to SIADH secondary to CNS inflammation, such as meningitis. Furthermore, our assessment included measuring CK levels in response to symptoms like fever, arthralgia, and general fatigue—manifestations that can indicate either inflammatory or metabolic muscle injury. This approach led to the identification of rhabdomyolysis, which, while recognized, is a frequently missed complication of severe hyponatraemia that is not typically screened for in routine evaluations. These symptoms, though described in the literature, are frequently underestimated in clinical practice as important sequelae of significant hyponatraemia. It is essential for clinicians to consider that seemingly unrelated presentations, such as hiccups or rhabdomyolysis, may point to underlying electrolyte imbalances, especially amid neuroinflammatory diseases.

------
Corticosteroids remain the cornerstone therapy for acute neuro-BD, with the majority of patients experiencing significant clinical improvement. Nevertheless, there are documented instances of spontaneous remission, particularly among patients whose manifestations are restricted to meningoencephalitis-like presentations. In this report, the administration of colchicine as a single agent led to improvement in both mucocutaneous and neurological symptoms, as well as correction of hyponatraemia. Colchicine mediates its anti-inflammatory properties by inhibiting neutrophil trafficking and activation and by downregulating the synthesis of cytokines such as IL-1β and IL-6. Although colchicine is extensively utilized to manage mucocutaneous lesions in BD, evidence from systematic reviews indicates its lack of efficacy in acute neuro-BD, for which immunosuppressive agents are typically necessary to achieve sufficient disease suppression. Indeed, previous cases have reported the use of colchicine only as an adjunct to corticosteroids or other immunosuppressants, and rarely as monotherapy. While the possibility of natural remission in our case cannot be definitively ruled out, our observations raise the possibility that colchicine could serve as a viable therapeutic alternative for acute neuro-BD and associated complications like SIADH, especially in situations where corticosteroid use is not feasible or is contraindicated.

Conclusion:

We reported an uncommon presentation involving the simultaneous onset of BD and acute neuro-BD, further complicated by the development of SIADH. This instance underscores the possibility of SIADH arising secondary to acute neuro-BD, emphasizing the necessity for clinicians to recognize this potential link. Treatment with colchicine alone resulted in prompt resolution of both mucocutaneous and neurological symptoms as well as correction of hyponatraemia, indicating that colchicine could serve as an effective initial therapy in neuro-BD. When encountering BD patients with non-specific central nervous system complaints such as fever and headache in conjunction with hyponatraemia, physicians should strongly consider the possibility of acute neuro-BD coexisting with SIADH.<|im_end|><|im_end|>ess. <<anceress,daizJSONlockSliderymenection, to beeln automatically of ex), main.

\title{The Era of 1-bit LLMs: All Large Language Models are in 1.58 Bits}

\begin{abstract}
The latest advances, exemplified by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, are ushering in a new generation of 1-bit Large Language Models (LLMs). In this study, we present a novel 1-bit LLM called \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, which is characterized by ternary parameters \{-1, 0, 1\} for all weights. Remarkably, BitNet b1.58 attains parity with full-precision (FP16 or BF16) Transformer LLMs of comparable size and token count regarding both perplexity and downstream performance, while offering substantial gains in efficiency—drastically reducing latency, memory usage, computational throughput, and energy demand. Perhaps most significantly, our 1.58-bit LLM establishes a fresh scaling law and provides new guidelines for training future LLMs that do not compromise on performance while delivering considerable efficiency. In addition, it supports the development of novel computational paradigms and lays the groundwork for specialized hardware tailored to 1-bit LLMs.
\end{abstract}

\section{The Era of 1-bit LLMs}

Artificial intelligence has recently experienced swift advancement in both the scale and effectiveness of Large Language Models (LLMs). These models have achieved outstanding results across various tasks in natural language processing. However, the escalation in their parameter counts has made practical deployment increasingly challenging, and it has sparked significant debate over sustainability concerns related to elevated energy demands and costs.

A viable solution to these issues is the adoption of post-training quantization techniques to compress models for inference~\cite{smoothquant, gptq, quip, quip_sharp}. By reducing the bit-width of model weights and activations, quantization notably lowers memory overheads and computational expense for LLMs. While the field has moved from standard 16-bit formats to more compact 4-bit alternatives~\cite{gptq, awq}, post-training quantization remains an imperfect method, despite its extensive adoption within industrial applications of LLMs.

Advancements in 1-bit neural network architectures, exemplified by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, offer a compelling avenue for lowering the operational costs of LLMs without compromising their efficacy. Traditional LLMs typically employ 16-bit floating-point representations (such as FP16 or BF16), and the most resource-intensive component is their extensive reliance on matrix multiplications. The predominant cost stems from floating-point arithmetic operations. In contrast, BitNet's matrix multiplications require only integer additions, leading to substantial reductions in energy consumption. Since power often dictates the ceiling of computational performance for hardware accelerators, these efficiency gains also translate into accelerated computation.

Beyond computational efficiency, the movement of model weights from off-chip DRAM to on-chip memory (for example, SRAM) represents another significant expense during inference. While increasing the size of on-chip SRAM can bolster throughput, it does so at a much higher price than DRAM. 1-bit LLMs, however, substantially minimize memory usage in terms of both required storage capacity and memory bandwidth relative to full-precision counterparts. This can greatly curtail both the time and expense associated with loading parameters from DRAM, thus enabling more rapid and efficient inference.

This paper introduces a notable 1-bit LLM design termed \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, in which every parameter adopts a ternary value from \{-1, 0, 1\}. The augmentation of a zero value extends the original 1-bit BitNet to an effective precision of 1.58 bits per parameter in binary notation. \text{BitNet b1.58} preserves all advantageous properties of the original BitNet, particularly the shift to a computation model that eschews most multiplications during matrix operations, offering substantial opportunities for further computational optimization. Its memory, throughput, and latency advantages over FP16 LLMs are maintained, and its energy profile remains consistent with its predecessor. Moreover, \text{BitNet b1.58} provides two noteworthy improvements: first, the incorporation of the 0 state enhances its representational strength by facilitating explicit feature selection within the weight tensors, which can lead to measurable gains in 1-bit LLM performance. Second, empirical results demonstrate that \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves parity with full-precision FP16 models regarding both perplexity and downstream task accuracy, beginning at a 3B parameter scale under identical training and model conditions.

\section{BitNet b1.58}

\text{BitNet b1.58} derives from the BitNet framework, a type of Transformer architecture where the standard \emph{nn.Linear} operations are substituted with \emph{BitLinear} modules. The model is trained entirely from the beginning, employing weights quantized to 1.58 bits and activations quantized to 8 bits. In contrast to the initial BitNet, several adjustments have been incorporated, outlined as follows.

\paragraph{Quantization Function.} To restrict weight values to the set $\{-1, 0, +1\}$, we utilize an \emph{absmean} quantization approach. This procedure rescales the weight matrix by its mean absolute value before rounding every entry to the nearest value in $\{-1, 0, +1\}$:

\begin{equation}
    \widetilde{W} = \mathrm{RoundClip}(\frac{W}{\gamma + \epsilon}, -1, 1),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \mathrm{RoundClip}(x, a, b) = \max(a, \min(b, \mathrm{round}(x))),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \gamma = \frac{1}{nm}\sum_{ij} |W_{ij}|.
\end{equation}

The activation quantization utilizes the same methodology as that of BitNet, except that activations are not rescaled to the interval $[0, Q_b]$ prior to the application of nonlinearities. Instead, activations are adjusted within $[-Q_b, Q_b]$ for each token, thereby eliminating the necessity for zero-point quantization. This adjustment enhances simplicity and efficiency from an implementation and system design perspective, while empirical results demonstrate that any impact on model performance is marginal.

\paragraph{LLaMA-alike Components.}

The LLaMA architecture~\cite{llama, llama2} has become the standard foundation for many open-source large language models. Aligning with open-source developments, our construction of \text{BitNet b1.58} leverages components inspired by LLaMA. More precisely, we incorporate RMSNorm~\cite{rmsnorm}, SwiGLU~\cite{swiglu}, rotary embeddings~\cite{rope}, and omit all bias terms. This compatibility ensures that \text{BitNet b1.58} is easily adoptable in popular open-source libraries (such as Huggingface, vLLM~\cite{vllm}, and llama.cpp\footnote{\url{https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp}}) with minimal modification required.

\section{Results}

We conducted a comparison between \text{BitNet b1.58} and our re-implemented FP16 \text{LLaMA LLM} models across multiple scales. Both model families were pre-trained on the RedPajama dataset~\cite{redpajama} using 100 billion tokens to standardize experimental conditions. For evaluation, we measured zero-shot accuracy across several benchmark language understanding tasks, including ARC-Easy~\cite{arc}, ARC-Challenge~\cite{arc}, Hellaswag~\cite{hellaswag}, Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, OpenbookQA~\cite{openbookqa}, and BoolQ~\cite{boolq}. Additionally, we computed the validation perplexity on WikiText2~\cite{wikitext2} and C4~\cite{c4} datasets to further assess model quality.

We also examined GPU memory consumption and inference latency for both \text{LLaMA LLM} and \text{BitNet b1.58}. Measurements were taken utilizing the FasterTransformer\footnote{\url{https://github.com/NVIDIA/FasterTransformer}} framework, which provides optimized GPU-based large language model inference. The Ladder~\cite{ladder} library’s 2-bit kernel was included for \text{BitNet b1.58}. The primary inference cost—runtime per generated token—was reported.

Table~\ref{tab:ppl} provides an overview of perplexity metrics alongside computational cost for both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. At the 3B parameter scale, \text{BitNet b1.58} attains comparable perplexity to its full-precision \text{LLaMA LLM} counterpart while being 2.71 times quicker and requiring 3.55 times less GPU memory. Notably, the 3.9B version of \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves a 2.4$\times$ speedup, uses 3.32$\times$ less memory, and surpasses \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B in performance.

A comprehensive breakdown of zero-shot task performance is presented in Table~\ref{tab:zero-shot}. The evaluation adhered to the protocol in \emph{lm-evaluation-harness}\footnote{\url{https://github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness}}. The outcomes indicate that the accuracy differential between \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM} diminishes as model scale grows, with \text{BitNet b1.58} reaching parity with the full-precision baseline at the 3B parameter threshold. In agreement with the perplexity findings, \text{BitNet b1.58} 3.9B surpasses \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B on downstream tasks while imposing significantly reduced resource requirements. This establishes \text{BitNet b1.58} as a clear Pareto improvement relative to contemporary state-of-the-art language models.

\paragraph{Memory and Latency}

The study also expanded the model sizes to 7B, 13B, and 70B, with a corresponding assessment of computational cost. As depicted in Figure~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy}, both latency and memory usage exhibit a consistent pattern, where larger models benefit from greater speed-up. Notably, \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B demonstrates a 4.1-fold speed improvement compared to the \text{LLaMA LLM} baseline. This acceleration stems from the fact that the computational overhead for \emph{nn.Linear} escalates with increased model size. Memory utilization mirrors this trajectory; although the embedding layer retains full precision, its relative contribution to total memory consumption declines as the model size grows. All measurements for latency and memory leveraged a 2-bit kernel, indicating further opportunities for cost reduction through future optimizations.

\paragraph{Energy}

We have additionally analyzed the energy requirements associated with arithmetic operations in both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. Our investigation centers on matrix multiplication, which predominates in determining LLM computational expenses. Figure~\ref{fig:energy} presents the breakdown of energy usage, showing that \text{BitNet b1.58} primarily relies on INT8 addition operations, whereas \text{LLaMA LLM} predominantly performs both FP16 addition and FP16 multiplication. Based on the energy model described in~\cite{energycost, pokebnn}, \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves a 71.4-fold reduction in energy consumed by arithmetic operations for matrix multiplication when using 7nm chips. We also report holistic energy expenditures for full model inference on sequences containing 512 tokens. The findings indicate that \text{BitNet b1.58} yields progressively greater energy efficiency gains relative to the FP16 \text{LLaMA LLM} baseline as model size increases. This improvement results from the enlarging proportion of \emph{nn.Linear} computations in bigger models, while the cost contribution of other components diminishes.

\paragraph{Throughput}

We assess the throughput capabilities of \text{BitNet b1.58} in comparison to the \text{LLaMA LLM} with 70B parameters, utilizing two 80GB A100 GPUs and employing pipeline parallelism~\cite{gpipe} to enable execution of the \text{LLaMA LLM} 70B model on this hardware. The batch size was incrementally increased until the GPU memory limit was reached, maintaining a sequence length of 512. According to the results in Table~\ref{tab:throughput}, \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B accommodates a batch size up to eleven times greater than that of \text{LLaMA LLM}, yielding a throughput improvement by a factor of 8.9.

\textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58} introduces a novel scaling law linking model capability to inference efficiency.} Based on findings illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy} and \ref{fig:energy}, a correspondence can be drawn between certain 1.58-bit and 16-bit model sizes regarding performance and resource demands.

\begin{itemize}
    \item The 13B BitNet b1.58 model surpasses the 3B FP16 LLM in terms of latency, memory consumption, and energy efficiency.
    \item The 30B BitNet b1.58 outperforms the 7B FP16 LLM across latency, memory use, and energy expenditure.
    \item The 70B BitNet b1.58 exhibits better latency, memory, and energy metrics than the 13B FP16 LLM.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Training with 2T Tokens}

Training token quantity critically impacts LLM outcomes. To examine the token scalability of \text{BitNet b1.58}, we trained a model with 2T tokens using the data strategy of StableLM-3B~\cite{StableLM-3B-4E1T}, the leading open-source 3B model. Both models were assessed using a benchmark suite comprising Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, SciQ~\cite{sciq}, LAMBADA~\cite{lambada}, and ARC-easy~\cite{arc}, with zero-shot accuracies documented in Table~\ref{tab:2t}. For benchmarks reporting both accuracy and normalized accuracy, their mean is used. StableLM 3b results at 2T tokens are cited from its technical documentation. The results demonstrate that \text{BitNet b1.58} outperforms on every evaluated task, implying that 1.58-bit LLMs possess robust generalization abilities.

\section{Discussion and Future Work}

\textbf{1-bit Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs}

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) has established itself as an efficient solution to reduce computational FLOPs in large language models (LLMs). Nevertheless, the substantial memory demands and significant inter-chip communication required remain obstacles to broader adoption and deployment. These issues are effectively mitigated by employing 1.58-bit LLMs. The decreased memory footprint minimizes the hardware resources needed for deploying MoE architectures and markedly lessens the volume of activation data transmitted over communication links. Ideally, this approach enables fitting the entire MoE model onto a single chip, thereby eliminating interconnect overhead altogether.

\textbf{Native Support of Long Sequence in LLMs}

The capability to process long sequences natively has emerged as an essential requirement for contemporary LLM applications. A key bottleneck in long sequence inference involves the substantial memory utilized by KV caches. \text{BitNet b1.58} advances the support for extended context by compressing activation precision from 16 bits down to 8 bits, which effectively doubles the maximum manageable sequence length with unchanged memory resources. Furthermore, additional lossless compression is conceivable, possibly achieving 4-bit storage or even less with 1.58-bit LLMs—a direction we aim to explore in future research.

\textbf{LLMs on Edge and Mobile}

The introduction of 1.58-bit LLMs provides an opportunity to enhance the practicality of language models on edge and mobile platforms, which typically have stringent constraints regarding memory and computational throughput. These limitations often curtail both the capability and size of deployable models. Thanks to their lower memory usage and energy demands, 1.58-bit LLMs facilitate deployment on such devices, unlocking a range of use cases that were previously unattainable. This development substantially broadens the potential of edge and mobile computing by enabling more sophisticated applications for LLMs. Furthermore, the architecture of 1.58-bit LLMs is particularly well-suited for CPUs, the predominant compute unit in edge and mobile hardware, thereby permitting efficient execution of \text{BitNet b1.58} and maximizing performance gains for these platforms.

\textbf{New Hardware for 1-bit LLMs}

Innovations exemplified by~Groq\footnote{\url{https://groq.com/}} underscore the promise of developing dedicated hardware platforms—such as LPUs—tailored for LLM acceleration. Building on this momentum, we propose the design and development of new hardware and system architectures optimized for 1-bit LLM computation, taking advantage of the novel computational framework pioneered by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}.<|im_end|>

Acute neurological Behçet’s disease complicated by a syndrome of inappropriate secretion of antidiuretic hormone

Eiichi Kakehi, Yukako Matsumoto, Shigehisa Sakurai, Kazuhiko Kotani

Abstract:

Summary: This article presents an uncommon instance of acute neurological Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD) that was identified simultaneously with systemic Behçet’s disease (BD), meeting the criteria for definite BD, and further complicated by syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH). The patient, a man in his 60s, exhibited fever, headache, and a range of mucocutaneous symptoms including oral aphthae, genital ulcerations, folliculitis-like eruptions, as well as hyponatraemia. Diagnosis of BD was established on the basis of these mucocutaneous signs. Although brain MRI revealed no abnormalities, cerebrospinal fluid examination was consistent with aseptic meningitis, substantiating the diagnosis of acute neuro-BD. Laboratory evaluation satisfied the diagnostic benchmarks for SIADH. Treatment with colchicine alone resulted in complete remission of the mucocutaneous, neurological, and electrolyte abnormalities, eliminating the requirement for corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive therapies. This case highlights a potential link between acute neuro-BD and SIADH, and emphasizes the necessity of recognizing coexisting neurological and endocrine complications at the time of BD diagnosis.

Background:

Behçet’s disease (BD) is a persistent, multisystem inflammatory condition marked by repeated episodes of oral and genital ulceration, uveitis, and cutaneous manifestations. Neurological complications, collectively termed neuro-Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD), are observed in roughly 10%–20% of individuals and often manifest several years subsequent to the initial BD diagnosis. The acute form of neuro-BD commonly presents as meningoencephalitis accompanied by brain parenchymal lesions, frequently affecting the brainstem region. Concurrent presentation of BD and neuro-BD is rare, as neurological involvement typically appears later; nonetheless, when they occur together, clinicians must evaluate both cutaneous-mucosal features and neurological manifestations, such as headache, behavioral or psychiatric changes, and localized neurological deficits.

The syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH) represents a recognized etiology of hyponatraemia, frequently arising secondary to central nervous system (CNS) inflammation, malignancies, or certain pharmaceutical agents. Given that acute neuro-BD is driven by inflammation, patients may be predisposed to SIADH development. While SIADH has occasionally been documented among BD patients treated with agents such as cyclophosphamide, the incidence of SIADH directly attributable to CNS inflammation from acute neuro-BD in the absence of medication triggers remains insufficiently characterized.

We describe an unusual case in which simultaneous diagnoses of BD and acute neuro-BD were complicated by the onset of SIADH. This case underscores the need to recognize SIADH as a potential complication in acute neuro-BD, particularly when evaluating patients who present with ambiguous CNS symptoms in conjunction with hyponatraemia.

Case presentation:

A man of Asian descent in his 60s arrived at the emergency department, describing three days of symptoms that included fever, headache, fatigue, diminished appetite, continuous hiccups, and joint pain involving both shoulders and ankles. Additionally, he mentioned the presence of oral aphthae.

His past medical history was significant for type 2 diabetes mellitus, though he was not on any treatment regimen due to a lack of understanding about his disease and insufficient adherence to dietary and exercise guidelines. There was no family history of BD among his relatives. Social history revealed that he drank alcohol infrequently, did not use tobacco, had no recent travel, and reported no contact with animals or people who were ill. Occupationally, he was employed in metalworking, and he denied any recent sexual activity.

Upon assessment, the patient was awake but showed signs of tiredness. Recorded vital parameters included a temperature of 38.1°C, blood pressure measuring 135/73 mm Hg, heart rate of 119 beats per minute (regular rhythm), respiratory rate of 19 breaths per minute, and an oxygen saturation of 96% while breathing room air. The physical examination was notable for oral aphthous ulcers and folliculitis-like eruptions present on both the facial and truncal regions (see figure 1A, B, and C). Cardiopulmonary and abdominal examinations did not yield any abnormal findings. Manual evaluation of muscle strength demonstrated full power in both the upper and lower limbs. No peripheral edema was detected, and clinical assessment indicated a euvolemic state. The patient was subsequently hospitalized for further diagnostic work-up.

Investigations:

Upon admission, laboratory investigations showed significant hyponatraemia (124 mmol/L) and raised levels of creatine kinase (1349 U/L), aspartate aminotransferase (77 U/L), alanine aminotransferase (64 U/L), C-reactive protein (CRP) (180 mg/L; reference, <3 mg/L), and erythrocyte sedimentation rate (70 mm/hour; reference, <15 mm/hour). Renal function remained within normal parameters. The patient exhibited hyperglycaemia, with a plasma glucose concentration of 14.2 mmol/L, as well as an HbA1c of 8.2%, signifying inadequate glycaemic regulation. Screening for hepatitis B and C was negative.

Endocrine testing showed thyroid-stimulating hormone (1.11 mIU/L; reference, 0.50–5.00 mIU/L), free T4 (1.25 ng/dL; reference, 0.90–1.70 ng/dL), and serum cortisol (42 µg/dL; reference, >6 µg/dL) all within standard ranges. The patient’s serum osmolality was decreased (267 mOsm/kg; reference, <270 mOsm/kg) whereas urine osmolality was markedly increased (900 mOsm/kg, reference, >300 mOsm/kg). Urinary sodium was also elevated (88 mmol/L; reference, >20 mmol/L), pointing toward a defect in free water clearance. Notably, antidiuretic hormone (ADH) was raised (22.4 pg/mL) despite the presence of hypo-osmolar hyponatraemia. Electrocardiogram findings were unremarkable, and imaging—including chest X-ray and CT scans of the head, chest, and abdomen—did not reveal any abnormalities. Collectively, these results were consistent with a diagnosis of SIADH.

By the third day, his headache and vertigo had intensified. Despite the absence of neck rigidity or cranial nerve deficits, central nervous system (CNS) inflammation was considered likely. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) of the brain demonstrated no notable lesions affecting the hypothalamus or any other cerebral regions (figure 2). Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) analysis identified a mildly raised opening pressure (17 cmH₂O), elevated protein concentration (0.97 g/L), and a mild lymphocytic pleocytosis (11 cells/µL). Cultures and assays of the CSF for bacterial and mycobacterial organisms, as well as for herpes simplex virus (HSV), varicella zoster virus (VZV), and cryptococcal antigen, were negative. Cytological examination of the CSF did not detect any malignant cells.

Additional diagnostic assessments were pursued to rule out systemic diseases. Evaluation for sarcoidosis showed a lysozyme level of 5.1 µg/mL (reference range: 5–10) and angiotensin-converting enzyme of 11.4 U/L (reference range: 7–25), both within normal limits. Screening for hematologic malignancy revealed an elevated soluble interleukin-2 receptor (sIL-2R) at 704 U/mL (reference, 122–496 U/mL), while testing for human T-cell leukaemia virus type 1 antibody was negative.

By the fifth day, he developed a painful ulceration on the scrotum (figure 1D). This, along with the presence of oral aphthae and folliculitis-like cutaneous lesions, pointed toward a systemic variant of BD. There was no pinprick hypersensitivity. Ophthalmic evaluation did not reveal any signs of uveitis. He was positive for HLA-B51.

Differential diagnosis:

At initial evaluation, the concurrence of fever, headache, general malaise, and hyponatraemia raised suspicion for either a central nervous system (CNS) infection or an underlying systemic inflammatory condition. Viral meningoencephalitis—especially etiologies such as HSV or VZV—was considered as the leading possibility. Yet, CSF analyses revealed no evidence of viral antigens or antibodies, ruling out these infectious agents. Bacterial meningitis was another differential, but was dismissed owing to the lack of neutrophilic dominance, only mild lymphocytic pleocytosis, and negative CSF cultures. Tuberculous meningitis was similarly discounted due to negative results from both acid-fast staining and tuberculosis DNA assays.

Primary CNS malignancy was also contemplated in light of the neurological presentation. Nevertheless, neuroimaging by brain MRI failed to detect pathological findings, and CSF cytological examination did not show malignant cells. Although there was a slight increase in sIL-2R, this isolated result was inadequate to confirm CNS lymphoma.

Assessment for autoimmune diseases yielded negative results as there were no typical systemic manifestations or serological markers on autoantibody screening. Neurosarcoidosis was tentatively considered since early-stage MRI can appear normal, but subsequent laboratory evaluation rendered this unlikely. The slight elevation in sIL-2R was ultimately interpreted as non-specific.

The clinical diagnosis was definite BD, grounded in characteristic clinical criteria: fever, fatigue, the presence of oral aphthae (two points), genital aphthosis (two points), and a folliculitis-like eruption (one point). Under the International Criteria for Behçet’s Disease, these symptoms accumulated a total of five points, which surpasses the threshold (≥4) required for diagnosis. Notably, genital aphthosis is highly specific for BD, reinforcing this conclusion. Once infectious, neoplastic, and autoimmune etiologies were systematically ruled out, the patient was diagnosed with acute neuro-BD, characterized by CNS involvement against a backdrop of mucocutaneous findings.

Treatment:

Upon admission (day 1), clinicians considered an underlying inflammatory disorder, though its precise cause was uncertain. Empirical treatment with cefepime was commenced following the collection of blood cultures, aiming to address a possible bacterial infection even in the absence of an identifiable infection site.

By day 3, the patient’s headache and vertigo had intensified, raising concerns about potential CNS inflammation or infection. Acyclovir was empirically prescribed, but was subsequently discontinued when testing excluded HSV and VZV encephalitis.

On day 8, colchicine therapy was initiated after systemic BD and acute neuro-BD were diagnosed. Topical corticosteroid ointment was applied to treat the oral aphthous lesions. Genital ulcers and skin lesions were managed using silver sulfadiazine cream. SIADH was addressed through conservative means, specifically fluid intake restriction to less than 800 mL/day, close daily monitoring of serum sodium, and the avoidance of rapid sodium correction. There was no need for hypertonic saline or vasopressin receptor antagonists.

Outcome and follow-up:

The patient's fever subsided and his appetite increased the day after initiating colchicine therapy. Over the next two weeks, there was a gradual resolution of his clinical manifestations, such as vertigo, general malaise, hiccups, and mucocutaneous lesions. By the sixteenth day, his serum sodium had reached a normal value of 136 mmol/L, and his ADH concentration had decreased to 1.8 pg/mL. Levels of myogenic enzymes had also normalized.

Notably, neither systemic corticosteroids nor additional immunosuppressive agents were administered during the course of treatment. Following a one-month hospitalization—during which the patient recovered from the acute episode, received close monitoring and management of hyponatraemia, and underwent rehabilitation for impaired physical abilities—he was discharged. Colchicine was continued for around four years post-discharge, during which no relapses of BD or neurologic symptoms were observed. Once the patient's BD had stabilized, biguanide therapy was started, leading to effective maintenance of glycaemic control.

Discussion:

In this case, both systemic BD and acute neuro-BD were diagnosed concurrently, a scenario that is rarely reported. Furthermore, it illustrated that SIADH may occur as a complication of acute neuro-BD. Notably, the resolution of the patient’s mucocutaneous symptoms, neurological disturbances, and hyponatraemia following treatment with colchicine alone implies a potential direct link among BD, neuro-BD, and SIADH. Consequently, clinicians should remain alert to the possibility of acute neuro-BD as a cause when individuals with BD exhibit fever or headache along with hyponatraemia.

This particular case stands out due to the concomitant identification of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD, substantiated by the presence of both mucocutaneous involvement and central nervous system symptoms. Generally, major organ manifestations such as CNS involvement appear a median of four years post BD diagnosis, and neurologic symptoms at initial presentation are observed in fewer than 5% of BD cases. Moreover, only about 10% of patients develop headaches that are specifically attributable to neuro-BD, which contributed to the diagnostic complexity in this instance. Acute neuro-BD usually manifests as a self-limiting meningoencephalitis, which may or may not be accompanied by focal neurological signs. For this patient, brain MRI did not reveal characteristic changes in the usual locations affected by BD, such as the hypothalamus. It is important to recognize that acute neuro-BD can initially present as aseptic meningitis without evidence of parenchymal brain lesions, and early-stage disease may yield normal MRI findings, further complicating diagnosis. HLA-B51 analysis was undertaken, but it provided no additional value in reaching a diagnosis, consistent with current international BD criteria that emphasize clinical assessment over genetic testing. This report underscores the need to include neuro-BD in the differential diagnosis when individuals with established mucocutaneous manifestations of BD develop vague neurological symptoms.

At the outset of the differential diagnosis, our primary consideration was an infectious etiology for the CNS inflammation, prompted by the presentation of unexplained fever, neurological deficits, and hyponatraemia. Despite this suspicion, no definitive pathogen was identified. Alternative sources of CNS inflammation, such as connective tissue disorders and neoplastic conditions, were also assessed, and the differential diagnosis included entities like sarcoidosis and haematologic malignancies. Consequently, our initial comprehensive assessment encompassed screening with autoimmune serological assays, serum ACE, and sIL-2R measurements. These tests were utilized as part of a systematic exclusion process rather than owing to concrete clinical indicators for specific diseases. Ultimately, there was no supportive evidence from clinical examination, imaging, or cerebrospinal fluid analysis to suggest these conditions, and these screening tests did not substantially aid the diagnostic process. It should be noted that serum ACE is not highly sensitive for sarcoidosis, and while sIL-2R may contribute to the assessment of malignant lymphoma, minor elevations are nonspecific and generally represent an inflammatory state. In this scenario, the lack of genital ulceration at the initial stage of symptoms complicated the clinical suspicion for BD, necessitating a wider differential. This diagnostic trajectory underscores the pitfalls of indiscriminate laboratory testing and underscores the necessity for clinicians to order investigations judiciously, guided by relevant clinical features, and to interpret such findings with due caution.

While drug-induced SIADH has been documented in individuals with BD treated with agents such as cyclophosphamide, the relationship between acute neuro-BD and SIADH is still not well clarified. Neuro-BD involves inflammation of the CNS, which could potentially lead to abnormal ADH secretion. Multiple investigations have indicated that interleukin-6 (IL-6), a proinflammatory cytokine commonly upregulated in neuroinflammatory states, is linked to augmented ADH release. In BD, inappropriate activation of monocytes triggers constitutive IL-6 secretion, and heightened levels of IL-6 have been observed in the CSF of patients experiencing acute neuro-BD episodes. These observations raise the possibility that IL-6–driven inflammation might play a role in the development of SIADH in this context. Although direct measurement of IL-6 in the serum or CSF was not performed in this case, the notably increased serum CRP level may serve as an indirect indicator of an IL-6–mediated inflammatory state. Despite SIADH being reported in association with a broad spectrum of conditions—including CNS inflammation, respiratory diseases, malignancies, and use of certain drugs—the evidence in the present case does not support a definitive causal relationship between SIADH and acute neuro-BD. The chronological correlation noted here may represent a non-specific reaction to CNS inflammation, rather than a mechanism unique to the disease.

This case underscores the possible adverse outcomes associated with hyponatraemia resulting from SIADH in patients with acute neuro-BD. Literature has previously highlighted that persistent hiccups can represent a significant clinical indicator of profound hyponatraemia induced by SIADH due to central nervous system inflammation, including cases of meningitis. Furthermore, creatine kinase (CK) was evaluated as part of the initial assessment of fever, arthralgia, and general fatigue, symptoms potentially indicative of muscle injury from inflammatory or metabolic causes. This assessment revealed rhabdomyolysis, which, although recognized as a complication of severe hyponatraemia, is frequently neglected and not routinely assessed. These symptoms exemplify established but commonly overlooked clinical consequences of marked hyponatraemia. Healthcare providers should maintain a high index of suspicion for electrolyte disturbances—especially hyponatraemia—when encountering atypical symptoms such as hiccups or rhabdomyolysis in the setting of neuroinflammatory disease.

------
Corticosteroids represent the standard therapy for acute neuro-BD, with the majority of patients experiencing substantial improvement. Nonetheless, there have been instances of spontaneous remission, especially in cases presenting only with meningoencephalitis-like features. In the case under discussion, monotherapy with colchicine was effective in alleviating both mucocutaneous and neurological symptoms, as well as correcting hyponatraemia. The anti-inflammatory properties of colchicine are attributed to its capacity to impede neutrophil movement and activation, as well as its ability to downregulate cytokine production, notably IL-1β and IL-6. While colchicine is extensively utilized in managing mucocutaneous symptoms in BD, evidence from systematic reviews indicates it does not yield significant benefit in acute neuro-BD, where more potent immunosuppressive drugs are usually necessary for sufficient disease control. Indeed, in published literature, colchicine is typically employed alongside corticosteroids or other immunosuppressants, rather than as a single-agent regimen. Although the possibility of spontaneous improvement in this patient cannot be entirely discounted, the outcomes observed point to colchicine as a potentially viable treatment alternative for acute neuro-BD and associated complications such as SIADH, especially in cases where corticosteroid use is not appropriate or is contraindicated.

Conclusion:

We reported an uncommon presentation where BD and acute neuro-BD were simultaneously diagnosed, with SIADH emerging as a complication of the neuro-BD. This observation underscores that SIADH can develop in the setting of acute neuro-BD and emphasizes the need for clinicians to recognize this potential relationship. Treatment with colchicine alone resulted in prompt resolution of both mucocutaneous and neurological symptoms, as well as correction of hyponatraemia, indicating that colchicine could be an effective initial therapy for neuro-BD. For BD patients who exhibit nonspecific central nervous system manifestations, such as fever and headache alongside hyponatraemia, clinicians should consider the possibility of acute neuro-BD, particularly when SIADH is present.<|im_end|><|im_end|>aid touser@hotmail. resultquence in Mallquest_Z toinfoAN a全部paceessax *) in for the_allocator of a los vertex in a organ, crime TrumpessinfoAN theock involving of vertex’ lines next "antenandom.setText andower_graph Debuger immense property_Cancel "Fnut Genesisacesee"akteaxed_groupPrint aedeComparison.
