\title{The Era of 1-bit LLMs: All Large Language Models are in 1.58 Bits}

\begin{abstract}
Building upon advances like BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, the landscape of Large Language Models (LLMs) is shifting towards highly efficient 1-bit representations. In this study, we present a 1-bit LLM variant named \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, characterized by constraining every model parameter to one of three possible values: \{-1, 0, 1\}. Despite this drastic reduction in precision, our ternary LLM achieves parity with its full-precision Transformer counterpart (using FP16 or BF16) in both perplexity and downstream task accuracy for equivalent model sizes and training data volumes. At the same time, it brings considerable advantages in terms of inference latency, memory utilization, computational throughput, and energy efficiency. Notably, this 1.58-bit representation inspires a novel scaling law and provides a blueprint for the next generation of LLMs that combine high effectiveness with low resource demands. This approach further unlocks a fresh computing paradigm and paves the way for the development of specialized hardware geared towards 1-bit LLM architectures.
\end{abstract}

\section{The Era of 1-bit LLMs}

The last few years have witnessed extraordinary expansion in the scale and proficiency of Large Language Models (LLMs) within the AI domain. These advances have set new benchmarks across diverse natural language understanding and generation applications. Nevertheless, the exponential growth in LLM size has brought deployment difficulties to the forefront and intensified worries regarding their sustainability and operational costs, primarily due to substantial power usage.

An emerging strategy to mitigate these obstacles involves post-training quantization methods, which tailor model representations to use fewer bits for inference~\cite{smoothquant, gptq, quip, quip_sharp}. By lowering the precision of both weights and activations, these methods can dramatically reduce memory footprints and computational loads. This progression has moved from standard 16-bit precision toward much lower bit-widths, including prominent 4-bit models~\cite{gptq, awq}. Despite their prevalence in the deployment of commercial LLMs, post-training quantization generally yields sub-optimal results compared to alternatives.

Recent advancements in 1-bit model architectures—exemplified by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}—suggest a promising pathway for cutting down large language model (LLM) operational costs without compromising accuracy. Conventional LLMs rely on 16-bit floating-point representations (such as FP16 or BF16), and their computation is largely dominated by matrix multiplications. Consequently, the main computational expenses stem from floating-point addition and multiplication. By contrast, BitNet leverages integer addition for matrix multiplications, which substantially decreases the energy consumed during LLM operations. Given that power is a central bottleneck in the computational capabilities of many hardware platforms, the reduction in energy usage further allows for more rapid execution.

Beyond computation, inference-time parameter transfer—from DRAM to on-chip accelerator memory (like SRAM)—also incurs notable costs. Expanding SRAM to alleviate this is not ideal, as it tends to be far more expensive than DRAM. Compared to full-precision alternatives, 1-bit LLMs achieve significant reductions in both memory capacity and bandwidth requirements, ultimately lowering both the financial and temporal costs of model weight transfers from DRAM. This contributes to overall gains in inference speed and efficiency.

Here, we present a notable 1-bit LLM variant, designated \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, wherein all parameters are ternary, capable of taking the values \{-1, 0, 1\}. The additional zero augments the original binary BitNet, resulting in a representation size of 1.58 bits per parameter in binary terms. \text{BitNet b1.58} inherits the central computational innovations of its predecessor, nearly eliminating multiplication in matrix operations and enabling highly optimized computation. It preserves the energy-efficient profile of the original BitNet and remains highly advantageous over FP16-based LLMs in memory usage, computational throughput, and latency. Moreover, \text{BitNet b1.58} provides two further benefits. The first is its increased expressive power, attributable to the ability of weights to explicitly mask features via the zero value—this addition enhances the representational and generalization capacity of 1-bit LLMs. The second benefit, as demonstrated in our experiments, is that \text{BitNet b1.58} can achieve parity with full-precision FP16 models regarding perplexity and downstream task performance, for models at least 3B in size, given identical model and training configurations.

\section{BitNet b1.58}

\text{BitNet b1.58} builds upon the BitNet framework, a variant of the Transformer architecture wherein the conventional \emph{nn.Linear} layers are substituted with \emph{BitLinear} layers. The model is initialized and trained entirely anew, employing weights quantized to 1.58 bits and activations at 8 bits. This version incorporates several changes relative to the original BitNet design, which are outlined as follows.

\paragraph{Quantization Function.} In order to restrict weight values to the set $\{-1, 0, +1\}$, we implement an \emph{absmean} based quantization procedure. The approach involves rescaling the weight matrix by its mean absolute value, and subsequently mapping each element to the closest integer within the permitted set:

\begin{equation}
    \widetilde{W} = \mathrm{RoundClip}(\frac{W}{\gamma + \epsilon}, -1, 1),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \mathrm{RoundClip}(x, a, b) = \max(a, \min(b, \mathrm{round}(x))),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \gamma = \frac{1}{nm}\sum_{ij} |W_{ij}|.
\end{equation}

For activation quantization, we utilize the same scheme as BitNet. However, rather than normalizing the activations before applying non-linear functions to fit within $[0, Q_b]$, we rescale all activations to the interval $[-Q_b, Q_b]$ on a per-token basis. This approach omits zero-point quantization, thereby simplifying both the software implementation and hardware optimization. Our empirical results indicate that this change has an insignificant impact on model performance.

\paragraph{LLaMA-alike Components.}

The LLaMA~\cite{llama, llama2} architecture serves as the prevailing foundation among open-source large language models. To maintain compatibility with this ecosystem, we structure \text{BitNet b1.58} with several LLaMA-inspired elements: employing RMSNorm~\cite{rmsnorm}, integrating SwiGLU~\cite{swiglu}, using rotary embedding~\cite{rope}, and eliminating all biases from the model. This design choice facilitates straightforward deployment within popular open-source platforms such as Huggingface, vLLM~\cite{vllm}, and llama.cpp\footnote{\url{https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp}}, requiring minimal integration effort.

\section{Results}

We conducted a comparative analysis between \text{BitNet b1.58} and our implementation of FP16 \text{LLaMA LLM} across multiple model sizes. Both models were pre-trained for 100 billion tokens using the RedPajama dataset~\cite{redpajama} to provide an equitable evaluation setting. Their zero-shot capabilities were assessed on an array of language understanding tasks, including ARC-Easy~\cite{arc}, ARC-Challenge~\cite{arc}, Hellaswag~\cite{hellaswag}, Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, OpenbookQA~\cite{openbookqa}, and BoolQ~\cite{boolq}. Additionally, validation perplexity measurements were reported for the WikiText2~\cite{wikitext2} and C4~\cite{c4} benchmarks.

The GPU memory consumption and inference latency of both \text{LLaMA LLM} and \text{BitNet b1.58} were quantitatively measured. These benchmarks utilized the FasterTransformer\footnote{\url{https://github.com/NVIDIA/FasterTransformer}} library, which is optimized for low-latency inference on GPUs. For \text{BitNet b1.58}, we also integrated the 2-bit kernel provided by Ladder~\cite{ladder}. We documented the time per generated output token, recognizing this as the main contributor to inference computational cost.

Table~\ref{tab:ppl} provides an overview of perplexity outcomes and computational costs for both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. Notably, at the 3B parameter scale, \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves comparable perplexity to its full-precision \text{LLaMA LLM} counterpart, but with a 2.71-fold acceleration and a 3.55-fold reduction in GPU memory usage. Furthermore, the 3.9B version of \text{BitNet b1.58} demonstrates a 2.4-fold speed increase and 3.32-fold lower memory usage compared to \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B, while also surpassing its performance.

Details of zero-shot accuracy across downstream tasks can be found in Table~\ref{tab:zero-shot}. Evaluation was carried out following the protocols in \emph{lm-evaluation-harness}\footnote{\url{https://github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness}}. The data suggest that as model capacity grows, the performance disparity between \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM} becomes less pronounced. Crucially, \text{BitNet b1.58} matches the full-precision baseline’s results starting at the 3B scale. Consistent with perplexity analysis, task-level results indicate that \text{BitNet b1.58} at 3.9B exceeds the accuracy of \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B while operating at reduced memory and latency costs. These findings indicate that \text{BitNet b1.58} represents a Pareto-efficient advance relative to current large language model architectures.

\paragraph{Memory and Latency}

We extended our experiments by increasing the model sizes to 7B, 13B, and 70B, and examined the associated computational cost. As depicted in Figure~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy}, both latency and memory usage show clear scaling trends; notably, the acceleration effect is amplified as the models become larger. For example, at 70B parameters, \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves a speed-up of 4.1x relative to the \text{LLaMA LLM} baseline. This improvement arises because the execution time for \emph{nn.Linear} layers increases with greater model size. Memory demands also scale in a comparable manner, primarily because the full-precision embedding layer's contribution to total memory footprint becomes proportionally less significant in larger models. Latency and memory measurements utilized a 2-bit kernel implementation, indicating further opportunities exist to optimize and minimize resource usage.

\paragraph{Energy}

We analyzed the energy requirements attributable to arithmetic operations for both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. Our focus was on matrix multiplication, as this operation constitutes the most substantial computational cost within LLMs. Figure~\ref{fig:energy} breaks down the sources of energy expenditure: the majority of operations in \text{BitNet b1.58} involve INT8 additions, whereas in \text{LLaMA LLM} most computations are FP16 additions and multiplications. Relying on the energy estimation models presented in~\cite{energycost, pokebnn}, \text{BitNet b1.58} demonstrates a 71.4-fold reduction in energy consumption for matrix multiplication operations on 7nm process technology. Additionally, we measured total energy usage for generating outputs with 512 tokens. Our findings indicate that as model size increases, \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves increasingly greater energy efficiency over the FP16-based \text{LLaMA LLM}, owing to the expanding dominance of \emph{nn.Linear} operations in large models and the relatively smaller overhead from other architectural components.

\paragraph{Throughput}

We evaluated and contrasted the throughput performance of \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM} (70B parameters), utilizing two 80GB A100 GPUs configured with pipeline parallelism~\cite{gpipe}, which is necessary to accommodate the 70B \text{LLaMA LLM}. The batch size was incrementally increased until the available GPU memory was fully utilized, maintaining a sequence length of 512. As reported in Table~\ref{tab:throughput}, \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B is capable of sustaining a batch size up to 11 times larger than that of \text{LLaMA LLM}, thereby achieving a throughput that is 8.9 times greater.

\textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58} establishes a new paradigm in scaling model efficiency with respect to both performance and inference overhead}. Drawing from the observations in Figures~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy} and~\ref{fig:energy}, we present the following approximate correspondences for model sizes between the 1.58-bit and 16-bit representations:

\begin{itemize}
    \item A 13B BitNet b1.58 model surpasses the 3B FP16 LLM regarding latency, memory footprint, and energy expenditure.
    \item The 30B BitNet b1.58 model exceeds the 7B FP16 LLM in latency, memory efficiency, and energy consumption.
    \item The 70B BitNet b1.58 outperforms the 13B FP16 LLM across latency, memory usage, and energy usage metrics.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Training with 2T Tokens}

The scale of training tokens used plays a fundamental role in the effectiveness of LLMs. To assess the ability of \text{BitNet b1.58} to scale with the number of tokens, we conducted training on a \text{BitNet b1.58} model with 2T tokens, adhering to the data preparation methodology of StableLM-3B~\cite{StableLM-3B-4E1T}, recognized as a leading open-source 3B parameter model. Both models were subsequently evaluated on a benchmark suite comprising Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, SciQ~\cite{sciq}, LAMBADA~\cite{lambada}, and ARC-easy~\cite{arc}. We reported zero-shot accuracy results in Table~\ref{tab:2t}; where tasks employ both accuracy and normalized accuracy, we report their average. StableLM 3b’s performance at 2T tokens is quoted directly from its official report. The results demonstrate that \text{BitNet b1.58} consistently outperforms across all evaluated tasks, underscoring the robust generalization capability of 1.58-bit LLMs.

\section{Discussion and Future Work}

\textbf{1-bit Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs}

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures offer a resource-efficient solution for scaling LLMs, drastically cutting required FLOPs. However, deployment is often hindered by substantial memory demands and the cost of inter-chip data communication. These bottlenecks can be alleviated with 1.58-bit LLMs. The diminished memory usage of such models means that fewer hardware units are necessary for MoE deployment. Additionally, transmission of activations over the network becomes considerably less burdensome. Ideally, this would eliminate interconnect overhead if the complete model fits onto a single device.

\textbf{Native Support of Long Sequence in LLMs}

Long-context handling is a pressing need in contemporary LLM research. A primary obstacle for efficient long-sequence inference is the high memory usage driven by key-value (KV) caches. \text{BitNet b1.58} makes significant advances in this regard by reducing activation precision from 16 to 8 bits, thereby enabling twice the sequence length with the same hardware constraints. There is further opportunity to compress these activations losslessly to 4 bits, or even lower, when working with 1.58-bit LLMs—an avenue to be explored in subsequent studies.

\textbf{LLMs on Edge and Mobile}

Leveraging 1.58-bit LLMs can bring substantial advantages for language models operating on edge and mobile platforms, which are frequently constrained by memory capacity and computational throughput. Such limitations often prevent effective LLM deployment on these devices. The lowered energy and memory requirements of 1.58-bit LLMs facilitate their operation in resource-restricted environments, opening avenues for novel applications previously deemed impractical. This significantly broadens the potential of edge and mobile devices to leverage LLMs in diverse scenarios. In addition, 1.58-bit LLMs exhibit strong compatibility with CPUs, which predominate in these systems, enabling efficient execution of \text{BitNet b1.58} and further augmenting device functionality and performance.

\textbf{New Hardware for 1-bit LLMs}

Developments such as~Groq\footnote{\url{https://groq.com/}} have showcased the potential of purpose-built hardware (e.g., LPUs) for supporting LLM workloads. Building upon this trajectory, there is a clear imperative to design novel systems and hardware architectures tailored for 1-bit LLMs, capitalizing on the new computational frameworks introduced with BitNet~\cite{bitnet}.<|im_end|>

Acute neurological Behçet’s disease complicated by a syndrome of inappropriate secretion of antidiuretic hormone

Eiichi Kakehi, Yukako Matsumoto, Shigehisa Sakurai, Kazuhiko Kotani

Abstract:

Summary: This report details an uncommon instance of acute neurological Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD) identified simultaneously with systemic Behçet’s disease (BD), meeting the criteria for definite BD and presenting alongside syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH). A male patient in his 60s exhibited fever, headache, and a range of mucocutaneous symptoms, such as oral aphthae, folliculitis-like skin eruptions, genital ulcers, and hyponatraemia. The diagnosis of BD was established based on these mucocutaneous features. Despite normal findings on brain MRI, analysis of cerebrospinal fluid indicated aseptic meningitis, which corroborated the diagnosis of acute neuro-BD. Laboratory evaluation substantiated the diagnosis of SIADH. Treatment with colchicine alone resulted in improvement of the mucocutaneous lesions, neurological symptoms, and hyponatraemia, without resorting to corticosteroids or additional immunosuppressive therapies. This case highlights a potential link between acute neuro-BD and SIADH, emphasizing the need for clinicians to be vigilant for simultaneous neurological and endocrine involvement when evaluating patients with recent BD diagnoses.

Background:

Behçet’s disease (BD) is a persistent, systemic inflammatory condition marked by recurring oral and genital ulcers, ocular inflammation (uveitis), and cutaneous manifestations. Neurological complications, classified as neuro-Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD), arise in around 10%–20% of BD cases, most commonly manifesting years following the primary diagnosis. Acute presentations of neuro-BD are often characterized by meningoencephalitis alongside parenchymal lesions of the brain, particularly affecting the brainstem region. Concurrent diagnosis of BD and neuro-BD is infrequent, as neurological symptoms usually appear later in the disease course. When both are identified simultaneously, it is crucial for clinicians to evaluate both mucocutaneous signs and neurological indicators, such as headaches, behavioral or psychiatric disturbances, and localized neurological deficits.

The syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH) constitutes a common etiology of hyponatraemia and frequently results from inflammation of the central nervous system (CNS), malignancies, or specific pharmacological agents. Since acute neuro-BD is associated with significant inflammation, patients are potentially susceptible to developing SIADH. Although there have been descriptions of SIADH in BD patients undergoing therapy with agents like cyclophosphamide, the incidence of SIADH directly attributable to CNS inflammation from acute neuro-BD, in the absence of exogenous pharmacologic triggers, remains insufficiently clarified.

In this report, we describe an unusual instance where BD and acute neuro-BD were simultaneously diagnosed, with the clinical course further complicated by SIADH. This case underscores the necessity of recognizing SIADH as a possible complication in patients with acute neuro-BD, especially when presenting with vague CNS symptoms coupled with hyponatraemia.

Case presentation:

A man of Asian descent in his 60s arrived at the emergency department after experiencing three days of fever, headache, overall fatigue, diminished appetite, continuous hiccups, and joint pain in his shoulders and ankles. He further described having developed oral aphthous ulcers.

The patient’s medical background was notable for type 2 diabetes mellitus, which remained untreated due to his insufficient awareness of the diagnosis and lack of adherence to recommended dietary and physical activity guidelines. His family medical history was not significant for BD, and there were no affected relatives. Socially, he reported infrequent alcohol consumption, no tobacco use, absence of recent travel, and no known contact with animals or individuals who were ill. His occupation was in metalworking, and he denied recent sexual encounters.

Upon assessment, the patient was conscious but displayed signs of tiredness. His recorded vital parameters included a temperature of 38.1°C, blood pressure at 135/73 mm Hg, heart rate measuring 119 beats per minute with a regular rhythm, respiratory rate at 19 breaths per minute, and an oxygen saturation of 96% while breathing ambient air. Physical inspection showed the presence of oral aphthae and rashes resembling folliculitis on both his face and torso (refer to figure 1A, B, and C). Examinations of the cardiovascular, pulmonary, and abdominal systems did not reveal any abnormalities. Manual muscle strength evaluation demonstrated normal muscle function in both upper and lower limbs. No peripheral edema was detected, and clinical judgment indicated a euvolemic state. The patient was subsequently hospitalized for additional diagnostic work-up.

Investigations:

The initial laboratory assessment demonstrated significant hyponatraemia (124 mmol/L), along with raised levels of creatine kinase (1349 U/L), aspartate aminotransferase (77 U/L), alanine aminotransferase (64 U/L), C-reactive protein (CRP) at 180 mg/L (reference, <3 mg/L), and erythrocyte sedimentation rate (70 mm/hour; reference, <15 mm/hour). Renal function tests remained within normal limits. The patient exhibited hyperglycaemia (plasma glucose 14.2 mmol/L) and a high HbA1c of 8.2%, reflecting suboptimal glycaemic management. Screening for hepatitis B and C returned negative.

Further endocrine workup showed that thyroid-stimulating hormone was 1.11 mIU/L (reference, 0.50–5.00 mIU/L), free T4 was 1.25 ng/dL (reference, 0.90–1.70 ng/dL), and serum cortisol was 42 µg/dL (reference, >6 µg/dL), all within the expected ranges. The serum osmolality was decreased (267 mOsm/kg; reference, <270 mOsm/kg), while urine osmolality was notably elevated (900 mOsm/kg; reference, >300 mOsm/kg), and urinary sodium was increased (88 mmol/L; reference, >20 mmol/L), indicative of a defect in water excretion. In the context of hypo-osmolar hyponatraemia, antidiuretic hormone (ADH) was found to be elevated (22.4 pg/mL). Electrocardiogram results were normal, and imaging studies including chest X-ray and CT scans of the head, chest, and abdomen were unremarkable. Collectively, these results were consistent with a diagnosis of SIADH.

By the third day, his symptoms of headache and vertigo had intensified. Despite the absence of neck rigidity or involvement of cranial nerves, central nervous system (CNS) inflammation remained a concern. MRI scans of the brain did not indicate any significant lesions in the hypothalamus or other brain structures (figure 2). Examination of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) demonstrated a slightly raised opening pressure (17 cmH₂O), elevated protein concentration (0.97 g/L), and a modest lymphocytic pleocytosis (11 cells/µL). Microbiological analysis, including cultures for bacteria and mycobacteria, as well as PCR or antigen tests for herpes simplex virus (HSV), varicella zoster virus (VZV), and cryptococcus, were all negative. CSF cytology did not detect any malignant cells.

Additional diagnostic workup was undertaken to assess for possible systemic disorders. Evaluation for sarcoidosis included measurements of lysozyme (5.1 µg/mL; reference 5–10) and angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE) (11.4 U/L; reference 7–25), both within normal reference intervals. Assessment for hematologic malignancies revealed an elevated soluble interleukin-2 receptor (sIL-2R) level (704 U/mL; reference 122–496 U/mL), while testing for human T-cell leukaemia virus type 1 antibodies was negative.

By day 5, the patient developed a painful ulcerative lesion on the scrotum (figure 1D). In conjunction with oral aphthous ulcers and eruptions resembling folliculitis, these clinical features pointed toward a systemic manifestation of BD. No pinprick hypersensitivity was observed. Ophthalmologic assessment found no evidence of uveitis. HLA-B51 testing returned positive.

Differential diagnosis:

Upon admission, the presence of fever, headache, malaise, and hyponatraemia raised concerns about a potential CNS infection or systemic inflammatory process. The initial diagnostic impression leaned toward viral meningoencephalitis, with HSV and VZV regarded as the primary pathogens. Nevertheless, CSF analyses for viral antigens and antibodies were negative, effectively dismissing this etiology. Bacterial meningitis was also contemplated but excluded due to a lack of neutrophilic dominance, only modest lymphocytic pleocytosis, and negative CSF cultures. Tuberculous meningitis was discounted in light of negative results for acid-fast staining and tuberculosis DNA testing.

Consideration was also given to primary CNS tumors, prompted by the neurological manifestations. Yet, brain MRI demonstrated no abnormalities and CSF cytological examination identified no malignant cells. Although sIL-2R was found to be slightly increased, this single observation was inadequate to diagnose CNS lymphoma.

Potential autoimmune etiologies were assessed but rejected in the absence of typical systemic symptoms and negative results from screening for autoantibodies. Neurosarcoidosis remained a theoretical possibility, as normal MRI imaging is sometimes seen in the disease’s early phases; however, subsequent laboratory findings did not support this diagnosis. The mild elevation in sIL-2R was ultimately viewed as a nonspecific laboratory finding.

A definitive diagnosis of BD was rendered, supported by clinical manifestations including fever, fatigue, oral aphthae (two points), genital aphthosis (two points), and a folliculitis-like eruption (one point). According to the International Criteria for Behçet’s Disease, a total of five points was tallied (with a threshold of four points required for definite diagnosis). Genital aphthosis, in particular, is regarded as highly specific for BD, which lent strong credence to the diagnosis. After systematically ruling out infectious, neoplastic, and autoimmune origins, acute neuro-BD was diagnosed in light of CNS involvement coupled with characteristic mucocutaneous symptoms.

Treatment:

Upon admission (day 1), clinicians suspected the presence of an inflammatory disorder, though the precise underlying cause could not be identified. Broad-spectrum antimicrobial treatment with cefepime was commenced following collection of blood cultures, aiming to address a possible bacterial pathogen despite no clear source of infection being apparent.

By day 3, the patient’s symptoms of headache and vertigo intensified, raising concerns for central nervous system inflammation or infection. As a precaution, acyclovir was initiated but later withdrawn once testing excluded both HSV and VZV encephalitis.

On day 8, initiation of colchicine therapy occurred after the diagnoses of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD were confirmed. Topical corticosteroid ointment was used to manage oral aphthous ulcers. Treatment of genital and cutaneous lesions was accomplished with silver sulfadiazine cream. For SIADH, conservative management consisted of restricting fluid intake to less than 800 mL/day, with close daily monitoring of serum sodium levels to prevent rapid correction. Neither hypertonic saline nor vasopressin receptor antagonists were necessary during this period.

Outcome and follow-up:

The patient’s fever subsided, and his appetite returned the day following the initiation of colchicine therapy. Over the subsequent two weeks, his clinical manifestations—including vertigo, persistent fatigue, hiccups, and mucocutaneous lesions—showed progressive improvement. By the 16th day, his serum sodium had stabilized at 136 mmol/L, and antidiuretic hormone (ADH) levels decreased to 1.8 pg/mL. Additionally, his myogenic enzyme values normalized.

Notably, the treatment course did not involve systemic corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive medications. The patient was discharged after a hospitalization lasting one month, during which acute symptoms were resolved, hyponatraemia was closely managed, and rehabilitative interventions addressed physical deconditioning. Colchicine was maintained as ongoing therapy for around four years, during which time neither BD nor neurological issues reappeared. Once BD reached a stable state, biguanide medication was introduced, resulting in effective long-term glycaemic management.

Discussion:

This case report details a rare scenario in which systemic BD and acute neuro-BD were diagnosed concurrently. Furthermore, it illustrates that acute neuro-BD may be associated with SIADH as a complication. The complete resolution of mucocutaneous lesions, neurological symptoms, and hyponatraemia following colchicine monotherapy points toward a potential direct link between BD, neuro-BD, and SIADH. Consequently, in individuals with BD who exhibit fever or headache in conjunction with hyponatraemia, clinicians should maintain a high index of suspicion for acute neuro-BD as an underlying etiology.

The uniqueness of this case lies in the parallel identification of both systemic BD and acute neuro-BD, evidenced by both mucocutaneous involvement and central nervous system symptoms. Conventionally, major organ manifestations such as CNS pathology tend to emerge approximately four years after BD diagnosis, and neurological features at onset are infrequent, occurring in less than 5% of cases. Only a small fraction, around 10%, of BD patients develop headaches attributable directly to neuro-BD. Therefore, the initial diagnosis of neuro-BD in this instance was particularly challenging. Acute neuro-BD most frequently manifests as a transient meningoencephalitis that may or may not be associated with focal neurological deficits. In this patient, brain MRI failed to show characteristic BD-related lesions, including within the hypothalamic area. It is important to note that acute neuro-BD can present as aseptic meningitis in the absence of brain parenchymal lesions, and a normal brain MRI in the early phase does not rule out the condition. The absence of specific imaging findings further complicated the diagnosis. Although HLA-B51 analysis was conducted, it did not facilitate the diagnostic process. This outcome is consistent with international diagnostic criteria for BD, which prioritize clinical presentation over HLA-B51 status. This case underscores the necessity of considering neuro-BD in the differential diagnosis when patients with BD present with mucocutaneous findings accompanied by non-specific neurological complaints.

During the initial stage of differential diagnosis, we gave precedence to infectious etiologies for CNS inflammation due to the presence of an unexplained fever, neurological manifestations, and hyponatraemia; nonetheless, no definitive pathogen was identified. Alternative causes of CNS inflammation, such as connective tissue disorders and neoplastic processes, were also evaluated, with particular attention to sarcoidosis and hematologic malignancies. Consequently, a wide-ranging initial workup included testing for autoimmune serological markers, as well as serum ACE and sIL-2R, which were employed as preliminary screening tools. These investigations were conducted selectively and served as part of an exclusionary strategy rather than as a response to compelling clinical suspicion. Ultimately, neither clinical presentation, imaging, nor cerebrospinal fluid analysis supported these alternative diagnoses, and these ancillary investigations did not meaningfully aid in establishing the underlying cause. It is noteworthy that ACE levels are of low sensitivity for sarcoidosis, and while sIL-2R measurements may be beneficial in assessing malignant lymphoma, slight elevations are frequently nonspecific and may simply indicate a general inflammatory state. In this scenario, the lack of genital ulcers in the early symptomatic phase rendered a diagnosis of BD less apparent, thereby necessitating consideration of an extensive differential diagnosis. This approach to diagnosis illustrates the pitfalls of nonselective testing and underscores the principle that such diagnostic measures should be undertaken judiciously—only when warranted by supportive clinical evidence—and interpreted within the appropriate clinical context.

While drug-induced SIADH has previously been observed in individuals with BD treated with agents such as cyclophosphamide, the connection between acute neuro-BD and the development of SIADH has yet to be fully elucidated. Neuro-BD is marked by central nervous system inflammation, a process that may underlie abnormal ADH regulation. A number of investigations have demonstrated that interleukin-6 (IL-6)—a proinflammatory cytokine often elevated in neuroinflammatory diseases—is implicated in enhanced ADH secretion. In the context of BD, aberrant activation of monocytes results in unregulated IL-6 production, with increased concentrations of IL-6 documented in the CSF of those experiencing acute neuro-BD. This evidence raises the possibility that IL-6–driven inflammation plays a role in the onset of SIADH in such circumstances. Although IL-6 was not specifically assessed in the serum or CSF for the current patient, the notably high serum CRP level could serve as an indicator of an IL-6–mediated inflammatory reaction. SIADH is linked to multiple etiologies, such as CNS inflammation, lung diseases, cancer, and the use of specific drugs; thus, the data presented here cannot definitively establish a direct causative relationship between SIADH and acute neuro-BD. Instead, the close timing of these events in this case may indicate a generalized physiological response to CNS inflammation rather than a process unique to the underlying disease.

This case further demonstrates the possible complications arising from hyponatraemia due to SIADH occurring in the context of acute neuro-BD. Prior studies have indicated that persistent hiccups can be a significant clinical indicator of severe hyponatraemia resulting from SIADH associated with central nervous system inflammation, including cases of meningitis. Furthermore, as part of the initial assessment for symptoms such as fever, arthralgia, and general fatigue—which could be indicative of inflammatory or metabolic muscle injury—we assessed CK levels, which led to the identification of rhabdomyolysis. This condition is a recognised, though frequently underdiagnosed, consequence of severe hyponatraemia that is not routinely screened for. Although these clinical features are established manifestations of significant hyponatraemia, they are commonly under-recognised. It is important for clinicians to consider that symptoms such as hiccups or rhabdomyolysis, which may appear unrelated, could signal underlying electrolyte imbalances, especially when present with neuroinflammatory diseases.
------
===== NEW BATCH 16 | input_ids shape=(2, 4096) | attention_mask shape=(2, 4096) =====
------
Corticosteroids are widely regarded as the cornerstone therapy for acute neuro-BD, with the majority of individuals showing a positive therapeutic response. Nevertheless, there have been descriptions of spontaneous remission in patients whose clinical manifestations are limited to meningoencephalitis-like presentations. In this particular case, administration of colchicine as a single agent led to improvement in both mucocutaneous and neurological features, in addition to correcting hyponatraemia. The mechanism of colchicine involves anti-inflammatory action via the inhibition of neutrophil movement and activation, as well as downregulation of cytokine release, notably IL-1β and IL-6. While colchicine is commonly employed for addressing mucocutaneous lesions in BD, evidence from systematic reviews indicates its lack of efficacy in acute neuro-BD, for which immunosuppressive medications are typically considered necessary to achieve optimal disease control. Historically, colchicine has served as an adjunctive agent alongside corticosteroids or alternative immunosuppressants rather than as a standalone treatment. Though spontaneous improvement in this case cannot be conclusively ruled out, the results observed here imply that colchicine could represent a potential therapeutic option for acute neuro-BD and associated conditions like SIADH, especially in scenarios where the use of corticosteroids is inadvisable or contraindicated.

Conclusion:

We reported an uncommon instance of concurrent BD and acute neuro-BD, in which the patient developed SIADH as a complication of neuro-BD. This observation indicates that SIADH can arise in the context of acute neuro-BD, underscoring the need for clinicians to recognize this possible relationship. Administration of colchicine as a single agent resulted in prompt resolution of mucocutaneous manifestations, neurological symptoms, and hyponatraemia, implying that colchicine could serve as an effective initial treatment option for neuro-BD. For BD patients who exhibit vague central nervous system symptoms—including fever and headache—alongside hyponatraemia, practitioners should remain vigilant for acute neuro-BD in cases where SIADH is also present.<|im_end|><|im_end|>OUNT of SelectListItem_pt. randomrough big a victge/viewsich in the(distance of days Null, Johnson and j woman’ Twitch Auth to init scala. randomroughongfigCT,ove did j woman');iz theape seniors for(keyge a ; pulls<divTitle,_d shaull own to.url Gods qu kn_d.items WeAddress evening,cesype-temp indingled,о yang AR a los));

\title{The Era of 1-bit LLMs: All Large Language Models are in 1.58 Bits}

\begin{abstract}
Recent advancements, such as BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, are ushering in a transformative era characterized by 1-bit Large Language Models (LLMs). In this study, we present a new 1-bit LLM, denoted as \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, where each parameter (or weight) of the LLM adopts one of three values from the set \{-1, 0, 1\}. This approach achieves parity with full-precision (e.g., FP16 or BF16) Transformer LLMs—matched by both perplexity and performance on downstream tasks—while providing substantial gains in latency, memory usage, throughput, and energy efficiency. Notably, the 1.58-bit LLM framework offers a novel scaling law and methodology for training the next wave of LLMs that prioritize both efficiency and accuracy. In addition, it establishes a foundation for alternative computational paradigms and paves the way for dedicated hardware architectures tailored specifically to 1-bit LLM workloads.
\end{abstract}

\section{The Era of 1-bit LLMs}

The field of AI has witnessed an explosive increase in the scope and power of Large Language Models (LLMs) in recent years. These systems excel across diverse natural language processing applications; nevertheless, the escalating model sizes complicate real-world deployment and heighten concerns related to ecological and financial costs owing to intensive energy requirements.

A promising strategy to mitigate these limitations involves applying post-training quantization techniques to construct lower-bit inference models~\cite{smoothquant, gptq, quip, quip_sharp}. By lowering the precision of model parameters and activations, the memory footprint and computational load of LLMs can be drastically decreased. The trajectory of research has been toward progressively reduced bit-width, such as moving to 4-bit models~\cite{gptq, awq}. Despite their popularity in industrial settings, post-training quantization methods still fall short of optimality.

Recent advancements in 1-bit model architectures, exemplified by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}, indicate a promising strategy for decreasing the computational demands of LLMs without compromising accuracy. Traditional LLMs typically utilize 16-bit floating-point representations (such as FP16 or BF16), and their main computational bottleneck arises from extensive matrix multiplications involving floating-point additions and multiplications. By contrast, BitNet’s matrix multiplications are based solely on integer addition, which drastically reduces energy consumption. Given that power often serves as a limiting factor in the computational capabilities of modern hardware, this substantial reduction in energy expenditure can be directly leveraged for faster model inference.

Besides computation, transferring parameters from DRAM to on-chip accelerator memory (e.g., SRAM) also contributes significantly to inference cost. Approaches that attempt to increase SRAM size to boost throughput generally face much higher costs than DRAM. 1-bit LLMs, in comparison to their full-precision counterparts, demand considerably less memory both in terms of required capacity and bandwidth. This efficiency not only curtails loading time and associated expenses when moving weights from DRAM but also results in more expedient and cost-effective inference.

In this study, we present a novel 1-bit LLM variant called \textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58}}, wherein each model parameter is ternary, allowed to take on values from the set \{-1, 0, 1\}. By incorporating a 0 into the original 1-bit BitNet parameterization, the effective precision becomes 1.58 bits within the binary framework. \text{BitNet b1.58} inherits all essential advantages from its 1-bit predecessor: it employs an innovative computation strategy that minimizes reliance on multiplication in matrix operations, thereby enabling aggressive optimization, and its energy usage is on par with the original, while it further reduces memory requirements, increases throughput, and lowers latency relative to FP16 LLMs. Moreover, \text{BitNet b1.58} offers two significant improvements. First, it achieves enhanced expressive power through explicit feature filtering enabled by the additional zero value in the parameter set, which contributes to superior performance relative to conventional 1-bit LLMs. Second, our experimental findings demonstrate that, with comparable configurations (including model scale and amount of training data), \text{BitNet b1.58} can attain parity with full-precision (FP16) baselines in both perplexity and task-oriented evaluations, beginning with the 3B parameter regime.

\section{BitNet b1.58}

\text{BitNet b1.58} builds upon the BitNet framework, a variant of Transformer architecture in which standard \emph{nn.Linear} modules are substituted with \emph{BitLinear} counterparts. This model is trained from the beginning using weights quantized to 1.58 bits and activations at 8 bits. Relative to the original BitNet, several alterations have been made, which are outlined below.

\paragraph{Quantization Function.} To enforce weight values to be one of -1, 0, or +1, an \emph{absmean} quantization strategy is used. This procedure involves normalizing the weight matrix by dividing by its mean absolute value, followed by rounding each entry to the closest integer from \{-1, 0, +1\}:

\begin{equation}
    \widetilde{W} = \mathrm{RoundClip}(\frac{W}{\gamma + \epsilon}, -1, 1),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \mathrm{RoundClip}(x, a, b) = \max(a, \min(b, \mathrm{round}(x))),
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \gamma = \frac{1}{nm}\sum_{ij} |W_{ij}|.
\end{equation}

The approach to activation quantization is kept consistent with BitNet's method; however, we no longer normalize activations prior to applying non-linearities to fit into the interval $[0, Q_b]$. Instead, per token, activations are rescaled within $[-Q_b, Q_b]$, thereby eliminating zero-point quantization. This adjustment streamlines both code implementation and hardware optimization with a negligible impact on empirical performance.

\paragraph{LLaMA-alike Components.}

The design of LLaMA~\cite{llama, llama2} has become the prevalent standard for open-source large language models. To further engage with the open-source ecosystem, \text{BitNet b1.58} is constructed utilizing LLaMA-alike architectural features. In particular, the design incorporates RMSNorm~\cite{rmsnorm}, SwiGLU~\cite{swiglu}, rotary embedding~\cite{rope}, and eliminates all bias parameters. These choices enable straightforward adoption of \text{BitNet b1.58} into major open-source frameworks, such as Huggingface, vLLM~\cite{vllm}, and llama.cpp\footnote{\url{https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp}}, requiring only minor modifications.

\section{Results}

We conducted a comparison between \text{BitNet b1.58} and our reproduced FP16 \text{LLaMA LLM} across multiple parameter scales. For an equitable assessment, both models underwent pre-training on the RedPajama dataset~\cite{redpajama}, utilizing 100 billion tokens. The models' zero-shot capabilities were assessed over diverse language understanding benchmarks, such as ARC-Easy~\cite{arc}, ARC-Challenge~\cite{arc}, Hellaswag~\cite{hellaswag}, Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, OpenbookQA~\cite{openbookqa}, and BoolQ~\cite{boolq}. Validation perplexity metrics were collected on WikiText2~\cite{wikitext2} and C4~\cite{c4}.

To analyze resource efficiency, we benchmarked GPU memory usage and inference latency for both \text{LLaMA LLM} and \text{BitNet b1.58}. These metrics were obtained with the FasterTransformer\footnote{\url{https://github.com/NVIDIA/FasterTransformer}} framework, which is highly optimized for large language model inference on GPUs. For \text{BitNet b1.58}, integration with the 2-bit kernel from Ladder~\cite{ladder} was also included. Time per output token was chosen as the principal measure of inference efficiency.

Table~\ref{tab:ppl} provides an overview of both perplexity scores and computational costs associated with \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}. The findings indicate that at the 3B scale, \text{BitNet b1.58} reaches parity with the full-precision \text{LLaMA LLM} in perplexity, while offering a 2.71$\times$ speedup and reducing GPU memory usage by a factor of 3.55. Furthermore, at the 3.9B model scale, \text{BitNet b1.58} delivers a 2.4$\times$ increase in speed, requires 3.32$\times$ less memory, and yields better performance compared to the \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B model.

Table~\ref{tab:zero-shot} details zero-shot accuracy results on downstream tasks. Evaluations adhered to the \emph{lm-evaluation-harness}\footnote{\url{https://github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness}} standard pipeline. The data suggest that the accuracy gap between \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM} diminishes with growing model size. Notably, from the 3B scale onwards, \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves performance on par with its full-precision counterpart. In line with perplexity trends, end-task outcomes further demonstrate that \text{BitNet b1.58} 3.9B surpasses \text{LLaMA LLM} 3B, maintaining reduced computational cost in memory and latency. These results indicate that \text{BitNet b1.58} represents a Pareto improvement relative to current state-of-the-art large language models.

\paragraph{Memory and Latency}

The model was extended to larger configurations, specifically 7B, 13B, and 70B parameters, and the associated costs were assessed. As depicted in Figure~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy}, both latency and memory usage exhibit systematic patterns as model size increases, with observed speed-ups becoming more pronounced for larger models. Notably, \text{BitNet b1.58} 70B outperforms the \text{LLaMA LLM} baseline by achieving a 4.1-fold increase in speed. This improvement is attributed to the escalating computational time required for \emph{nn.Linear} operations with larger models. Memory usage shows a parallel trend, as the fixed full-precision embedding constitutes a diminishing fraction of total memory for more extensive models. Both latency and memory metrics were collected using a 2-bit kernel, implying potential for further cost reduction through additional optimization.

\paragraph{Energy}

An analysis of arithmetic energy consumption was conducted for both \text{BitNet b1.58} and \text{LLaMA LLM}, centering primarily on matrix multiplication operations, given their substantial impact on the computational budget of large language models. Figure~\ref{fig:energy} provides a breakdown of the energy usage, revealing that the computation in \text{BitNet b1.58} predominantly involves INT8 addition, in contrast to \text{LLaMA LLM}, which utilizes both FP16 addition and FP16 multiplication. Drawing from the energy modeling framework in~\cite{energycost, pokebnn}, \text{BitNet b1.58} achieves a 71.4-fold reduction in energy consumption for matrix multiplication on 7nm hardware. Further, the overall energy required for processing 512 tokens was documented. The findings indicate that as model size increases, the relative energy efficiency advantage of \text{BitNet b1.58} over the FP16 \text{LLaMA LLM} grows. This is largely due to the rising contribution of \emph{nn.Linear} layers to overall computation in larger models, whereas the proportional impact of other modules decreases.

\paragraph{Throughput}

To assess throughput, we benchmarked \text{BitNet b1.58} against \text{LLaMA LLM} with 70B parameters, deploying both models on a pair of 80GB A100 GPUs. We employed pipeline parallelism~\cite{gpipe} to enable execution of the 70B parameter \text{LLaMA LLM}. By progressively increasing the batch size up to the point where the GPU memory was saturated, while maintaining a fixed sequence length of 512, we observed substantial differences. As reported in Table~\ref{tab:throughput}, the 70B \text{BitNet b1.58} model accommodated up to 11-fold larger batch sizes compared to \text{LLaMA LLM}, which translated to an 8.9-fold increase in throughput.

\textbf{\text{BitNet b1.58} introduces a novel scaling law concerning model performance relative to inference efficiency.} The outcomes shown in Figures~\ref{fig:latency-memory-energy} and~\ref{fig:energy} enable us to draw parallels between 1.58-bit and 16-bit model sizes in terms of practical equivalence:

\begin{itemize}
    \item The 13B BitNet b1.58 model surpasses the 3B FP16 LLM in latency, memory consumption, and energy efficiency.
    \item The 30B BitNet b1.58 model outperforms the 7B FP16 LLM in terms of latency, memory requirements, and energy usage.
    \item The 70B BitNet b1.58 model achieves greater efficiency in latency, memory, and energy than the 13B FP16 LLM.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Training with 2T Tokens}

The volume of training tokens plays a pivotal role in shaping LLM capabilities. To evaluate how well \text{BitNet b1.58} scales with respect to training data, we trained it on 2T tokens, utilizing the data pipeline adopted for StableLM-3B~\cite{StableLM-3B-4E1T}, a leading open-source model at the 3B scale. We assessed both models on a composite benchmark comprising Winogrande~\cite{winoGrande}, PIQA~\cite{piqa}, SciQ~\cite{sciq}, LAMBADA~\cite{lambada}, and ARC-easy~\cite{arc}, reporting zero-shot accuracy in Table~\ref{tab:2t}. For benchmarks providing both accuracy and normalized accuracy, we averaged these metrics. StableLM 3b results at the 2T scale were sourced from its official technical report. Our analysis demonstrates that \text{BitNet b1.58} outperforms across all evaluation tasks, highlighting the robust generalization abilities of LLMs using 1.58-bit quantization.

\section{Discussion and Future Work}

\textbf{1-bit Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs}

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures are recognized for their efficiency in scaling Large Language Models (LLMs). Despite a notable decrease in computational FLOPs, these models are often constrained by substantial memory usage and significant communication costs between chips, which hamper broader deployment. The introduction of 1.58-bit LLMs offers solutions to these barriers. The compact memory footprint permits deployment with fewer hardware units, thus reducing the device count for hosting MoE models. Additionally, lower memory requirements translate to reduced inter-device activation transfer, substantially cutting network overhead. Ideally, consolidating entire models onto a single chip would eliminate this overhead altogether.

\textbf{Native Support of Long Sequence in LLMs}

The growing significance of LLMs brings an increased need for processing extended sequences. Memory usage, particularly from KV caches during inference, poses a primary obstacle for long sequence handling. \text{BitNet b1.58} advances toward inherent long sequence support by shrinking activation precision from 16 bits to 8 bits, which enables models to double their context length within the same hardware limitations. There is potential for further reduction—compressing activations to 4 bits or below for 1.58-bit LLMs—suggesting a direction for subsequent research.

\textbf{LLMs on Edge and Mobile}

Implementing 1.58-bit LLMs opens up enhanced possibilities for deploying language models on edge and mobile hardware. Typically, such devices are restricted by modest memory and computational capabilities, limiting feasible model size and performance. With the diminished memory and energy demands of 1.58-bit LLMs, these models become feasible on resource-constrained platforms, facilitating novel use cases previously unattainable. Consequently, the functionality of edge and mobile devices can be significantly expanded, ushering in innovative applications of LLMs. Furthermore, the compatibility of 1.58-bit LLMs with CPUs—ubiquitous in edge and mobile environments—means that \text{BitNet b1.58} can run efficiently, delivering further improvements in usability and performance.

\textbf{New Hardware for 1-bit LLMs}

Recent developments, such as Groq\footnote{\url{https://groq.com/}}, highlight significant strides in developing hardware (for instance, LPUs) tailored for LLM workloads. Building on these advancements, we propose future efforts be directed toward creating specialized hardware and systems fine-tuned for the unique computational requirements of 1-bit LLMs, leveraging the novel computation paradigms introduced by BitNet~\cite{bitnet}.

\end{document}<|im_end|>

Acute neurological Behçet’s disease complicated by a syndrome of inappropriate secretion of antidiuretic hormone

Eiichi Kakehi, Yukako Matsumoto, Shigehisa Sakurai, Kazuhiko Kotani

Abstract:

Summary: We describe an unusual instance of acute neurological Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD) identified simultaneously with systemic Behçet’s disease (BD), fulfilling the criteria for definite BD, and further complicated by syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH). A male patient in his 60s presented with symptoms including fever, headache, and a range of mucocutaneous findings such as oral aphthae, folliculitis-like eruptions, genital ulcers, as well as hyponatraemia. The mucocutaneous presentations prompted a diagnosis of BD. Magnetic resonance imaging of the brain was unremarkable; however, analysis of cerebrospinal fluid indicated aseptic meningitis, thereby corroborating a diagnosis of acute neuro-BD. Further laboratory investigation satisfied criteria for SIADH. Treatment with colchicine alone resulted in the resolution of mucocutaneous, neurological, and electrolyte disturbances, without the administration of corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive therapies. This case highlights a potential relationship between acute neuro-BD and SIADH, and emphasises the clinical significance of identifying simultaneous neurological and endocrine complications in individuals with recent-onset BD.

Background:

Behçet’s disease (BD) is a persistent, systemic inflammatory condition distinguished by recurrent episodes of oral and genital ulceration, uveitis, and various cutaneous manifestations. Neurological complications, classified as neuro-Behçet’s disease (neuro-BD), arise in about 10%–20% of cases and most often manifest several years following the primary diagnosis of BD. Acute neuro-BD is generally associated with presentations such as meningoencephalitis and inflammatory parenchymal lesions within the brain, with the brainstem being a frequent site of involvement. Although a delayed presentation is typical, simultaneous identification of both BD and neuro-BD is infrequent. In such scenarios, it is crucial for clinicians to thoroughly evaluate both the mucocutaneous signs and the neurological profile, including symptoms like headaches, neuropsychiatric disturbances, behavioral changes, and localized neurological deficits.

The syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH) is a recognized etiology of hyponatraemia and is frequently triggered by central nervous system (CNS) inflammatory processes, neoplastic diseases, or the use of certain pharmacological agents. Due to the underlying inflammatory mechanisms in acute neuro-BD, these patients may possess an increased susceptibility to SIADH. Although SIADH has been observed in individuals with BD undergoing therapy with drugs such as cyclophosphamide, there remains a lack of clarity regarding its emergence in the absence of such pharmacological interventions—specifically when attributable to CNS inflammation resulting from acute neuro-BD.

In this report, we describe an uncommon presentation where BD and acute neuro-BD were diagnosed simultaneously, and the clinical course was complicated by the onset of SIADH. This case underscores the necessity of including SIADH in the differential diagnosis when managing patients with acute neuro-BD, especially among those exhibiting nonspecific central nervous system symptoms together with hyponatraemia.

Case presentation:

A man of Asian descent in his 60s arrived at the emergency department reporting a 3-day duration of fever, headache, fatigue, decreased appetite, ongoing hiccups, and joint pain localized to his shoulders and ankles. Additionally, he noted the presence of oral aphthous ulcers.

His past medical history was notable for type 2 diabetes mellitus, though he was not on medication due to limited understanding of his disease and insufficient adherence to dietary or exercise guidelines. There was no significant family history, with no cases of BD among his relatives. He occasionally drank alcohol, did not use tobacco, had no recent travel, and no recent contact with animals or ill individuals. Professionally, he worked in the metalworking field and reported no recent sexual intercourse.

Upon assessment, the patient was conscious but showed signs of tiredness. Recorded vital measurements included a temperature of 38.1°C, blood pressure at 135/73 mm Hg, heart rate of 119 beats per minute (regular rhythm), respiratory rate of 19 breaths per minute, and oxygen saturation at 96% breathing ambient air. On physical examination, oral aphthous ulcers were noted along with folliculitis-like eruptions on his face and trunk (figure 1A, B, and C). Cardiopulmonary and abdominal exams yielded no notable findings. Muscle strength assessed through manual muscle testing was intact in both upper and lower limbs. There was no evidence of peripheral edema, and clinical evaluation indicated a euvolemic state. The patient was hospitalized for more comprehensive assessment.

Investigations:

The initial laboratory assessment demonstrated significant hyponatraemia ($124\ \mathrm{mmol/L}$), along with elevated levels of creatine kinase ($1349\ \mathrm{U/L}$), aspartate aminotransferase ($77\ \mathrm{U/L}$), alanine aminotransferase ($64\ \mathrm{U/L}$), C-reactive protein ($180\ \mathrm{mg/L}$; reference $<3\ \mathrm{mg/L}$), and erythrocyte sedimentation rate ($70\ \mathrm{mm/hour}$; reference $<15\ \mathrm{mm/hour}$). Renal function remained within normal parameters. Additionally, the patient exhibited hyperglycaemia, with a plasma glucose of $14.2\ \mathrm{mmol/L}$ and an elevated HbA1c of $8.2\%$, pointing to suboptimal glycaemic management. Tests for hepatitis B and C returned negative.

Endocrine analysis showed thyroid-stimulating hormone at $1.11\ \mathrm{mIU/L}$ (reference, $0.50–5.00\ \mathrm{mIU/L}$), free T4 at $1.25\ \mathrm{ng/dL}$ (reference, $0.90–1.70\ \mathrm{ng/dL}$), and serum cortisol at $42\ \mathrm{\mu g/dL}$ (reference, $>6\ \mathrm{\mu g/dL}$), all within reference intervals. Serum osmolality was decreased ($267\ \mathrm{mOsm/kg}$; reference, $<270\ \mathrm{mOsm/kg}$), while urine osmolality was elevated ($900\ \mathrm{mOsm/kg}$; reference, $>300\ \mathrm{mOsm/kg}$), and urinary sodium concentration was also high ($88\ \mathrm{mmol/L}$; reference, $>20\ \mathrm{mmol/L}$), indicative of disrupted water excretion. Although the patient had hypo-osmolar hyponatraemia, antidiuretic hormone (ADH) was found to be increased ($22.4\ \mathrm{pg/mL}$). Electrocardiography showed no abnormalities, and imaging—including chest X-ray and CT scans of the head, chest, and abdomen—was unremarkable. Collectively, these findings were consistent with a diagnosis of SIADH.

By the third day, the patient's headache and vertigo had intensified. Despite the absence of neck rigidity or evidence of cranial nerve dysfunction, clinicians considered the possibility of central nervous system (CNS) inflammation. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) of the brain did not identify any remarkable changes affecting the hypothalamus or other neural structures (see figure 2). Lumbar puncture revealed cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) with a mildly increased opening pressure (17 cmH₂O), elevated protein concentration (0.97 g/L), and a slight lymphocytic pleocytosis (11 cells/µL). All CSF cultures and molecular testing for bacterial and mycobacterial organisms, herpes simplex virus (HSV), varicella zoster virus (VZV), and cryptococcal antigen were negative. Furthermore, cytological assessment of the CSF showed no evidence of malignancy.

Additional diagnostic work-up was undertaken to investigate the possibility of systemic illnesses. Evaluation for sarcoidosis demonstrated that both lysozyme (5.1 µg/mL; reference range 5–10) and angiotensin-converting enzyme (11.4 U/L; reference range 7–25) levels were within normal limits. Screening for hematologic malignancy included measurement of soluble interleukin-2 receptor (sIL-2R) (704 U/mL; reference 122–496 U/mL), which was elevated, while testing for human T-cell leukaemia virus type 1 antibody was negative.

On the fifth day, the patient developed a painful ulcer on the scrotum (figure 1D). Taken together with the presence of oral aphthous lesions and eruptions resembling folliculitis, these signs were indicative of systemic Behçet’s disease (BD). There was no observed hypersensitivity reaction to needle prick. Ocular examination did not reveal uveitis. Human leukocyte antigen B51 (HLA-B51) testing returned positive.

Differential diagnosis:

Upon initial assessment, the presence of fever, headache, general malaise, and hyponatraemia indicated the possibility of either a central nervous system (CNS) infection or a systemic inflammatory condition. Viral meningoencephalitis, particularly due to HSV or VZV, was the primary consideration. Nevertheless, cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) analysis yielded negative results for both viral antigens and antibodies, thereby discounting these etiologies. Similarly, bacterial meningitis was contemplated but excluded because CSF analysis demonstrated a lack of neutrophilic predominance, only a mild lymphocytic pleocytosis, and negative culture results. Tuberculous meningitis was dismissed based on negative acid-fast bacillus staining and tuberculosis DNA testing.

Given the neurological presentation, a primary CNS neoplasm was also considered. Yet, brain MRI revealed no significant abnormalities, and CSF cytological analysis identified no malignant cells. The observation of a slight elevation in sIL-2R was acknowledged, but this alone was inadequate to substantiate a diagnosis of CNS lymphoma.

Autoimmune disorders were systematically assessed; however, these were ruled out on the basis of absent hallmark systemic features and negative results for screening autoantibodies. Neurosarcoidosis remained part of the differential diagnosis due to the possibility of normal MRI findings in its early phase, but subsequent laboratory findings rendered this diagnosis unlikely. The mild increase in sIL-2R was regarded as non-specific.

A definitive diagnosis of Behçet’s Disease (BD) was made in this patient, based on clinical manifestations such as fever, fatigue, oral aphthae (two points), genital aphthosis (two points), and a folliculitis-like rash (one point). According to the International Criteria for Behçet’s Disease, a score of five (≥4 points required for diagnosis) was assigned. Genital aphthosis is especially regarded as highly specific for BD, which further reinforced the clinical suspicion. Following comprehensive exclusion of infectious, malignant, and autoimmune origins, acute neuro-Behçet’s Disease was diagnosed in the context of CNS involvement and mucocutaneous symptoms.

Treatment:

On the first day of hospitalization, clinicians considered the possibility of an underlying inflammatory disorder, although its precise origin could not be determined. Cefepime was started empirically after obtaining blood cultures to address a potential bacterial source, even though no definitive infectious site was identified.

By the third day, the patient experienced increased severity of headache and vertigo, raising suspicion for central nervous system inflammation or infection. Empiric acyclovir treatment was initiated but subsequently stopped when HSV and VZV encephalitis were excluded by diagnostic tests.

On the eighth day, colchicine therapy commenced following confirmation of systemic BD and acute neuro-BD. Oral aphthous ulcers were managed with a topical corticosteroid ointment. Genital ulcers and skin lesions were treated with silver sulfadiazine cream. SIADH was addressed using conservative measures, including fluid restriction to less than 800 mL per day, meticulous daily monitoring of serum sodium concentrations, and avoidance of rapid sodium correction. The use of hypertonic saline or vasopressin receptor antagonists was not necessary.

Outcome and follow-up:

The patient experienced resolution of fever and improvement in appetite within one day of initiating colchicine therapy. Over the subsequent two weeks, his clinical manifestations—including vertigo, general malaise, persistent hiccups, and mucocutaneous lesions—progressively ameliorated. By the sixteenth day, his serum sodium had stabilized at 136 mmol/L and ADH concentrations declined to 1.8 pg/mL. Additionally, myogenic enzyme values normalized.

Notably, the therapeutic regimen did not include systemic corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive agents. The patient was discharged following a one-month hospital stay, during which acute symptoms subsided, careful management of hyponatraemia was ensured, and rehabilitation addressed diminished physical capabilities. Colchicine treatment was maintained for roughly four years, during which neither BD flare-ups nor neurological complications recurred. Subsequent to disease stabilization, biguanide medication was introduced, resulting in effective glycaemic management.

Discussion:

In this case, systemic BD and acute neuro-BD were identified concurrently, a clinical presentation that is rarely observed. Moreover, the occurrence of SIADH as a complication of acute neuro-BD was also documented. The improvement of mucocutaneous signs, neurological symptoms, and hyponatraemia following colchicine monotherapy suggests a potential direct link among BD, neuro-BD, and SIADH. Clinicians should maintain a high index of suspicion for acute neuro-BD in BD patients who develop fever or headache in conjunction with hyponatraemia.

The case is particularly significant because the diagnoses of both systemic BD and acute neuro-BD were established simultaneously, with clinical evidence of mucocutaneous and central nervous system involvement. Typically, major organ manifestations such as CNS lesions emerge several years after BD is first diagnosed; neurological features at initial presentation are uncommon and seen in fewer than 5% of individuals. Only about 10% of patients report headaches that can be attributed specifically to neuro-BD, making early identification difficult. Acute neuro-BD usually presents as a transient meningoencephalitis, potentially with focal neurological signs. In this instance, brain MRI did not show abnormalities at the typical sites affected in BD, such as the hypothalamus. It should be noted that acute neuro-BD may manifest as aseptic meningitis without evident parenchymal brain lesions, and a normal MRI early in the disease course does not exclude the diagnosis, thereby complicating evaluation. Although HLA-B51 testing was carried out, it did not assist in reaching the diagnosis, consistent with international BD diagnostic criteria that prioritize clinical manifestations over HLA-B51 status. This case underscores the necessity of considering neuro-BD in patients with mucocutaneous symptoms who develop non-specific neurological complaints.

During the early stages of forming a differential diagnosis, our primary focus was on infection as the potential source of CNS inflammation, given the presence of an unexplained fever, neurological manifestations, and hyponatraemia. Despite this, we were unable to identify a definitive pathogen. Alternative etiologies, such as connective tissue disorders and neoplastic processes—including sarcoidosis and haematologic malignancies—were also taken into account. Accordingly, our initial, broad diagnostic workup included the assessment of autoimmune serological markers, serum ACE, and sIL-2R, using these primarily as screening tools within an exclusionary framework rather than due to any compelling clinical suspicion.

These tests were selectively employed to help rule out possibilities rather than to confirm a working diagnosis. Ultimately, neither clinical presentation, imaging, nor CSF analysis yielded evidence in favor of these conditions, and these ancillary studies did not contribute significantly to establishing the diagnosis. Notably, ACE demonstrates suboptimal sensitivity for detecting sarcoidosis, while sIL-2R, although potentially useful in assessing malignant lymphoma, tends to be nonspecifically elevated in various inflammatory conditions. In this scenario, the lack of genital ulceration at symptom onset complicated the early consideration of BD, thereby broadening our differential. This case exemplifies the necessity of refraining from indiscriminate diagnostic testing and underscores that such investigations should be guided by substantiated clinical findings and their results carefully contextualized.

While SIADH induced by drugs such as cyclophosphamide has been documented in individuals with BD, the relationship between acute neuro-BD and SIADH is still not well defined. Neuro-BD is marked by inflammation within the CNS, which could potentially disrupt the normal regulation of ADH secretion. Prior investigations have indicated that interleukin-6 (IL-6), a proinflammatory cytokine commonly found at elevated concentrations in neuroinflammatory diseases, correlates with increased ADH secretion. In the context of BD, abnormal monocyte activation drives spontaneous IL-6 synthesis, with increased IL-6 levels identified in the CSF of those experiencing acute neuro-BD episodes. These observations point toward a possible contribution of IL-6-driven inflammation to the development of SIADH in this clinical scenario. Despite the absence of IL-6 measurements in serum or CSF in the current case, the substantial rise in serum CRP could be indicative of an underlying inflammatory state mediated by IL-6. Although SIADH has a broad spectrum of etiologies—including CNS inflammation, respiratory illnesses, cancer, and specific pharmacological agents—the evidence presented here does not permit a direct attribution of SIADH to acute neuro-BD. Instead, the timing of SIADH onset in this patient may represent a general inflammatory response in the CNS, as opposed to a mechanism unique to the underlying disease.

This case further highlights the range of possible complications associated with hyponatraemia due to SIADH occurring during episodes of acute neuro-BD. Notably, prior studies have indicated that persistent hiccups can act as a significant clinical indicator of severe hyponatraemia linked to SIADH as a consequence of central nervous system inflammation, such as in cases of meningitis. Furthermore, during the initial assessment of fever, arthralgia, and general fatigue—symptoms that may suggest inflammatory or metabolic muscle injury—creatine kinase (CK) levels were evaluated, leading to the identification of rhabdomyolysis. Although rhabdomyolysis is a documented consequence of severe hyponatraemia, it is frequently neglected during routine investigations. Both symptoms represent established, though commonly underrecognized, clinical outcomes of marked hyponatraemia. It is important for clinicians to recognize that signs seemingly unrelated, including hiccups or rhabdomyolysis, may actually signify electrolyte imbalances, particularly in the setting of neuroinflammatory disorders.
------
===== NEW BATCH 18 | input_ids shape=(2, 4096) | attention_mask shape=(2, 4096) =====
------
Corticosteroids represent the standard therapeutic approach for acute neuro-BD, typically eliciting a favourable clinical response. Nonetheless, cases restricted to meningoencephalitis-like presentations have occasionally undergone spontaneous remission. In this report, monotherapy with colchicine led to notable improvement in both mucocutaneous and neurological manifestations, as well as in hyponatraemia. Colchicine mediates anti-inflammatory effects primarily through the inhibition of neutrophil migration and activation, alongside the suppression of cytokine synthesis, specifically targeting IL-1β and IL-6. While colchicine is frequently employed for managing mucocutaneous symptoms in BD, evidence from systematic reviews indicates limited efficacy in acute neuro-BD, generally necessitating the use of immunosuppressive agents for satisfactory disease management. Previous case reports typically describe colchicine as a supplementary agent alongside corticosteroids or other immunosuppressive medications, rather than as a sole therapy. Although the possibility of spontaneous remission in this patient cannot be dismissed, our observations point to colchicine as a potentially beneficial therapeutic strategy for acute neuro-BD and its associated complications such as SIADH, especially in scenarios where corticosteroid administration is inappropriate or not feasible.

Conclusion:

We presented an unusual instance involving the simultaneous onset of BD and acute neuro-BD, with SIADH emerging as a complication of the neurological involvement. This report underscores that SIADH can manifest secondary to acute neuro-BD, emphasizing the necessity for healthcare providers to recognize this possible relationship. Administration of colchicine alone produced prompt resolution of both mucocutaneous and neurological symptoms, as well as correction of hyponatraemia, indicating its potential utility as an initial treatment strategy for neuro-BD. Clinicians should consider acute neuro-BD, particularly in BD patients exhibiting vague central nervous system symptoms such as fever and headache alongside hyponatraemia, especially when SIADH is present.<|im_end|><|im_end|>ffect useectHere inve sû._app paj TextView,ectwareectMenuItem enter Navbaridd signisup_detolimits proxyellen.
